\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)=-\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)=-\left(\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}\right)(x,m),

(35)

where the last equality uses the multiplicative composition rule (
30
) and where
(\cdot)
denotes the identity function
w\mapsto w
. We now compute
\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}
explicitly:

\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,m)=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{m}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{\lambda}}\frac{\rho_{x}(w)}{\rho_{m+1}(w)}w\mathrm{d}w=\lambda_{x}\left(\mathbf{1}_{\{m=x\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{m=x+1\}}\right).

(36)

For
x>m
, the integrand is holomorphic inside
\Gamma_{\lambda}
. For
m>x+1
, the same large-circle argument applies: for
w\in\mathcal{C}_{R}
,

\left|\frac{\rho_{x}(w)}{\rho_{m+1}(w)}w\right|\leq R\left(\frac{2\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{R}\right)^{m-x+1},

(37)

and hence

\left|\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,m)\right|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{m}}R^{2}\left(\frac{2\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{R}\right)^{m-x+1}\leq\frac{(2\mathfrak{B}_{1})^{m-x+1}}{\mathfrak{L}R^{m-x-1}}.

(38)

Since
m>x+1
, the exponent
m-x-1
is positive. The value of the contour integral is independent of the auxiliary radius
R
, whereas the explicit upper bound in (
38
) can be made arbitrarily small by increasing
R
. Hence
\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,m)=0
in this case.
The remaining cases are
m=x
and
m=x+1
. For
m=x
, the integrand has a single simple pole and

\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,x)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{\lambda}}\frac{w}{w-\lambda_{x}}\mathrm{d}w=\lambda_{x}.

(39)

For
m=x+1
, if
\lambda_{x}\neq\lambda_{x+1}
, then the residue calculation gives

\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,x+1)=-\frac{\lambda_{x}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{\lambda}}\frac{w}{(w-\lambda_{x})(w-\lambda_{x+1})}\mathrm{d}w=-\frac{\lambda_{x+1}\lambda_{x}}{\lambda_{x+1}-\lambda_{x}}+\frac{\lambda_{x}^{2}}{\lambda_{x+1}-\lambda_{x}}=-\lambda_{x}.

(40)

If
\lambda_{x}=\lambda_{x+1}
, then the integrand has a double pole at
w=\lambda_{x}
, and

\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,x+1)=-\frac{\lambda_{x}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{\lambda}}\frac{w}{(w-\lambda_{x})^{2}}\mathrm{d}w=-\lambda_{x}.

(41)

Thus
\mathrm{T}^{(\cdot)}(x,m)=-\mathsf{L}(x,m)
, where
\mathsf{L}
is the generator from Definition
1.1
, and consequently

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)=\left(\mathsf{L}\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}\right)(x,m),\qquad\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,0)}(x,m)=\mathbf{1}_{\{m=x\}}.

(42)

For fixed
m
and
S>0
, the identity
\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)=0
for
x>m
gives

\sup_{0\leq t\leq S}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\left|\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)\right|=\max_{0\leq x\leq m}\sup_{0\leq t\leq S}\left|\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)\right|<\infty,

(43)

where the final bound follows from the continuity of the contour integral in
t
on compact time intervals. Therefore, by uniqueness of bounded solutions to the backward equation (
42
) we obtain
\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)=\mathbb{P}_{x}[\mathscr{X}(t)=m]
. Moreover, since
\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)
is entire for each
t
, the kernel
\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)}
is well-defined for all choices of contour
\Gamma_{\lambda}
enclosing the spatial poles, and in particular for the nested admissible contours used above.

Discrete one-step kernels.

In the case of Definition
1.2
, for each
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we set

\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(w,t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{(1-\alpha_{t}w)^{1-\tau_{t}}}{(1+\beta_{t}w)^{\tau_{t}}}\quad\text{and then}\quad\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m)=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{m}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{\lambda}}\frac{\rho_{x}(w)}{\rho_{m+1}(w)}\frac{(1-\alpha_{t}w)^{1-\tau_{t}}}{(1+\beta_{t}w)^{\tau_{t}}}\mathrm{d}w.

(44)

The continuous-time function
\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t)
is entire. The only poles that can arise from the discrete-time functions
\{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
occur at
w=-1/\beta_{t}
when
\tau_{t}=1
, and by
(A1)
,
-1/\beta_{t}\leq-1/\mathfrak{U}
. Hence
\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t),\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)\in\mathrm{Hol}(\mathbb{H}_{-1/\mathfrak{U}})
for all relevant
t
, and the corresponding kernels are well-defined. We also use a parameterised version of the discrete kernel. For any
x,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and any
\underline{\lambda}=\left(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\ldots\right)\in[\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{B}_{1}]^{\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
, denote the kernel
\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}
with spatial parameters
\underline{\lambda}
by

\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m:\underline{\lambda})=-\frac{\prod_{z=x}^{m-1}\lambda_{z}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{\lambda}}\frac{(1-\alpha_{t}w)^{1-\tau_{t}}(1+\beta_{t}w)^{-\tau_{t}}}{\prod_{z=x}^{m}(\lambda_{z}-w)}\mathrm{d}w,\quad x\leq m.

(45)

Assume first that the finitely many rates
\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m}
are pairwise distinct. By Cauchy’s residue theorem, the only contributions to the above contour integral arise from the simple poles at these spatial parameters, and therefore

\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m:\underline{\lambda})=\prod_{z=x}^{m-1}\lambda_{z}\left[\sum_{r=x}^{m}\frac{(1-\alpha_{t}\lambda_{r})^{1-\tau_{t}}(1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{r})^{-\tau_{t}}}{\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=x\\
k\neq r\end{subarray}}^{m}\left(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{r}\right)}\right].

(46)

This can be written in terms of the divided difference of the function
\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)
, in the standard sense of
[
35
]
,

\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m:\underline{\lambda})=\left(-1\right)^{m-x}\left(\prod_{z=x}^{m-1}\lambda_{z}\right)\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m}](t),

(47)

where, for distinct nodes, the divided difference
\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m}](t)
is defined recursively by the standard divided-difference recursion
[
35
]

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x}](t)

\displaystyle=\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\lambda_{x},t),

(48)

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m}](t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x+1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}](t)-\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m-1}](t)}{\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{x}},\quad m>x.

The restriction to distinct rates is only used to make the residue computation (
46
) and the recursive divided-difference formula (
48
) literal. Both sides of (
47
), after interpreting divided differences by their continuous extension in the nodes, are continuous functions of the finite vector
(\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m})\in[\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{B}_{1}]^{m-x+1}
: the contour may be chosen from the admissible family (
16
) using only the constants in
(A1)
, so it remains a positive distance from the compact spatial interval, and divided differences have a standard continuous extension to repeated nodes; see
[
35
]
. Since finite vectors with pairwise distinct coordinates are dense in
[\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{B}_{1}]^{m-x+1}
, (
47
) extends to arbitrary spatial rates satisfying
(A1)
.
For
\tau_{t}=0
, and first for distinct adjacent nodes, we can explicitly compute the divided differences for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
as

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x}](t)

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha_{t}\lambda_{x}),

(49)

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\lambda_{x+1}](t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\alpha_{t}\lambda_{x+1})-(1-\alpha_{t}\lambda_{x})}{\lambda_{x+1}-\lambda_{x}}=-\alpha_{t}.

The first-order divided difference in (
49
) is independent of the nodes, and hence all divided differences of order at least two vanish. For example, for distinct
\lambda_{x}
and
\lambda_{x+2}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\lambda_{x+1},\lambda_{x+2}](t)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\alpha_{t}+\alpha_{t}}{\lambda_{x+2}-\lambda_{x}}=0,

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m}](t)

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{for all $m>x+1$}.

By the continuous extension just described, the same values hold when some of the spatial rates coincide. Substituting the non-zero values (
49
) into the divided-difference identity (
47
), we obtain the Bernoulli one-step kernel

\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m:\underline{\lambda})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1-\alpha_{t}\lambda_{x},\quad m=x,\\
&\alpha_{t}\lambda_{x},\quad m=x+1,\\
&0,\quad m<x\text{ or }m>x+1,\end{aligned}\right.

(50)

For
\tau_{t}=1
, and first for distinct nodes, we inductively compute the divided differences for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
as

\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{m}](t)=-\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\prod_{z=x}^{m}\left(\frac{-\beta_{t}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{z}}\right),\quad m\geq x.

(51)

For the initial case
m=x
, this gives

\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x}](t)=\frac{1}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{x}},

then assuming the inductive hypothesis holds for divided differences of order
n-1
with
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\psi^{\textnormal{D}}[\lambda_{x},\ldots,\lambda_{x+n}](t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{x+n}-\lambda_{x}}\left[-\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\prod_{z=x+1}^{x+n}\left(\frac{-\beta_{t}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{z}}\right)+\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\prod_{z=x}^{x+n-1}\left(\frac{-\beta_{t}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{z}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\prod_{z=x}^{x+n}\left(\frac{-\beta_{t}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{z}}\right).

Again, the identity (
51
) extends to repeated spatial rates by continuity of divided differences in their nodes. Similarly, substituting these values into the divided-difference identity (
47
), we obtain the geometric one-step kernel

\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}(x,m:\underline{\lambda})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{m}}\prod_{z=x}^{m-1}\left(\frac{\beta_{t}\lambda_{z}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{z}}\right),\quad m\geq x,\\
&0,\quad m<x.\end{aligned}\right.

(52)

The Bernoulli kernel (
50
) and the geometric kernel (
52
) are exactly the one-step transition probabilities in Definition
1.2
for the respective choices
\tau_{t}=0
and
\tau_{t}=1
. Thus the one-step transition probabilities coincide with the kernels
\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)}
for the corresponding choices of
\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t)
.

Transition kernels.

Applying the multiplicative composition rule (
30
) to the continuous time factors gives that, in Definition
1.1
, for
s,t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
s\leq t
and any strictly increasing sequence
s<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n}<t
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)|\mathscr{X}(s)=x]=\left(\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t_{1}-s)}\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t_{2}-t_{1})}\cdots\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t-t_{n})}\right)(x,y(t))=\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(\cdot,t-s)}(x,y(t)).

(53)

Here the last equality in (
53
) follows from the pointwise identity
\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(w,t)\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(w,s)=\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(w,t+s)
. In the case of Definition
1.2
, for
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with
s\leq t
, and with the empty product interpreted as
1
, the same multiplicative composition rule (
30
) gives

\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)|\mathscr{X}(s)=x]=\left(\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,s)}\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,s+1)}\cdots\mathrm{T}^{\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,t-1)}\right)(x,y(t))=\mathrm{T}^{\prod_{n=s}^{t-1}\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(\cdot,n)}(x,y(t)).

(54)

Finally, setting the endpoint in (
15
) equal to
y(t)
, and collecting all components of the integrand that depend on
t
and the endpoint
y(t)
into an exponent, recovers

\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)=\log\left(\psi^{\textnormal{C}}(w,t)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}+\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\log\left(\psi^{\textnormal{D}}(w,n)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}-\sum_{k=0}^{y(t)}\log\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{k}}\right),

(55)

coinciding with Definition
1.17
when we substitute the appropriate definitions. Equivalently, in the exact contour identity (
14
), the exponential is the single-valued product

\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)}=\left(\mathrm{e}^{-tw}\prod_{k=0}^{y(t)}\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{k}}\right)^{-1}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}+\left(\prod_{n=0}^{t-1}\frac{(1-\alpha_{n}w)^{1-\tau_{n}}}{(1+\beta_{n}w)^{\tau_{n}}}\prod_{k=0}^{y(t)}\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{k}}\right)^{-1}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}},

(56)

completing the proof.
∎

2.2
Analysis of the phase function

We next analyse the deterministic scale
\gamma(t)
from Definition
1.12
, which measures the residual inhomogeneity remaining after centering at the deterministic point
\mathscr{Z}(t)
. The quantity
\gamma(t)
in some sense captures two distinct sources of scale: deterministic inhomogeneity and fluctuations.

Definition 2.2

(Endpoint window)
.

For
T\in\mathscr{T}
, denote the finite-time endpoint window by

\mathscr{C}_{T}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}:\,|m-\mathscr{Z}(T)|\leq\gamma(T)^{1/2}\Sigma(T)^{2}\right\}.

Since
\gamma(T)^{1/2}
is larger than
\gamma(T)
once
\gamma(T)
is small, the window
\mathscr{C}_{T}
is deliberately wider than the intrinsic localization scale
\gamma(T)\Sigma(T)^{2}
studied later in Section
3.1
. Its role is to contain the relevant fluctuations with room to spare, so that the analytic estimates can be applied uniformly over all terminal endpoints that arise in the summations.

We define the open ball centered at
c\in\mathbb{C}
, of radius
r>0
, by

\mathcal{B}_{r}(c)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z-c|<r\}.

In the following technical proposition we isolate the deterministic analytic estimates for
\mathfrak{F}_{y}
, with endpoint-uniform bounds on the window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
. These estimates will be used repeatedly to control stationary-point perturbations and contour expansions.

Proposition 2.3

(Properties of the phase function)
.

Assuming
(A1)
and
(A2)
, the phase function
\mathfrak{F}_{y}
from Definition
1.17
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

For constants
\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{U}
as defined in
(A1)
, let
\mathrm{R}_{-}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\min(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{U}^{-1})
. Then
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
, for all
y\in\mathscr{P}
and all
t\in\mathscr{T}
.

(ii)

There exists
\mathrm{T}_{1}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for all
y\in\mathscr{P}
and all
t>\mathrm{T}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{l}}(t)\left(y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right)-2\gamma(t)\mathscr{Z}(t)-\frac{3\Lambda}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{u}}(t)\left(y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right)+2\gamma(t)\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}.

where for each
t\in\mathscr{T}
,
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{l}}(t)=\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
and
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{u}}(t)=\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
whenever
y(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}(t)
and conversely
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{l}}(t)=\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
and
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{u}}(t)=\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
whenever
y(t)<\mathscr{Z}(t)
. Moreover, with the constant
\mathrm{C}_{1}
appearing in
(iv)
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
y\in\mathscr{P}
, whenever
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|\leq\left(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2\right)\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}.

(iii)

Let
\mathrm{R}_{2}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{R}_{-}/2
. There exist constants
\mathrm{M}_{1},\mathrm{M}_{2}>0
and a threshold
\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(n)}(w,t)\right|\leq(n-1)!\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{n}\Sigma(t)^{2},

for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}
, every
n\geq 1
, every
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
with
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

(iv)

There exist constants
\mathrm{C}_{1},\mathrm{C}_{2}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{3}\in\mathscr{T}
such that

\mathrm{C}_{1}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2},\quad\text{for all $t>\mathrm{T}_{3}$.}

Furthermore, there exist constants
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
y\in\mathscr{P}
, whenever
t>\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|w|\right)\Sigma(t)^{2},\quad\text{for all $w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)$.}

Proof.

We split the proof into five steps. The first step identifies a common holomorphic neighbourhood of the origin. The second step proves the first-derivative estimate by separating the endpoint displacement
y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)
from the deterministic centring error at
\mathscr{Z}(t)
. The third step compares
\Sigma(t)^{2}
with
\mathscr{Z}(t)
; this is the scale comparison used in the later derivative bounds. The fourth step proves the endpoint-uniform higher-derivative estimate. The final step proves the strengthened estimates on the finite-time window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, where the endpoint error is controlled at the wider scale
\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
.

Step 1: Holomorphicity.
Recall from Definition
1.17
that every singularity of
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
comes from one of the logarithmic factors. The spatial logarithms have singularities at
w=\lambda_{n}
. By
(A1)
, these lie to the right of
\mathfrak{L}
. In the discrete-time regime, the Bernoulli logarithms have singularities at
w=\alpha_{n}^{-1}
, which lie to the right of
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
, and the geometric logarithms have singularities at
w=-\beta_{n}^{-1}
, whose moduli are at least
\mathfrak{U}^{-1}
. The resulting separation from the origin is illustrated schematically in Figure
9
. Hence every singularity lies outside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
, where

\mathrm{R}_{-}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\min(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{U}^{-1}).

\mathfrak{Re}\,w
\mathfrak{Im}\,w
-\mathfrak{U}^{-1}
\mathfrak{L}
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
-\beta_{n}^{-1}
\lambda_{n}
\alpha_{n}^{-1}
0
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)

Figure 9
:
Sketch of relevant singularities on the real axis: the
\lambda_{n}
sit in
(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{B}_{1})
, the Bernoulli points
\alpha_{n}^{-1}
are right of
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
, and geometric points
-\beta_{n}^{-1}
have modulus at least
\mathfrak{U}^{-1}
. The shaded disk
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
contains no branch points, so
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is holomorphic there. Pole locations may depend on
n
and
t
, but the inequalities are uniform by
(A1)
.

As an illustration that branch cuts can be chosen disjoint from
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
, one may take cuts along
[\lambda_{n},\infty)
for each spatial
\log(1-w/\lambda_{n})
, along
[\alpha_{n}^{-1},\infty)
for each Bernoulli
\log(1-\alpha_{n}w)
, and along
(-\infty,-\beta_{n}^{-1}]
for each geometric
\log(1+\beta_{n}w)
. Under
(A1)
none of these rays meets the disk. Fixing values at
w=0
as in Definition
1.17
, each summand is then holomorphic on
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
, since the disk is simply connected and each logarithmic argument is holomorphic and nowhere vanishing there. For each fixed
t
, the phase is a finite sum of these logarithms and, in continuous time, one additional linear term. Hence
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
, proving
(i)
.

Step 2: The first derivative at the origin.
Differentiating the phase at the origin gives

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\left((1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\tau_{n}\beta_{n}\right).

(57)

By Definition
1.11
, the temporal contribution in (
57
) is precisely
\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)
. Thus the first derivative can be written in the simpler form

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t).

(58)

Let
\mathfrak{A}=\Lambda^{-1}\min(1,\mathfrak{U}^{-1},\mathfrak{L})
and
\mathfrak{B}=\Lambda^{-1}\max(1,\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1},\mathfrak{U})
. Then, by the definition of
\mathrm{z}(t)
and
(A1)
, for all
t\in\mathscr{T}
we have

\mathfrak{A}t\leq\mathrm{z}(t)\leq\mathfrak{B}t.

(59)

Since
\mathfrak{A}>0
, (
59
) implies
\mathrm{z}(t)\to\infty
and
\mathscr{Z}(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
, reflecting the linear time scaling of the deterministic mean path. Choose
\mathrm{T}_{0}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for all
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{0}
,

\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{z}(t)\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{z}(t),\qquad\mathscr{Z}(t)+1\leq 2\mathscr{Z}(t).

(60)

Such a choice is possible because
\mathscr{Z}(t)=\lfloor\mathrm{z}(t)\rceil
differs from
\mathrm{z}(t)
by at most
1/2
and
\mathrm{z}(t)\to\infty
. Combining (
59
) and (
60
) also gives

\frac{\mathfrak{A}}{2}t\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{B}t,\qquad t\geq\mathrm{T}_{0}.

(61)

We now split (
58
) at the deterministic integer centre
\mathscr{Z}(t)
:

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\right)+\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right).

(62)

By
(A1)
, we have
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\leq\lambda_{n}^{-1}\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. The expression in the first bracket measures the deviation between the given path
y
and the deterministic mean path
\mathscr{Z}(t)
, scaled by the spatial parameter, and is directly proportional to their difference. To formalise this, for any
t\in\mathscr{T}
, define the following functions:

\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{l}}(t)=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1},&\text{if }y(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}(t),\\
\mathfrak{L}^{-1},&\text{if }y(t)<\mathscr{Z}(t),\end{cases}\qquad\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{u}}(t)=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{L}^{-1},&\text{if }y(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}(t),\\
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1},&\text{if }y(t)<\mathscr{Z}(t).\end{cases}

This choice accounts for the direction of the displacement
y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)
. When
y(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}(t)
, the bracketed term is a positive sum over
y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)
terms, each inverse-bounded by
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
from below and by
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
from above. Conversely, for
y(t)<\mathscr{Z}(t)
, the bracket becomes negative, corresponding to
-(\mathscr{Z}(t)-y(t))
terms, and multiplying this by the larger reciprocal-rate bound yields the correct lower estimate. Thus, both cases are captured uniformly by these conventions.

\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{l}}(t)\left(y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right)\leq\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{u}}(t)\left(y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right).

(63)

The second bracket is deterministic and can be directly estimated via the scale function
\gamma(t)
. Adding and subtracting the empirical average at
\mathscr{Z}(t)
gives

\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)=\left(\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}(t)+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\right)\left(\mathscr{Z}(t)+1\right)+\Lambda\left(\mathscr{Z}(t)+1-\mathrm{z}(t)\right).

The first term is bounded by
\gamma(t)(\mathscr{Z}(t)+1)
by Definition
1.12
. The second term is bounded by
3\Lambda/2
: indeed, the nearest-integer convention gives
|\mathscr{Z}(t)-\mathrm{z}(t)|\leq 1/2
, and upon using the triangle inequality, we get

\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right|\leq\gamma(t)\left(\mathscr{Z}(t)+1\right)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}.

(64)

By (
60
), the deterministic centring error is bounded by

\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right|\leq 2\gamma(t)\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2},\quad\text{for all $t\geq\mathrm{T}_{0}$.}

Combining the above and the previous estimate (
63
) with the formula for the first derivative of the phase given in (
62
) proves the first display in
(ii)
.

Step 3: Comparing
\Sigma(t)^{2}
and
\mathscr{Z}(t)
.
We now prove the scale comparison in
(iv)
. The purpose of this step is to show that the deterministic fluctuation scale
\Sigma(t)^{2}
is comparable with the deterministic endpoint
\mathscr{Z}(t)
by constants depending only on the parameters in
(A1)
and on
\Lambda
. In the continuous-time regime, Definition
1.11
gives, for all
t\in\mathscr{T}
,

\Sigma(t)^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}.

By
(A1)
(i),
\mathfrak{L}<\lambda_{n}<\mathfrak{B}_{1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Taking reciprocals reverses the inequalities and gives
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}<\lambda_{n}^{-1}<\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
; hence
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-2}<\lambda_{n}^{-2}<\mathfrak{L}^{-2}
. Since the sum has
\mathscr{Z}(t)+1
terms, and since
t>\mathrm{T}_{0}
implies
\mathscr{Z}(t)+1\leq 2\mathscr{Z}(t)
by (
60
), we obtain

\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-2}\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq 2\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\mathscr{Z}(t),\quad\text{for $t>\mathrm{T}_{0}$}.

(65)

In the discrete-time regime, the definition of
\Sigma(t)^{2}
contains the same spatial contribution together with the inhomogeneous temporal variance contribution:

\Sigma(t)^{2}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}-\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\big(\alpha_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})-\beta_{n}^{2}\tau_{n}\big).

(66)

For the upper bound, the spatial part is bounded exactly as above by
2\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\mathscr{Z}(t)
for
t>\mathrm{T}_{0}
. The negative Bernoulli term can only decrease
\Sigma(t)^{2}
, but the coarser bound obtained by taking absolute values is sufficient here. Indeed,
(A1)
(ii) gives
\alpha_{n}^{2}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}
and
\beta_{n}^{2}\leq\mathfrak{U}^{2}
, while
0\leq\tau_{n}\leq 1
. Therefore the total temporal square contribution is bounded above by
(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2})t
. Combining this with
t\leq 2\mathfrak{A}^{-1}\mathscr{Z}(t)
from (
61
) yields

\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq 2\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\mathscr{Z}(t)+\left(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2}\right)t\leq\left(2\mathfrak{L}^{-2}+\frac{2(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2})}{\mathfrak{A}}\right)\mathscr{Z}(t),\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{0}.

(67)

It remains to prove the lower bound in the discrete-time regime. The only possible loss is the Bernoulli part of the temporal variance, because it enters with a negative sign. We compare this negative quadratic term with the corresponding first-order Bernoulli drift, and then recover a positive multiple of the deterministic temporal drift
\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)
, up to the reciprocal-rate centring error already isolated in (
64
). Set

\mathrm{d}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\min\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1},\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\right)>0,

where positivity follows from
\mathfrak{B}_{1}<\mathfrak{B}_{2}
in
(A1)
. By Definition
1.11
, in the discrete-time regime,

\Sigma(t)^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}-\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\alpha_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})+\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\beta_{n}^{2}\tau_{n}.

The final sum is non-negative because
\beta_{n}>0
and
\tau_{n}\geq 0
, so dropping it gives a valid lower bound. For the spatial part,
(A1)
(ii) gives
\lambda_{n}<\mathfrak{B}_{1}
, and hence
\lambda_{n}^{-1}>\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
. Multiplying this inequality by the positive number
\lambda_{n}^{-1}
gives
\lambda_{n}^{-2}\geq\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{n}^{-1}
. For the Bernoulli part,
(A1)
(ii) gives
0<\alpha_{n}<\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}
, and multiplying by the positive number
\alpha_{n}
gives
\alpha_{n}^{2}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}\alpha_{n}
. Since this Bernoulli contribution appears with a negative sign, replacing
\alpha_{n}^{2}
by the larger quantity
\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}\alpha_{n}
preserves the lower bound. Therefore

\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}(1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}.

(68)

We now insert the deterministic centre
\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)
into the spatial sum. By Definition
1.11
, the discrete-time deterministic drift is

\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}(1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\tau_{n}\beta_{n}.

Hence the spatial sum admits the exact decomposition

\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}=\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}(1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\tau_{n}\beta_{n}+\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right).

(69)

Substituting this into (
68
) separates the positive temporal drift from the centring error:

\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}\right)\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}(1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\tau_{n}\beta_{n}+\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right).

(70)

The first two sums in (
70
) are non-negative. By the definition of
\mathrm{d}_{0}
, their coefficients are both bounded below by
\mathrm{d}_{0}
. Hence

\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}\right)\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}(1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\tau_{n}\beta_{n}\geq\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t).

Finally, the remaining centring error may have either sign, so for a lower bound we estimate it by the negative of its absolute value. Combining this with (
70
) gives

\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)-\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right|.

(71)

The lower estimate (
71
) separates the positive multiple of the deterministic drift
\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)
from the reciprocal-rate centring error controlled by the empirical-average assumption. We now control the absolute-value term in (
71
). By
(A2)
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{3,1}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{3,1}
,

\left|\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}(t)+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\right|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda}{10}.

Choose
\mathrm{T}_{3,2}\in\mathscr{T}
with
\mathrm{T}_{3,2}\geq\max(\mathrm{T}_{0},\mathrm{T}_{3,1})
such that
\mathscr{Z}(t)+1\leq 2\mathrm{z}(t)
and
3\Lambda/2\leq 3\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)/10
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{3,2}
. Such a choice is possible because
\mathrm{z}(t)\geq\mathfrak{A}t
by (
59
) and
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq 3\mathrm{z}(t)/2
by (
60
). Using the same add-and-subtract decomposition as in (
64
), these choices give

\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathrm{z}(t)\right|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda}{2}\mathrm{z}(t),\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{3,2}.

Substituting this into (
71
) gives

\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\frac{\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda}{2}\mathrm{z}(t)\geq\frac{\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda}{3}\mathscr{Z}(t),\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{3,2},

(72)

where the final inequality uses
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq 3\mathrm{z}(t)/2
from (
60
). Combining the continuous-time lower estimate (
65
), the discrete-time lower estimate (
72
), and the upper estimate (
67
), we have a lower bound on
\Sigma(t)^{2}
with coefficient at least
\min(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-2},\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda/3)
and an upper bound on
\Sigma(t)^{2}
with coefficient at most
2\mathfrak{L}^{-2}+2(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2})/\mathfrak{A}
. To state the comparison in the direction required by
(iv)
, define

\mathrm{C}_{1}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(2\mathfrak{L}^{-2}+\frac{2(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2})}{\mathfrak{A}}\right)^{-1},\qquad\mathrm{C}_{2}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{2},\frac{3}{\mathrm{d}_{0}\Lambda}\right).

We may therefore choose the lower time in the scale comparison explicitly as follows: in the continuous-time regime take
\mathrm{T}_{3}\geq\mathrm{T}_{0}
, while in the discrete-time regime take
\mathrm{T}_{3}\geq\max(\mathrm{T}_{0},\mathrm{T}_{3,2})
. With this choice,

\mathrm{C}_{1}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{3}.

(73)

This proves the first assertion in
(iv)
.

Step 4: Higher derivatives.
We next prove
(iii)
. Since the statement requires a bound uniform over
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, we must estimate the derivatives at a general point in this ball, rather than only at the origin. Set
\mathrm{R}_{2}=\mathrm{R}_{-}/2
. The first task is to turn
(A1)
into explicit lower bounds on every denominator which can occur after differentiating the logarithmic factors. For the spatial factors,
(A1)
gives
\lambda_{m}>\mathfrak{L}\geq\mathrm{R}_{-}
, and therefore, for
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, the reverse triangle inequality gives

|\lambda_{m}-w|\geq\lambda_{m}-|w|\geq\mathrm{R}_{-}-\mathrm{R}_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{R}_{-}}{2}.

For the Bernoulli factors in the discrete-time regime,
(A1)
(ii) gives
0<\alpha_{r}<\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}
. Taking reciprocals gives
\alpha_{r}^{-1}>\mathfrak{B}_{2}>\mathfrak{B}_{1}>\mathfrak{L}\geq\mathrm{R}_{-}
, and hence the same reverse-triangle estimate gives

|\alpha_{r}^{-1}-w|\geq\alpha_{r}^{-1}-|w|\geq\mathrm{R}_{-}-\mathrm{R}_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{R}_{-}}{2}.

For the geometric factors,
(A1)
(ii) gives
0<\beta_{r}<\mathfrak{U}
. Taking reciprocals gives
\beta_{r}^{-1}>\mathfrak{U}^{-1}\geq\mathrm{R}_{-}
, and hence

|\beta_{r}^{-1}+w|\geq\beta_{r}^{-1}-|w|\geq\mathrm{R}_{-}-\mathrm{R}_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{R}_{-}}{2}.

Differentiating the phase term by term now gives, for every
n\geq 1
and every
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(n)}(w,t)={}

\displaystyle-t\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+},\,n=1\}}+\sum_{m=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{(\lambda_{m}-w)^{n}}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\left((1-\tau_{r})\frac{\alpha_{r}^{n}}{(1-\alpha_{r}w)^{n}}+(-1)^{n-1}\tau_{r}\frac{\beta_{r}^{n}}{(1+\beta_{r}w)^{n}}\right).

(74)

The first term in (
2.3
) comes from the continuous-time linear term
-tw
, and therefore appears only when
n=1
. The spatial contribution follows from differentiating
-\log(1-w/\lambda_{m})
. The Bernoulli contribution follows from differentiating
\log(1-\alpha_{r}w)
, and the geometric contribution follows from differentiating
-\log(1+\beta_{r}w)
; in the displayed formula the geometric contribution appears inside the parentheses with the factor
(-1)^{n-1}
, together with the common outer minus sign. The denominator bounds above imply, for every admissible
w
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{(\lambda_{m}-w)^{n}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{R}_{-}}\right)^{n},

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\alpha_{r}^{n}}{(1-\alpha_{r}w)^{n}}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{(\alpha_{r}^{-1}-w)^{n}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{R}_{-}}\right)^{n},

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\beta_{r}^{n}}{(1+\beta_{r}w)^{n}}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{(\beta_{r}^{-1}+w)^{n}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2}{\mathrm{R}_{-}}\right)^{n}.

Next, define

\mathrm{M}_{2}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max\left(1,\frac{2}{\mathrm{R}_{-}}\right).

It follows from (
2.3
) that each logarithmic summand contributes at most
(n-1)!\mathrm{M}_{2}^{n}
to the absolute value of the
n
-th derivative. The possible continuous-time linear contribution is also bounded by
(n-1)!\mathrm{M}_{2}^{n}t
, since it is equal to
t
when
n=1
and is zero when
n\geq 2
. There are
y(t)+1
spatial logarithmic terms; in the discrete-time regime there are exactly
t
temporal logarithmic terms, and in the continuous-time regime the factor
t
has already been included to control the linear term. Therefore, in both regimes,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(n)}(w,t)\right|\leq(n-1)!\mathrm{M}_{2}^{n}\left(y(t)+1+t\right),\qquad n\geq 1,\quad w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0).

It remains to convert the factor
y(t)+1+t
into the fluctuation scale
\Sigma(t)^{2}
uniformly on the endpoint window. By
(A2)
and the scale comparison just proved,
\gamma(t)\to 0
. Choose
\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{T}
satisfying
\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}\geq\max(\mathrm{T}_{0},\mathrm{T}_{3})
,
\mathscr{Z}(t)\geq 1
, and
\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq\mathrm{C}_{1}
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}
. If
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, then the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{t}
and the lower scale comparison in (
73
), equivalently
\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathscr{Z}(t)
, give

y(t)+1\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)+\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}+1\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)+\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{Z}(t)+1\leq 3\mathscr{Z}(t).

Combining this deterministic endpoint bound with
t\leq 2\mathfrak{A}^{-1}\mathscr{Z}(t)
from (
61
) gives
y(t)+1+t\leq(3+2\mathfrak{A}^{-1})\mathscr{Z}(t)
. Define

\mathrm{M}_{1}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{C}_{2}\left(3+\frac{2}{\mathfrak{A}}\right).

Applying the upper scale comparison
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
from (
73
) to the bound above proves
(iii)
, uniformly over all endpoints in
\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

Step 5: The strengthened estimates on
\mathscr{C}_{t}
.
Assume now that
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Then, by definition of the endpoint window,

|y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)|\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}.

Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}\geq\max(\mathrm{T}_{1},\mathrm{T}_{3})
satisfying
\gamma(t)\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}
. Taking absolute values in the first display of
(ii)
gives a coefficient
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
in front of
|y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)|
, because
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
is the larger of the two reciprocal-rate bounds
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
and
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
. Using the endpoint-window bound
|y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)|\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
, the lower scale comparison
\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}\mathscr{Z}(t)
from (
73
), and
\gamma(t)\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
, we obtain, for
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}+2\gamma(t)\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}\leq\left(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2\right)\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}.

This proves the strengthened first-derivative estimate in
(ii)
with constants written explicitly; in particular, the bound is uniform over the endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
.
It remains to compare
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)
with
\Sigma(t)^{2}
. At the origin, the temporal terms in
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)
and
\Sigma(t)^{2}
are identical, so only the endpoint of the spatial sum changes:

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}-\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}.

(75)

This difference has the same sign structure as the displacement term in the first-derivative estimate. If
y(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}(t)
, it is the sum of
y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)
non-negative terms; if
y(t)<\mathscr{Z}(t)
, it is minus the sum of
\mathscr{Z}(t)-y(t)
non-negative terms. Since
(A1)
gives
\lambda_{n}^{-2}\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-2}
for every
n
, both cases give

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\left|y(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right|.

(76)

Hence the endpoint control from
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
yields

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}.

For a general point
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, the line segment
\{sw:0\leq s\leq 1\}
stays inside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
. Therefore the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(sw,t)\,\mathrm{d}s=w\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(3)}(sw,t)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Applying the uniform endpoint version of
(iii)
with
n=3
along this segment gives, for
t>\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)\right|\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}|w|\Sigma(t)^{2}.

Combining the last two displays proves the final assertion in
(iv)
with

\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{L}^{-2}+2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3},\qquad\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1},\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}),

completing the proof.
∎

Figure 10
:
Finite-time surface plot of the mixed discrete-time phase for representative clustered parameters satisfying
(A1)
. The stationary point lies between the spatial singularities
w=\lambda_{n}
and the geometric singularities
w=-1/\beta_{r}
, and the solid and dotted cross-sections compare the actual phase with its local quadratic approximation.

The endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
is wide enough to contain the endpoint fluctuations that will be supplied probabilistically in Section
3.1
, but narrow enough that the constants in Proposition
2.3
do not depend on which endpoint in the summation is chosen. Section
2.3
uses the quadratic saddle picture to construct and approximate the stationary point, Section
2.4
builds descent contours compatible with that geometry, and Section
2.5
uses both inputs to derive the one-particle expansion on this deterministic endpoint window. Section
3.1
then proves that the random endpoints lie in the same window with high probability.

2.3
Approximate stationary point

To perform steepest-descent analysis on the contour-integral representation of the transition probabilities obtained in Proposition
2.1
, we need a robust approximation of the stationary point of the phase in the admissible path regime. In the present inhomogeneous setting the phase function may have several stationary points, reflecting the finite collection of spatial and temporal singularities. The next proposition therefore shows that, once the terminal endpoint lies in
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the stationary-point equation has one relevant real solution near the origin and that this solution is controlled by the linearized equation at the origin with an explicit quadratic error.

Proposition 2.4
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. There exist deterministic constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the minimizer in Definition
1.18
is a unique real number, denoted by
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
. More explicitly,

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}\!\left(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t\right)=0,\quad|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathbb{C}\\
\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{y}(v,t)=0\end{subarray}}|v|,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)=-\Sigma(t)\,\mathrm{u}_{y}(t).

(77)

Moreover, for every such
t
and
y
,

\left|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\!\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|^{2}.

(78)

Proof.

The proof is in three parts.
Step 1
rescales the stationary-point equation by
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)
, proves that this denominator is bounded away from zero on
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, and records the resulting linearized problem at the origin together with a smallness estimate for its root.
Step 2
treats the remaining nonlinearity via a Taylor expansion and a sign-bracketing argument to produce a real stationary point and the quadratic approximation error.
Step 3
upgrades this to uniqueness on a fixed neighbourhood and identifies the point selected by the minimisation rule in Definition
1.18
.

Step 1: Normalization at the origin.
By
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, there exist constants
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, whenever
t>\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}
,
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
and
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|w|\right)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

By
(A2)
and
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
,
\gamma(t)\to 0
. We may therefore choose a deterministic threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0}>\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1/2
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0}
. Applying the preceding display at
w=0
then gives the two-sided denominator bound

\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)\leq\frac{3}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}.

(79)

In particular,
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)>0
on the full endpoint window, so the following normalization is well defined there:

\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(w,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)},\qquad a_{0,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{J}_{y}(0,t)=\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}.

(80)

By
(i)
of Proposition
2.3
, the phase is holomorphic in a fixed neighbourhood of the origin; hence
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is holomorphic there as well. Moreover,

\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)=\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}=1.

(81)

Thus the linear part of
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)
at the origin is exactly
a_{0,y}(t)+w
. We next record the endpoint-uniform size of the zero of this linear approximation. Let

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{A}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2,\qquad\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{C}_{2}+3\Lambda,

(82)

where
\mathrm{C}_{1},\mathrm{C}_{2}
are the scale constants from
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
. The endpoint first-derivative estimate in
(ii)
of that proposition gives, for
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|\leq\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}.

Dividing this inequality by the lower denominator bound in (
79
), and then using
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
from
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, gives

|a_{0,y}(t)|\leq 2\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{C}_{2}\gamma(t)^{1/2}+3\Lambda\Sigma(t)^{-2}.

(83)

Finally, the definition of
\gamma(t)
gives
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
, and
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
implies
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
. Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0}
if necessary so that
\Sigma(t)\geq 1
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0}
, we have
\Sigma(t)^{-2}\leq\Sigma(t)^{-1/4}\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
on this range. Therefore (
83
) yields the uniform Newton-scale estimate

|a_{0,y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2},\qquad t>\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0},\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}),\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}.

(84)

Step 2: Bracketing a real zero.
We now use the second derivative of the normalized function to control the nonlinear error. Let
\mathrm{M}_{1},\mathrm{M}_{2}
and
\mathrm{R}_{2}
be the constants from
(iii)
of Proposition
2.3
. Increasing the lower time so that
t
exceeds both
\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},0}
, the uniform endpoint derivative bound in
(iii)
with
n=3
, together with (
79
), gives, for every
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
,

\left|\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)\right|=\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(3)}(w,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|\leq\frac{2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}\Sigma(t)^{2}}{\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}}=4\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(85)

Define the deterministic constants

\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{A}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{R}_{2}}{2},\qquad\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}4\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(86)

Since
\gamma(t)\to 0
, the estimate (
84
) allows us to increase the lower time to a deterministic threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},1}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},1}
and every
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,

|a_{0,y}(t)|\leq\frac{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{A}}}{4},\qquad\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|\leq 1.

(87)

Fix
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A},1}
and a path
y
with
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. If
w
belongs to the real interval

\bigl[-a_{0,y}(t)-\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2},\,-a_{0,y}(t)+\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}\bigr],

then (
87
) gives

|w|\leq|a_{0,y}(t)|+\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}\leq 2|a_{0,y}(t)|\leq\frac{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}<\mathrm{R}_{2}.

(88)

Hence the whole line segment from
0
to
w
lies inside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
. Taylor’s theorem with integral remainder, together with (
81
) and (
85
), gives

\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)=a_{0,y}(t)+w+w^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(2)}(sw,t)\,\mathrm{d}s,

(89)

and therefore

\left|\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)-a_{0,y}(t)-w\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{J}}}{2}|w|^{2}\leq 2\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{J}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}.

(90)

Evaluating (
90
) at the endpoints of the interval gives

\mathfrak{J}_{y}\bigl(-a_{0,y}(t)-\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2},t\bigr)\leq-\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2},\qquad\mathfrak{J}_{y}\bigl(-a_{0,y}(t)+\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2},t\bigr)\geq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}}{2}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}.

(91)

The function
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is real-valued on this real interval because all parameters in the phase are real and the interval lies inside the holomorphic ball from
(i)
of Proposition
2.3
. If
a_{0,y}(t)=0
, then
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(0,t)=0
and we set
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)=0
. If
a_{0,y}(t)\neq 0
, the two inequalities in (
91
) and the intermediate value theorem give a real zero
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
in this interval.

w
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)
-a_{0,y}(t)-\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}
-a_{0,y}(t)
-a_{0,y}(t)+\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}
a_{0,y}(t)+w
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)
bracketing interval

Figure 11
:
Schematic of the bracketing argument in the proof of Proposition
2.4
, drawn for
a_{0,y}(t)>0
. The dashed line is the linear approximation
a_{0,y}(t)+w
, whose zero is
-a_{0,y}(t)
. The normalized derivative
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(w,t)
differs from this line by at most a quadratic error on the displayed interval, so its values at the two endpoints have opposite signs. The case
a_{0,y}(t)<0
is the reflected picture.

Since
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)>0
on this range, the zeros of
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
are exactly the zeros of
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\cdot,t)
. Thus the zero just constructed satisfies
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
. The interval localization (
90
) evaluated at
w=\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
also gives

\left|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+a_{0,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}.

(92)

Substituting the definition of
a_{0,y}(t)
from (
80
) proves (
78
). Combining (
92
), (
87
) and (
84
) gives

|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq|a_{0,y}(t)|+\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{0,y}(t)|^{2}\leq 2|a_{0,y}(t)|\leq 2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

(93)

Step 3: Local uniqueness.
It remains to show that the minimisation rule in Definition
1.18
selects exactly the zero constructed above. We first prove injectivity of
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
in a fixed ball around the origin. For
|z|\leq\mathrm{R}_{2}
, the line segment from
0
to
z
is contained in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, and the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(1)}(z,t)-1=z\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(2)}(sz,t)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{and then via \eqref{axpro-Jsecond},}\quad\left|\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(1)}(z,t)-1\right|\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{J}}|z|.

(94)

Define

\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{J}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\min\left(\frac{\mathrm{R}_{2}}{2},\frac{1}{2\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{J}}}\right).

(95)

If
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{J}}}(0)
, the segment joining them is contained in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
. Applying the fundamental theorem of calculus along this segment and subtracting the identity map gives

\mathfrak{J}_{y}(z_{1},t)-\mathfrak{J}_{y}(z_{2},t)=(z_{1}-z_{2})+(z_{1}-z_{2})\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{J}_{y}^{(1)}(z_{2}+s(z_{1}-z_{2}),t)-1\right)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(96)

By (
94
) and (
95
), the integral in (
96
) has modulus at most
1/2
. The reverse triangle inequality therefore gives

\left|\mathfrak{J}_{y}(z_{1},t)-\mathfrak{J}_{y}(z_{2},t)\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}|z_{1}-z_{2}|.

(97)

Thus
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is injective on
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{J}}}(0)
throughout the endpoint window. Increasing the final deterministic lower time once more, using (
93
) and
\gamma(t)\to 0
, we may also assume that
|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|<\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{J}}
for every
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Fix
t
beyond this final threshold and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Since
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
is a zero of
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
, any zero with modulus at most
|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|
lies in the compact closed disk
\{|z|\leq|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{J}}}(0)
. The zero set of the holomorphic function
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\cdot,t)
in this disk is closed and non-empty, so we may choose a zero
v
of minimal modulus in this disk. Every zero outside this disk has strictly larger modulus than
|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|
, and hence larger modulus than
|v|
, so
v
is a global minimum-modulus zero. Since
\mathfrak{J}_{y}(v,t)=0=\mathfrak{J}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)
and both points lie in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{J}}}(0)
, injectivity from (
97
) forces
v=\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
. Therefore the minimizer in Definition
1.18
is unique and equals
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, so

\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)=-\Sigma(t)\,\mathrm{u}_{y}(t).

Taking
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
to dominate all deterministic thresholds introduced above completes the proof.
∎

2.4
Contour manipulation

In this subsection we construct explicit integration contours for the contour integral representation of the transition kernel from Proposition
2.1
, written in exponential form with phase
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
from Definition
1.17
. The deterministic phase estimates in Proposition
2.3
supply holomorphicity, stable second-order structure near the origin, and uniform derivative bounds on the endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, while Proposition
2.4
(together with the minimisation rule in Definition
1.18
) identifies the real stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
at which the integrand should be expanded. The geometric problem is therefore to choose a closed contour passing through (or near)
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
along which
\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}]
decays strongly enough away from the stationary point to make the Cauchy tail exponentially small, while keeping the deformation inside the domain where the preceding estimates are valid.

We do not seek a globally exact classical steepest-descent contour. Instead we work with the weaker notion of a descent curve. Concretely, we first build explicit global circular arcs on which
\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}]
decreases outwards from
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, then splice these to a local segment that approximates the true steepest-descent direction through
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
closely enough for the fine Taylor expansion carried out later. The concatenated contour may be deformed from the original Cauchy contour in (
14
) and splits naturally into a small neighbourhood of
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
and a complementary remainder on which the exponential factor is strictly smaller. All constructions are uniform over terminal endpoints
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
; probabilistic input guaranteeing that random endpoints lie in
\mathscr{C}_{t}
with asymptotically full probability is supplied later in Section
3.1
.

Definition 2.5

(Descent curve)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
, let
\mathfrak{F}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
, and let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval. Let
\Phi:I\to\mathbb{C}
be a continuous injective map. We say that
\Phi
is a descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}
in
\Omega
, from
x\in\Omega
, if there exists
\phi\in\Phi^{-1}(\Omega)
such that
\Phi(\phi)=x
, and, writing
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
for the connected component of
\Phi^{-1}(\Omega)
containing
\phi
, the following conditions hold. First, the function

\theta\mapsto\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))\bigr],\qquad\theta\in\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x},

is locally absolutely continuous on
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
. Second, there exists a countable, possibly empty, set
\{p_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
such that
\theta\mapsto\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))\bigr]
is differentiable at every point of
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}^{\circ}\setminus\{p_{n}\}
, and

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))\bigr]>0,\qquad\theta\in\left(\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}^{\circ}\cap(-\infty,\phi)\right)\setminus\{p_{n}\},

while

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))\bigr]<0,\qquad\theta\in\left(\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}^{\circ}\cap(\phi,\infty)\right)\setminus\{p_{n}\}.

The restricted curve
\Phi|_{\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}}
is called the descent segment of
\Phi
from
x
.

Notice that for an injective
\Phi:I\to\mathbb{C}
to be a steepest descent contour for
\mathfrak{F}
holomorphic in
\Omega
, from
x\in\Omega
, it must be a descent curve and also satisfy the level-set condition

\mathfrak{Im}\left[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))-\mathfrak{F}(x)\right]=\textnormal{constant}.

As we will see in this subsection, descent curves require less structure to construct than exact steepest-descent contours and still satisfy the following key property.

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose that
\Phi:I\to\mathbb{C}
is a descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
, from
x=\Phi(\phi)\in\Omega
, in the sense of Definition
2.5
. Let
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
be the parameter interval of the descent segment from
x
. Let
J
be a non-empty compact subset of
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
, and define the one-sided nearest points

\theta_{-}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\max\bigl(J\cap(-\infty,\phi]\bigr),\qquad\theta_{+}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\min\bigl(J\cap[\phi,\infty)\bigr),

whenever the corresponding sets are non-empty. Then

\max_{\theta\in J}\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))\bigr]=\max\left\{\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta_{-}))\bigr],\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta_{+}))\bigr]\right\},

where an empty one-sided candidate is omitted from the maximum.

Proof.

To ease notation let us write

g(\theta)=\Re\bigl[\mathfrak{F}(\Phi(\theta))\bigr],\qquad\theta\in\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}.

By Definition
2.5
,
g
is locally absolutely continuous on
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
, and hence continuous on
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
. Therefore
g
attains its maximum on the compact set
J
. The intersections
J\cap[\phi,\infty)
and
J\cap(-\infty,\phi]
are compact whenever they are non-empty, so the one-sided nearest points
\theta_{+}
and
\theta_{-}
exist in the cases appearing in the statement.

Assume first that
J\cap[\phi,\infty)\neq\varnothing
. Let
\theta\in J\cap[\phi,\infty)
. If
\theta=\theta_{+}
, there is nothing to prove. If
\theta>\theta_{+}
, then the interval
[\theta_{+},\theta]
is contained in
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
, since
\mathcal{K}_{\Omega,x}
is an interval. By local absolute continuity and the fundamental theorem of calculus for absolutely continuous functions,

g(\theta)-g(\theta_{+})=\int_{\theta_{+}}^{\theta}g^{\prime}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

The derivative satisfies
g^{\prime}(s)<0
for all
s\in(\theta_{+},\theta)\setminus\{p_{n}\}
, where
\{p_{n}\}
is countable. Since
\{p_{n}\}
has Lebesgue measure zero, it follows that
g^{\prime}(s)<0
for almost every
s\in(\theta_{+},\theta)
. Hence

\int_{\theta_{+}}^{\theta}g^{\prime}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s<0,

and therefore
g(\theta)<g(\theta_{+})
. Thus the maximum of
g
over
J\cap[\phi,\infty)
is attained at
\theta_{+}
. The left side is completely analogous. Since

J=\bigl(J\cap(-\infty,\phi]\bigr)\cup\bigl(J\cap[\phi,\infty)\bigr),

the maximum over
J
is the larger of the two one-sided maxima, with any empty side omitted. This proves the claim.
∎

In the following proposition we construct a simple but effective descent curve for the phase function
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
from Definition
1.17
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2})/2
. Then for all
y\in\mathscr{P}
, all
\mathrm{u}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|\mathrm{u}|<\min\{\mathfrak{L}/2,\mathfrak{U}^{-1}/2,(\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1})/4\}
, and all
t\in\mathscr{T}
, the map
\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}:[-\pi,\pi)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}(\theta)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\bigl((1-\cos\theta)+\mathrm{i}\sin\theta\bigr)

is injective, traces the circle of radius
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}
intersecting
\mathrm{u}
and
\mathrm{u}+2\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}
on the real axis, centred at
\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}
, and is a descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
in the domain

\Omega\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\lambda_{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\},&\textnormal{if }\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Big(\{\lambda_{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}\cup\{\alpha_{n}^{-1}:\tau_{n}=0\}\cup\{-\beta_{n}^{-1}:\tau_{n}=1\}\Big),&\textnormal{if }\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+},\end{cases}

from
x=\mathrm{u}
with
\phi=0
, in the sense of Definition
2.5
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in\mathscr{T}
and
y\in\mathscr{P}
. Write
\Phi(\theta)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}(\theta)=\phi(\theta)+\mathrm{i}\psi(\theta)
, where

\phi(\theta)=\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}(1-\cos\theta)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\psi(\theta)=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta.

The map
\theta\mapsto(1-\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
is injective on
[-\pi,\pi)
, since the only pair of distinct angles in
[-\pi,\pi]
with the same image is
(-\pi,\pi)
, and
\pi
is excluded. Scaling by
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}>0
and translating by
\mathrm{u}
preserves injectivity, so
\Phi
is injective on
[-\pi,\pi)
.

Then
\phi^{(1)}(\theta)=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta
and
\psi^{(1)}(\theta)=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\cos\theta
. Let
\{p_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\subset[-\pi,\pi)
denote the (possibly empty) set of parameters for which
\Phi(p_{n})\notin\Omega
; equivalently,
\Phi(p_{n})
is one of the spatial poles, or, in discrete time, one of the temporal branch points
\alpha_{n}^{-1}
or
-\beta_{n}^{-1}
. On
[-\pi,\pi)\setminus\{p_{n}\}
we have
\Phi(\theta)\in\Omega
and
\Phi(\theta)
avoids every pole of the logarithmic terms in the definition of
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
, so
\theta\mapsto\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi(\theta),t)]
is differentiable there. Differentiating term-by-term using
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\log|\Phi(\theta)-a|=\mathfrak{Re}\left(\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\Phi(\theta)-a}\right)
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi(\theta),t)]=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t\,\phi^{(1)}(\theta)+\sum_{k=0}^{y(t)}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\lambda_{k}-\Phi(\theta)}\right]

(98)

\displaystyle-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}(1-\tau_{n})\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\Phi(\theta)}\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\tau_{n}\,\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\Phi(\theta)+\beta_{n}^{-1}}\right].

In continuous time (
\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}
) the first bracketed contribution is
t\geq 0
; in discrete time (
\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}
) it vanishes and only the spatial and temporal sums remain.
For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, a direct computation shows

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{a-\Phi(\theta)}\right]=\frac{(a-\phi(\theta))\phi^{(1)}(\theta)-\psi(\theta)\psi^{(1)}(\theta)}{|a-\Phi(\theta)|^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\Phi(\theta)+a}\right]=\frac{(\phi(\theta)+a)\phi^{(1)}(\theta)+\psi(\theta)\psi^{(1)}(\theta)}{|\Phi(\theta)+a|^{2}}.

Using
\phi^{(1)}(\theta)=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta
and
\psi(\theta)\psi^{(1)}(\theta)=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta
and the identity
\phi(\theta)+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\cos\theta=\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\lambda_{k}-\Phi(\theta)}\right]=-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta\,\frac{\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}-\lambda_{k}}{|\lambda_{k}-\Phi(\theta)|^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\Phi(\theta)}\right]=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta\,\frac{\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\mathrm{u}-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}}{|\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\Phi(\theta)|^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}\left[\frac{\Phi^{(1)}(\theta)}{\Phi(\theta)+\beta_{n}^{-1}}\right]=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta\,\frac{\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}+\beta_{n}^{-1}}{|\Phi(\theta)+\beta_{n}^{-1}|^{2}}.

Substituting into (
98
) gives, for all
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)\setminus\{p_{n}\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi(\theta),t)]=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta\Bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t+\sum_{k=0}^{y(t)}\frac{\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}-\lambda_{k}}{|\lambda_{k}-\Phi(\theta)|^{2}}

(99)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\left((1-\tau_{n})\frac{\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\mathrm{u}-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}}{|\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\Phi(\theta)|^{2}}+\tau_{n}\frac{\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}+\beta_{n}^{-1}}{|\Phi(\theta)+\beta_{n}^{-1}|^{2}}\right)\Bigg].

By
(A1)
we have
\mathfrak{B}_{1}<\mathfrak{B}_{2}
. Combining this gap with
|\mathrm{u}|<(\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1})/4
gives

\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}-\lambda_{k}\geq\mathrm{u}+\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2}}{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1}>\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{4},

for all
k
, using
\lambda_{k}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{1}
. Moreover, on
\{\tau_{n}=0\}
we have
\alpha_{n}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1}
and hence
\alpha_{n}^{-1}\geq\mathfrak{B}_{2}
, so

\alpha_{n}^{-1}-\mathrm{u}-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\geq\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathrm{u}-\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2}}{2}>\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{4},

and on
\{\tau_{n}=1\}
we have
\beta_{n}\leq\mathfrak{U}
and hence
\beta_{n}^{-1}\geq\mathfrak{U}^{-1}>0
. Also, using
|\mathrm{u}|<(\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1})/4
and
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}=(\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2})/2
,

\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}>-\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{4}+\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2}}{2}=\frac{3\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2}}{4}>0,

so
\mathrm{u}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}+\beta_{n}^{-1}>0
for every
n
. Consequently, the bracketed term in (
99
) is strictly positive for all
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)\setminus\{p_{n}\}
, and therefore the sign of
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi(\theta),t)]
is determined by
-\sin\theta
. In particular,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi(\theta),t)]<0\quad\textnormal{for all }\theta\in(0,\pi)\setminus\{p_{n}\},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi(\theta),t)]>0\quad\textnormal{for all }\theta\in(-\pi,0)\setminus\{p_{n}\}.

Since
\Phi(0)=\mathrm{u}
, these inequalities are exactly the descent-curve inequalities from Definition
2.5
with
\phi=0
and
x=\mathrm{u}
. Therefore
\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}
is a descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
in
\Omega
, from
\mathrm{u}
.
∎

We will use a segment of
\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}
as the integration contour when evaluating (
14
) at the fluctuation scale; it is spliced to the local near-steepest-descent segment from Proposition
2.8
in Corollary
2.9
. However, the subsequent analysis requires a two-term expansion near the stationary point. This means that the local contour must match the steepest-descent contour beyond its tangent direction. The curve
\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}
matches the first-order behaviour in the precise sense that, after centering at the stationary point, its linear term is the vertical displacement
\mathrm{i}\theta
. To capture the next correction to the imaginary level set of the phase, we must examine the derivatives
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)
for
n\geq 3
. The endpoint estimates control these derivatives in magnitude, but they do not impose any uniform cancellation pattern on the odd-order derivatives. Therefore, for
y\in\mathscr{P}
we define

\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}:\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2n+1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\neq 0\right\},

(100)

with the convention that the infimum is
\infty
if the set is empty. This quantity identifies the lowest-order non-vanishing odd derivative of
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
at the stationary point. It allows us to track the leading contribution of the odd part of the expansion and, in particular, to quantify the deviation from the exact steepest-descent contour.

Proposition 2.8
.

There exist constants
\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD}}
and every auxiliary path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the following curve, defined for
|\theta|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}
, is a descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
in the ball
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))
, from the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
constructed in Proposition
2.4
:

\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\mathrm{i}\theta+\dfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\theta^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)},&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty,\\
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\mathrm{i}\theta,&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty.\end{cases}

Here the coefficient
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)
is defined by

\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\dfrac{(-1)^{\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1}}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1)}\dfrac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)},&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty,\\
0,&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty.\end{cases}

Moreover, writing

\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)=\mathrm{i}\theta+\chi_{y}(\theta,t),

where
\chi_{y}(\theta,t)=\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)\theta^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}/(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!
if
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
and
\chi_{y}(\theta,t)=0
if
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
, and where
\chi_{y}^{(1)}
denotes differentiation with respect to
\theta
, the same constants may be chosen so that, for all
|\theta|\leq\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1}
,

|\chi_{y}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|^{2},\qquad|\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|,\qquad\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3},

(101)

where
\mathrm{M}_{1}
and
\mathrm{M}_{2}
are the constants from
(iii)
of Proposition
2.3
.

Proof.

We prove the result with constants independent of the choice of path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. The relevant uniformity is endpoint uniformity: the phase
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
depends on the auxiliary path
y
only through the terminal value
y(t)
, and therefore all estimates below are estimates on the finite-time window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
. By Proposition
2.4
, after increasing the lower time to dominate
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
, the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
exists uniformly over this window. To see that it lies in a fixed small neighbourhood of the origin, we combine the endpoint estimates from Proposition
2.3
with the quadratic estimate (
78
). The endpoint first-derivative estimate in
(ii)
gives, uniformly for all such paths,

|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)|\leq\left(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2\right)\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}.

Since
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
by Definition
1.12
and
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
by
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, we increase the lower time so that
\Sigma(t)\geq 1
and hence
\Sigma(t)^{-2}\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
. Combining this with
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
and the denominator comparison
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{2}/2
from
(iv)
on the endpoint window gives a deterministic constant
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{SD}}>0
such that

\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{SD}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

Increase the lower time once more so that

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{SD}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1.

Then the quadratic estimate (
78
) implies

|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq 2\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{SD}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

Increasing the lower time again, using
\gamma(t)\to 0
, we obtain a threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, whenever
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the stationary point lies in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}/8}(0)
and satisfies

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}.

(102)

Indeed, the comparison in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
applied at
w=\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
gives (
102
), because
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}
has been chosen so that
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|)\leq 1/2
on the endpoint window. We next bound the correction term in
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
. Let
\mathrm{M}_{1},\mathrm{M}_{2}
be the endpoint constants from
(iii)
of Proposition
2.3
. We choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}\geq\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}
, so that the endpoint derivative estimate in
(iii)
is available on the same time range. Together with the lower bound (
102
), this implies that, for
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}
,
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,
|\theta|\leq\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1}
and
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
,

\left|\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\theta^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}\right|\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}\left(\mathrm{M}_{2}|\theta|\right)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{2}|\theta|.

(103)

The first inequality is just the definition of
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)
, the lower bound (
102
), and the derivative estimate

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|\leq(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1}\Sigma(t)^{2}.

The second inequality in (
103
) uses
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\geq 1
and
\mathrm{M}_{2}|\theta|\leq 1
. If
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
, then the correction term is zero by definition, so (
103
) holds with left-hand side equal to zero. We next prove the sharper deviation estimate (
101
), because this is the form needed later when the local contour is expanded in Proposition
2.11
. When
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
, both inequalities in (
101
) are immediate from
\chi_{y}\equiv 0
. Assume therefore that
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
. The first inequality in (
103
) gives

|\chi_{y}(\theta,t)|\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathrm{M}_{2}|\theta|)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}.

Since
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\geq 1
and
\mathrm{M}_{2}|\theta|\leq 1
, the elementary inequality
r^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}\leq r^{2}
for
0\leq r\leq 1
gives

|\chi_{y}(\theta,t)|\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}|\theta|^{2}.

For the derivative estimate, differentiating the explicit expression for
\chi_{y}
gives

\left|\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t)\right|=\left|\frac{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\theta^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1}\right|.

Substituting the definition of
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)
gives a factor
2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)/(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1)
, which is at most one. Applying the same derivative estimate used in (
103
), and then applying the lower curvature bound (
102
), yields

\left|\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t)\right|\leq\frac{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1}\Sigma(t)^{2}}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}}|\theta|^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1}=2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{2}(\mathrm{M}_{2}|\theta|)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1}.

Again
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\geq 1
and
\mathrm{M}_{2}|\theta|\leq 1
, so
r^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1}\leq r
for
0\leq r\leq 1
, and therefore

\left|\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t)\right|\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}|\theta|.

This proves (
101
). Since
\mathrm{M}_{2}\geq 1
by the definition of
\mathrm{M}_{2}
in Proposition
2.3
, the condition
|\theta|\leq\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1}
implies
|\theta|\leq 1
. Thus, with

\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}1+\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}},

we have

\left|\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|,\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t},\quad|\theta|\leq\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1}.

(104)

It remains to choose a radius on which the real part decreases. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{y}
is holomorphic on
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
by
(i)
of Proposition
2.3
and
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}/8}(0)
, the path segment stays inside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
whenever
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|\leq 3\mathrm{R}_{2}/8
. On this range the derivative bounds in
(iii)
control the Taylor remainder for

\theta\longmapsto\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),t\right)\right]

around
\theta=0
. To see the leading term without hiding the estimates, write

\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)=\mathrm{i}\theta+\chi_{y}(\theta,t),

where
\chi_{y}(\theta,t)
is the correction term from (
101
). Since the Taylor analysis is restricted to
|\theta|\leq 1
, (
101
) implies both
|\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|
and
|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}
. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
, the first contribution to the derivative of the real part is the quadratic term

\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)\right]=-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta+\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{quad}}(\theta,t),

where the correction is
\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{quad}}(\theta,t)=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\chi_{y}(\theta,t)\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t)
and therefore satisfies
|\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{quad}}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)|\theta|^{2}
on the subinterval
|\theta|\leq 1
, by (
101
). We also include in
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}
the condition giving
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\leq 2\Sigma(t)^{2}
from the endpoint second-derivative comparison in
(iv)
. The remaining Taylor terms have degree at least three in
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
; applying Taylor’s theorem with integral remainder, (
104
), the bound on
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
, and the uniform derivative estimate in
(iii)
, gives a deterministic constant

\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{SD}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}8\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{3}+2\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}^{2}

such that, for
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}
,
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
and
|\theta|\leq\min(1,\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1},3\mathrm{R}_{2}/(8\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}))
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),t\right)\right]=-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),\qquad|\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{SD}}\Sigma(t)^{2}\theta^{2}.

(105)

The coefficient
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)
is the correction that cancels the first non-zero odd contribution to the imaginary level of the phase. For the descent property, estimate (
105
) is the decisive input: after differentiating the real part along the curve, the leading term is the negative quadratic contribution and every other contribution is bounded by a constant times
\Sigma(t)^{2}\theta^{2}
, uniformly over
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. We now fix the radius once and for all by setting

\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\min\left(1,\frac{1}{\mathrm{M}_{2}},\frac{3\mathrm{R}_{2}}{8\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}},\frac{1}{4\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{SD}}}\right).

If
