0<\theta\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}
, then (
102
) and (
105
) give

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),t\right)\right]\leq-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}\theta+\frac{1}{4}\Sigma(t)^{2}\theta<0.

If
-\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}\leq\theta<0
, then (
102
) and (
105
) instead give

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),t\right)\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}|\theta|-\frac{1}{4}\Sigma(t)^{2}|\theta|>0,

because now
-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta
is positive and the remainder is bounded by
\frac{1}{4}\Sigma(t)^{2}|\theta|
. Therefore

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),t\right)\right]<0,\quad\text{for all }\theta\in(0,\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}),

and

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t),t\right)\right]>0,\quad\text{for all }\theta\in(-\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}},0).

These two inequalities give the descent property in
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))
. Taking
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD},0}
completes the proof.
∎

Since
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
is known to be a descent curve only in a neighbourhood of the stationary point, we splice it with
\Phi_{u}^{\mathrm{C}}
to obtain the global contour used in the steepest-descent analysis. The statement is formulated on the endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, because this is the only range used in Proposition
2.11
and it is the range on which the constants are uniform.

Corollary 2.9
:

There exist constants
\theta_{\mathrm{C}}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
, every auxiliary path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, and every
\theta\in(0,\theta_{\mathrm{C}})
, the following concatenation of
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\cdot,t)
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}
is an injective descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
in the logarithmic domain
\Omega
from Proposition
2.7
, from the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
. Set

\phi_{\theta}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta,\qquad U_{\theta}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta}{\theta}\pi,

where
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}=(\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2})/2
is the circular radius from Proposition
2.7
, and define
\Phi_{y}(\cdot,t,\theta):[-U_{\theta},U_{\theta})\to\mathbb{C}
by

\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}\!\left(\dfrac{\theta u}{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta}\right),&-U_{\theta}\leq u<-\phi_{\theta},\\
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t),&-\phi_{\theta}\leq u\leq\phi_{\theta},\\
\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}\!\left(\dfrac{\theta u}{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta}\right),&\phi_{\theta}<u<U_{\theta}.\end{cases}

The corresponding closed contour is used in contour integrals with positive orientation, opposite to the increasing-
u
parametrisation above. When a compact parametrisation is needed, we use the endpoint convention
\Phi_{y}(U_{\theta},t,\theta)=\Phi_{y}(-U_{\theta},t,\theta)
. Set

\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\dfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\bigl(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta\bigr)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}(1-\cos(\theta)),&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty,\\
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}(1-\cos(\theta)),&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty.\end{cases}

Moreover, with this positive orientation, the interior of
\Phi_{y}(\cdot,t,\theta)
encloses the sequence of spatial rates
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
.

Proof.

The proof has two parts. First we check that the local and circular pieces meet exactly and that the local piece remains inside the neighbourhood from Proposition
2.8
. Then we choose
\theta_{\mathrm{C}}
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
so that the centre of the circular piece satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
2.7
. By the definition of
\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)
, for every
\theta\in(0,\pi)
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\left(1-\cos(\theta)+\mathrm{i}\sin(\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin(\theta)+\dfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\left(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin(\theta)\right)^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)},&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty,\\
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\mathrm{i}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin(\theta),&\text{if }\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty,\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta,t).

Thus the two pieces join at the point corresponding to
u=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta
; the same computation with
-\theta
gives the join at
u=-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta
. Since
\sin(\theta)\leq\theta
for
\theta>0
, (
104
) gives, for
0<u\leq\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta
and
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD}}
,

\left|\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\theta.

We therefore require
\theta\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}/(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}})
, which ensures that the whole local piece lies in
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))
and is a descent segment by Proposition
2.8
. It remains to control the centre
\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)
. By (
103
),
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta\leq\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\theta
, and
1-\cos(\theta)\leq\theta^{2}/2
, whenever
\theta\leq(\mathrm{M}_{2}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}})^{-1}
we have

|\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)|\leq|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|+\left(2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}}{2}\right)\theta^{2}.

(106)

We now make the separation from the real singularities explicit. Set

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{8}\min\left(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{U}^{-1},\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1}\right).

By (
93
), the point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
is bounded by
2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}
uniformly on
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Since
\gamma(t)
can be made arbitrarily small by increasing the deterministic lower time, the endpoint stationary-point construction used at the start of Proposition
2.8
gives a threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C},1}\in\mathscr{T}
such that

|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C},1},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}.

Define

\theta_{\mathrm{C}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\min\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{1}{\mathrm{M}_{2}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}},\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{SD}}}{\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}},\frac{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}}{\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}},\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}}{2\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}^{3}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}/2}\right)^{1/2},\left(\frac{2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}}{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}}\right)^{1/2}\right).

Finally, set
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD}},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C},1})
. Then (
106
) implies

|\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)|<2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{B}_{2}-\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{4},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t},\quad 0<\theta<\theta_{\mathrm{C}}.

This verifies the gap condition required in Proposition
2.7
. Moreover, whenever

\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta<|u|<\left(\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta}{\theta}\right)\pi,

the angle
\widetilde{\theta}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\theta u/(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta)
lies in
(-\pi,-\theta)\cup(\theta,\pi)\subset[-\pi,\pi)
. Hence the circular segment is exactly the map
\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}(\widetilde{\theta})
from Proposition
2.7
with centre
\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)
, and is therefore a descent contour on that segment. Since
\theta/(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta)>0
for
0<\theta<\pi
, this affine change of parameter preserves the sign of the derivative of the real part.

We next record injectivity of the splice. The local piece is injective by Proposition
2.8
, and the two retained circular pieces are injective by Proposition
2.7
. Their only common points are the two splice endpoints. Indeed, the local piece has imaginary part in
[-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta,\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta]
. On the retained circular arc, if
\theta<|\widetilde{\theta}|<\pi-\theta
, then
|\sin\widetilde{\theta}|>\sin\theta
, so the imaginary part is outside this interval. If instead
\pi-\theta\leq|\widetilde{\theta}|<\pi
, then, since
\theta<\theta_{\mathrm{C}}\leq\pi/2
,

\mathfrak{Re}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}(\widetilde{\theta})\right]\geq\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}(1+\cos\theta)>2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}},

where the last inequality uses
|\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)|<2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}
and
4\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}<\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}
. By contrast, the local real-part bound in (
104
) and the choice of
\theta_{\mathrm{C}}
give
|\mathfrak{Re}[\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)]|\leq 2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}
on the local piece. Thus the local piece does not meet the retained circular arc except at the splice endpoints. Combining this injectivity with the descent property of the local segment from Proposition
2.8
and the circular descent property above proves that
\Phi_{y}(\cdot,t,\theta)
is a descent contour from
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
.

It remains to justify the enclosure statement for the closed concatenation. This is the topology shown in Figure
12
: the red circular part is a large contour around the spatial rates, and the blue segment replaces only a small left-hand arc near the stationary point. To make this precise, first consider the complete circle
\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}([-\pi,\pi))
, viewed as a closed curve with its positive boundary orientation when discussing winding numbers. Its left and right real-axis intersection points are
\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)
and
\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)+2\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}
, so the real points lying inside the circle are exactly those between these two values. Since
|\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)|<2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}
, we have
-\mathfrak{U}^{-1}<\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)<\mathfrak{L}
, so the left intersection point is strictly to the right of every geometric pole
-\beta_{n}^{-1}<-\mathfrak{U}^{-1}
and strictly to the left of every spatial rate
\lambda_{n}>\mathfrak{L}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)+2\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}>\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2}-2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}>\mathfrak{B}_{1}
, because
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}=(\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2})/2
and
2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}<\mathfrak{B}_{2}
. Hence the complete circle encloses every spatial rate and no geometric pole; equivalently, with positive orientation, its winding number is one around each spatial rate and zero around each geometric pole.

The spliced contour
\Phi_{y}(\cdot,t,\theta)
is obtained from this complete circle by removing the small circular arc with
|\widetilde{\theta}|\leq\theta
and inserting the local steepest-descent segment
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
. Both curves have the same endpoints by the matching computation above. Moreover, both lie in the singularity-free strip
-\mathfrak{U}^{-1}<\mathfrak{Re}(w)<\mathfrak{L}
. Indeed, on the omitted circular arc,

\left|\mathfrak{Re}\left[\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}(\widetilde{\theta})\right]\right|\leq|\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)|+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}(1-\cos\widetilde{\theta})<2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}+\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\theta^{2}}{2}\leq 3\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}},

while on the blue local segment, (
104
) and the choice of
\theta_{\mathrm{C}}
give

\left|\mathfrak{Re}\left[\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)\right]\right|\leq|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|+\left|\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right|\leq 2\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}.

Since
3\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{gap}}<\min(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{U}^{-1})
, both bounds place the relevant replacement region inside the strip. This strip contains no spatial rates and no geometric poles. Because the strip is convex, the omitted circular arc can be deformed to the inserted local segment, with endpoints fixed, without crossing any of these real singularities. The replacement therefore preserves the winding numbers computed for the complete circle. Consequently, the interior of the concatenated contour encloses the spatial rates
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
and excludes the geometric poles.
∎

illustrates schematically how the local segment
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
from Proposition
2.8
meets the translated circular arc
\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}
from Corollary
2.9
, relative to the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
and the axis markers for the geometric poles
-\beta_{n}^{-1}
, the spatial rates
\lambda_{n}
, and the Bernoulli zeros
\alpha_{n}^{-1}
. The three panels correspond to the sign regimes
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)<0
,
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)=0
, and
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)>0
, which determine whether the local correction bends the near-vertical branch to the left, leaves it vertical, or bends it to the right.

Figure 12
:
Schematic contour geometry for Corollary
2.9
. In each panel, the blue curve is the local segment
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
and the red arc is the circular piece
\Phi_{\mathrm{c}(\theta,t)}^{\mathrm{C}}
used to close the contour. The black dot marks the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, and the real-axis markers indicate the relative locations of the geometric poles
-\beta_{n}^{-1}
, the spatial rates

, and the Bernoulli zeros
\alpha_{n}^{-1}
. From left to right, the panels show the three sign regimes for
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)
.

Lemma 2.10

(Endpoint reduction on the complementary contour)
.

Fix
t\in\mathscr{T}
, a path
y\in\mathscr{P}
with
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, angles
\theta_{\star}\in(0,\theta_{\mathrm{C}})
and a cutoff
\vartheta\in(0,\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star})
such that Corollary
2.9
applies at
(t,\theta_{\star})
. Let
\Gamma(t)
denote the resulting closed contour from that corollary, with the endpoint convention from Corollary
2.9
, parametrized as

\Gamma(t)=\left\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):-\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}}{\theta_{\star}}\pi\leq u\leq\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}}{\theta_{\star}}\pi\right\}.

Define the
\vartheta
-short and
\vartheta
-long pieces

\Gamma_{1}^{(\vartheta)}(t)=\left\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):|u|\leq\vartheta\right\},\qquad\Gamma_{2}^{(\vartheta)}(t)=\left\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):\vartheta\leq|u|\leq\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}}{\theta_{\star}}\pi\right\}.

Then, we have

\max_{z\in\Gamma_{2}^{(\vartheta)}(t)}\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(z,t)\bigr]\leq\max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}_{y}\bigl(\Phi_{y}(\sigma\vartheta,t,\theta_{\star}),t\bigr)\bigr].

(107)

Proof.

Write
U\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin(\theta_{\star})/\theta_{\star}\cdot\pi
, so
\Gamma(t)=\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):-U\leq u\leq U\}
and
\Gamma_{2}^{(\vartheta)}(t)=\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):\vartheta\leq|u|\leq U\}
, with the two endpoint values
u=-U
and
u=U
identified.
On each local outward interval where
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}
and
\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})=\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)
, the map
u\mapsto\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})
is a descent curve for
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
from
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
in the sense of Definition
2.5
, with interior stationary parameter
u=0
.
Hence Proposition
2.6
applies on the compact intervals

J_{+}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}[\vartheta,\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}]\quad\text{and}\quad J_{-}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}[-\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star},-\vartheta],

showing that the maximum of
\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(\cdot,t,\theta_{\star}),t)]
over each local outward branch is attained at
u=\pm\vartheta
. On each circular subarc of
\Gamma(t)
, the trace agrees with a translated circular template from Proposition
2.7
with centre
\mathrm{c}(\theta_{\star},t)
from Corollary
2.9
, hence is a descent curve in its native angle variable. The final sentence in the proof of Corollary
2.9
shows that the affine reparametrisation in
u
preserves the sign of the derivative of
\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}]
along that circular piece. Therefore Proposition
2.6
applies on each compact circular subinterval contained in
\Gamma_{2}^{(\vartheta)}(t)
, and continuity at the identified endpoint
u=\pm U
covers the closed circular piece. Hence the maximum of
\mathfrak{Re}[\mathfrak{F}_{y}]
over the circular part of
\Gamma_{2}^{(\vartheta)}(t)
is attained at the splice parameters
u=\pm\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}
. Finally, since
\vartheta<\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}
, the same descent monotonicity along the local outward branches implies

\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(\pm\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star},t,\theta_{\star}),t)\bigr]\leq\mathfrak{Re}\bigl[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(\pm\vartheta,t,\theta_{\star}),t)\bigr],

because moving from
|u|=\vartheta
to
|u|=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}
along
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\cdot,t)
moves away from
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
along a descent segment. Combining the local and circular cases yields (
107
).
∎

2.5
Decoupling initial data

This section completes the deterministic one-particle analysis of the Markov kernel. Throughout the section
y\in\mathscr{P}
is a path, and the endpoint condition is imposed by requiring
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. On this endpoint window, the uniform versions of
(ii)
–
(iv)
in Proposition
2.3
and the stationary-point construction in Proposition
2.4
identify and approximate
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
with constants independent of the particular path satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. The key outcome of this subsection is a kind of decoupling principle. The next proposition gives the relevant expansion uniformly over the deterministic endpoint window.

Proposition 2.11
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. There exist constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
is well defined and belongs to
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
. Moreover, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, with the explicit constant
\mathrm{C}_{x}
defined in (
133
), independently of
t
and
y
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}{\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}-\sum_{n=0}^{x}\frac{\rho_{x}^{(n)}\left(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)}{n!}\eta_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{\frac{2}{3}}}.

(108)

The coefficients
\eta_{n,y}(t)
are defined by (
112
) in the proof below.
Finally, for each fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
there is a lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
such that, with the convention
(-1)!!=1
and with the explicit constant
\mathrm{C}_{n}
defined in (
138
), independently of
t
and
y
,

\displaystyle\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)-\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}(n-1)!!}{\Sigma(t)^{n}}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{n}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{-n},\quad\text{if $n$ is even,}

(109)

\displaystyle\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{n}\Sigma(t)^{-n-1},\quad\text{if $n$ is odd.}

The bound (
108
) holds for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. For each fixed
n
, the coefficient bounds in (
109
) hold for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}
and every such path.

Proof.

Outline.
The proof is long and technical so we give an outline. We fix a deterministic lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
by finitely many increases, each justified by uniform bounds on the endpoint window
\{y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
from Proposition
2.3
and Proposition
2.4
.
The argument is organised in seven labelled segments below (Step 4 is subdivided into Steps 4a–4c). Step 1 fixes the deterministic threshold and deforms (
14
) to the descent contour from Corollary
2.9
. Step 2 splits that contour into the central arc
\Gamma_{1,y}(t)
and the complementary arc
\Gamma_{2,y}(t)
, with Lemma
2.10
in Section
2.4
providing the endpoint reduction on
\Gamma_{2,y}(t)
. Step 3 expands the polynomial prefactor
\rho_{x}
on
\Gamma_{1,y}(t)
and introduces the normalised coefficients
\eta_{n,y}(t)
. Steps 4a–4c estimate the local integrals
\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)
by passing to the steepest-descent parameter, expanding the phase, and evaluating the resulting Gaussian main term with explicit remainders. Step 5 proves exponential smallness of the complementary integral
I_{2,x,y}(t)
. Step 6 proves the denominator lower bound, transfers the local integral estimates to
\eta_{n,y}(t)
, and replaces the exact curvature by
\Sigma(t)^{2}
, yielding (
109
). Step 7 combines the exact decomposition with the complementary-contour estimate to obtain (
108
).
Step 1
threshold and
contour deformation
Step 2
central/complement
split
Step 3
prefactor expansion
and
\eta_{n,y}
Steps 4a–c
local integrals
and Gaussian term
Step 5
complementary
tail
I_{2,x,y}
Step 6
denominator and
coefficients
Step 7
final ratio
estimate

Step 1: threshold and contour deformation.

We construct the threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
appearing in the statement by starting from a deterministic lower time that dominates the uniform endpoint-window estimates in Proposition
2.3
and Proposition
2.4
, and then increasing it finitely many further times as the argument demands; each increase is justified by a uniform inequality on
\{y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
, hence is independent of the particular path
y
. By Proposition
2.4
, once the lower time dominates
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
, the point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
is the unique real stationary point identified by Definition
1.18
for every path satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. The localization estimate (
93
) and the fact that
\gamma(t)\to 0
allow us to increase
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
throughout the endpoint window. We shall take

\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{8\,2^{1/3}}.

We now fix
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and a path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, and fix the splicing angle

\theta_{\star}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\theta_{\mathrm{C}},\quad\text{and set}\quad\phi_{\star}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin(\theta_{\star}),

where
\theta_{\mathrm{C}}
is the constant from Corollary
2.9
and
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}=(\mathfrak{B}_{1}+\mathfrak{B}_{2})/2
is the circular radius from Proposition
2.7
. After increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
, that corollary gives the descent contour

\Gamma_{y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):-\frac{\phi_{\star}}{\theta_{\star}}\pi\leq u<\frac{\phi_{\star}}{\theta_{\star}}\pi\right\}.

This contour is the positively oriented contour determined by Corollary
2.9
; when
u
increases, the parametrisation gives the descent direction and therefore runs in the opposite orientation. The contour constructed in Corollary
2.9
is homologous to the original Cauchy contour from (
14
) in the domain obtained by deleting the spatial poles
\{\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{y(t)}\}
and the geometric poles. The Bernoulli factors are zeros in the branch-free product form of
\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}}
, and hence do not obstruct this deformation. More explicitly, both contours have winding number one around each spatial pole
\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{y(t)}
and winding number zero around every geometric pole; the connecting homotopy stays inside the holomorphic domain of the full integrand away from those spatial poles. Consequently, Cauchy’s theorem applied to the meromorphic integrand
\rho_{x}(w)\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)}
gives the same residue sum and permits the deformation in (
14
):

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{y(t)}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\Gamma_{y}(t)}\rho_{x}(w)\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)}\mathrm{d}w.

We now choose the local cutoff

\theta_{y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|^{-1/3}.

By the lower comparison in (
102
),
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{2}/2
on the endpoint window, and
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
by
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
. Hence
\theta_{y}(t)\leq 2^{1/3}\Sigma(t)^{-2/3}
and
\theta_{y}(t)\to 0
uniformly on the endpoint window. We choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
to dominate the lower time for which
2^{1/3}\Sigma(t)^{-2/3}<\phi_{\star}
; then
\theta_{y}(t)<\phi_{\star}
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

Step 2: central arc versus complementary arc.

We split the contour integral into two parts: for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

I_{i,x,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{y(t)}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{i,y}(t)}\rho_{x}(w)\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)}\mathrm{d}w,

where we define the two segments of
\Gamma_{y}(t)
,
\Gamma_{1,y}(t)
and
\Gamma_{2,y}(t)
as follows:

\Gamma_{1,y}(t)=\left\{\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}):u\in\left[-\theta_{y}(t),\theta_{y}(t)\right]\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma_{2,y}(t)=\Gamma_{y}(t)\setminus\Gamma_{1,y}(t).

Step 3: Taylor expansion of the prefactor and coefficient normalisation.

Since
\rho_{x}
is a polynomial of degree
x
, its Taylor expansion around
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
is finite. Expanding
\rho_{x}
inside the integral defining
I_{1,x,y}(t)
therefore gives

I_{1,x,y}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{x}\frac{\rho_{x}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{n!}\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t),

(110)

where for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we define

\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{y(t)}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Gamma_{1,y}(t)}\left(w-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)}\mathrm{d}w.

(111)

Equivalently,

I_{1,x,y}(t)=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{x}\frac{\rho_{x}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{n!}\eta_{n,y}(t)\right)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right],

where
\eta_{n,y}(t)
is defined by

\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)=\eta_{n,y}(t)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right].

(112)

This quotient is legitimate on the time range ultimately used in the proposition: Step 6 proves the explicit lower bound (
134
), and hence
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]>0
, uniformly on the endpoint window after the stated lower-time increase.
Subsequently, setting
x=0
, we can use this decomposition to obtain

\mathbb{P}_{0}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]=\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)+I_{2,0,y}(t),

and therefore, whenever
|I_{2,0,y}(t)|\leq|\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)|/2
, the coefficient
\eta_{n,y}(t)
differs from the local ratio by the explicit bound

\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)-\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)}{\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)}\right|\leq 2\left|\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)}{\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)}\right|\left|\frac{I_{2,0,y}(t)}{\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)}\right|.

(113)

The regime
|I_{2,0,y}(t)|\leq|\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)|/2
used here is recorded in (
135
) at the start of Step 6.
Indeed, (
113
) is obtained by writing
\eta_{n,y}(t)=\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)/(\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)+I_{2,0,y}(t))
and applying the elementary inequality
|(1+z)^{-1}-1|\leq 2|z|
for
|z|\leq 1/2
.

Step 4a: Local parametrisation and Taylor coefficients of the phase.

It remains to estimate the local integrals
\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)
and the complementary contour integral
I_{2,x,y}(t)
explicitly. For the local part, we write
\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)
in parametric form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{y(t)}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\theta_{y}(t)}^{\theta_{y}(t)}\left(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)^{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}),t)}\mathrm{d}u.

(114)

In (
111
), the integral over
\Gamma_{1,y}(t)
uses the positive orientation inherited from
\Gamma_{y}(t)
. On the local segment, the increasing-
u
parametrisation
u\mapsto\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})
runs through the same geometric arc in the reverse direction from the contour orientation on
\Gamma_{1,y}(t)
; therefore the sign from reversing the parametrisation cancels the minus sign in (
111
), giving (
114
). By construction of
\Phi
, for
t\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\theta_{y}(t)<\phi_{\star}
, we are integrating over the segment of the contour corresponding to
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
.

The case
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
.

We treat this case in one self-contained paragraph, since all subsequent formulas simplify drastically. The definition of
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
gives
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2m+1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
for every
m\geq 1
,
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)=\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)+\mathrm{i}u
,
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)=0
, and
\chi_{y}(\theta,t)=0
. Consequently
\Phi_{y}(-u,t,\theta_{\star})-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)=-(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))
, all odd Taylor coefficients
G_{i,y}(t)
of
H_{y}
vanish, and
H_{y}(-u,t)=H_{y}(u,t)
on the local segment. Hence
\exp(H_{y}(u,t))
is even in
u
while
(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))^{n}=\mathrm{i}^{n}u^{n}
has parity
n
, so the integrand in (
114
) is odd whenever
n
is odd and
\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)=0
for every odd
n
. The remaining estimates for even
n
then reduce to the special case of the formulas below with
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)=0
and the single term
k=0
retained; explicitly, the parenthesis in the rescaled integral (
124
) collapses to the single vertical term
\mathrm{i}^{n+1}u^{n}/\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{(n+1)/2}
. Thus the local bounds (
128
), (
129
), (
130
), and the normalising bound (
131
) below also hold in the case
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
, with the odd local integrals equal to zero and with the correction terms deleted.

The case
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
.

We now assume
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
throughout the remainder of Step 4. (A few formulas below still display both branches of
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
/
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
for compactness; under the standing assumption of this case only the
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
branch is in active use, the other being a place-holder that vanishes by the simplification of the preceding paragraph.) Substituting the formula for
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
into
\left(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)^{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})
yields the expansion

\displaystyle\left(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)^{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star})=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}\mathrm{i}^{n-k+1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\right)^{k}\mathfrak{c}_{n,k,y}(t)u^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)k+n-k},

(115)

where to reduce the complexity of later sums, we define

\mathfrak{c}_{n,k,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\binom{n}{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{0\leq k\leq n\}}+2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\binom{n}{k-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{1\leq k\leq n+1\}}.

To organise the local expansion, set

H_{y}(u,t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}),t)-\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t).

By
(i)
of Proposition
2.3
,
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
is holomorphic in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
. Since
\theta_{y}(t)<\phi_{\star}
and the local segment is exactly
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
, (
104
) shows that the whole segment
\Gamma_{1,y}(t)
lies in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
once
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\theta_{y}(t)\leq 3\mathrm{R}_{2}/8
. We include this deterministic condition in the definition of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, again possible uniformly over paths
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
because
\theta_{y}(t)\leq 2^{1/3}\Sigma(t)^{-2/3}
. Therefore
H_{y}(\cdot,t)
is holomorphic on a fixed neighbourhood of the origin, and Taylor’s theorem gives

\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}),t)=\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}G_{n,y}(t)\frac{u^{n}}{n!},

(116)

where the Taylor coefficients are defined by

G_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}u^{n}}\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(u,t,\theta_{\star}),t)\Bigg|_{u=0}.

For
n\geq 1
, these are also the Taylor coefficients of
H_{y}(\cdot,t)
at the origin. By Faà di Bruno’s formula via exponential Bell polynomials,

G_{n,y}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(k)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)B_{n,k,y}(\Phi,t),\qquad\text{for all $n\geq 1$,}

where the path-dependent exponential Bell polynomials are defined, for each
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, by

\displaystyle B_{n,k,y}(\Phi,t)=n!\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n,k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n-k+1}\frac{\Phi_{y}^{(i)}(0,t)^{j_{i}}}{(i!)^{j_{i}}j_{i}!},\quad

\displaystyle\text{for $1\leq k\leq n$}

(117)

\displaystyle B_{n,k,y}(\Phi,t)=0

\displaystyle\text{ otherwise.}

and the combinatorial index set is

\mathcal{R}_{n,k}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\underline{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-k+1})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n-k+1}:\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}j_{i}=k\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{n-k+1}ij_{i}=n\right\}.

In the finite-
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
case, the definition of
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
gives, for all
n\geq 1
,

\Phi_{y}^{(n)}(0,t)=\mathrm{i}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{n=1\}}+\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)\,\mathbf{1}_{\{n=2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\}}.

(118)

Hence all derivatives of
\Phi_{y}
at the origin vanish except the first and the
2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
-th.

Combinatorial specialisation:

We now specialise the Bell-polynomial sum in (
117
) using the sparse derivative structure in (
118
). Since
\Phi_{y}^{(i)}(0,t)=0
unless
i=1
or
i=2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
, a nonzero term in (
117
) must satisfy
j_{i}=0
for every
i\notin\{1,2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)\}
. Thus the two partition constraints defining
\mathcal{R}_{n,k}
reduce to

j_{1}+j_{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}=k,\qquad j_{1}+2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)j_{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}=n.

Subtracting the first identity from the second shows that any contribution using the
2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
-th derivative must have

j_{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}=\frac{n-k}{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}r_{n,k,y}(t).

This quotient must belong to
\mathbb{Z}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
for the correction term; if it does not, no partition with a higher-derivative factor exists. Substituting (
118
) into (
117
) therefore gives

B_{n,k,y}(\Phi,t)=\mathrm{i}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{n=k\}}+R_{n,k,y}(t).

where
R_{n,k,y}(t)
is given by

R_{n,k,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\dfrac{n!}{k!}\binom{k}{r_{n,k,y}(t)}\left(\dfrac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\right)^{r_{n,k,y}(t)}\mathrm{i}^{k-r_{n,k,y}(t)},&\text{if }r_{n,k,y}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\setminus\{0\},\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The term
\mathrm{i}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{n=k\}}
corresponds to the pure first-derivative partition
j_{1}=k=n
, which is the case
r_{n,k,y}(t)=0
and is deliberately separated from
R_{n,k,y}(t)
. The remainder
R_{n,k,y}(t)
collects exactly the partitions with at least one block of size
2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
: when
r_{n,k,y}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
there are
r_{n,k,y}(t)
such blocks and
k-r_{n,k,y}(t)
first-derivative blocks, producing the displayed multinomial factor; otherwise the Bell constraints have no solution contributing to the correction term, and the remainder is zero.

Step 4b: Bell polynomials, tail bounds on
B_{n,y}
, and the Gaussian prefactor.

Therefore, using the stationary-point identity
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
, we obtain

G_{n,y}(t)=\mathrm{i}^{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)+\sum_{k=2}^{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(k)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)R_{n,k,y}(t).

(119)

We now read off the low-order coefficients from (
119
). If
n<2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
and
k\geq 2
, then
n-k<2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1
, so the quotient
r_{n,k,y}(t)=(n-k)/(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1)
cannot be a positive integer. Hence
R_{n,k,y}(t)=0
for every
k\geq 2
, and

G_{n,y}(t)=\mathrm{i}^{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t),\qquad\text{for all $n<2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)$.}

When
n=2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
, the only possible extra term is the one with
k=1
, and that term again vanishes because
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
. Hence

G_{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t),y}(t)=\mathrm{i}^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t).

Finally, when
n=2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1
, the only non-zero contribution in the sum in (
119
) comes from
k=2
, so

G_{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1,y}(t)=\mathrm{i}^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)+\mathrm{i}(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1)\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0,

where the final equality is exactly the definition of
\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)
. Moreover, by the definition of
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
,

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2m+1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0,\qquad\text{for all $m<\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)$.}

Combining the previous identities, we obtain

G_{n,y}(t)=0,\qquad\text{for all odd $n\leq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1$ in the finite-$\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)$ case.}

We now isolate the Gaussian quadratic term. Since

G_{2,y}(t)=\mathrm{i}^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t),

we now define the truncated Taylor coefficients as

\widetilde{G}_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}G_{n,y}(t)\mathbf{1}_{\{n\geq 3\}}.

Then the truncated Bell expansion is given by

H_{y}(u,t)=-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{G}_{n,y}(t)\frac{u^{n}}{n!},

(120)

and applying the generating function for complete exponential Bell polynomials gives

\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{G}_{n,y}(t)\frac{u^{n}}{n!}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}B_{n,y}(t)\frac{u^{n}}{n!},

(121)

where, in this context, the Bell coefficients are defined by

B_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}n!\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\widetilde{G}_{i,y}(t)^{j_{i}}}{(i!)^{j_{i}}j_{i}!},\quad

with the combinatorial index set
\mathcal{R}_{n}
, given by

\mathcal{R}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\underline{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}:\sum_{i=1}^{n}ij_{i}=n\right\}.

Therefore, the exponent in (
114
) excluding the
\exp(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t))
term, can be written as

\exp\left(H_{y}(u,t)\right)=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}B_{n,y}(t)\frac{u^{n}}{n!}\right)\exp\left(-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right).

Since
\widetilde{G}_{i,y}(t)=0
for
i=1,2
and for all odd
i\leq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1
(recall we have assumed
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
since Step 4a), if
n
is odd and
n\leq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1
, then every non-zero term in the Bell expansion of
B_{n,y}(t)
would have to involve only even indices, which is impossible. Therefore

B_{n,y}(t)=0,\qquad\text{for all odd $n$ with $n\leq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1$.}

To control the higher coefficients, we use the fixed holomorphic neighbourhood just obtained and Cauchy’s estimate. Choose

\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\min\left(\phi_{\star},\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1},\frac{3\mathrm{R}_{2}}{8\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}}\right).

Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that
\theta_{y}(t)\leq\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}
, the disk
\mathcal{B}_{2\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}}(0)
in the
u
-plane is mapped into
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
by the local contour parametrisation. Indeed, on this disk we use the analytic continuation of the local polynomial branch
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}
, not the globally spliced map, and the proof of (
104
) applies with
|u|
in place of the real variable
|\theta|
, because the correction term is a monomial in
u
and
2\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}\leq\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1}
. Explicitly, for every complex
u
with
|u|\leq 2\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}
,

|\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}|u|\leq 2\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}\leq\frac{3\mathrm{R}_{2}}{8}.

Together with
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}/8}(0)
, this gives
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
for every complex
u
with
|u|\leq 2\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}
. Hence all points used in Cauchy’s estimate lie in the region where
(iii)
of Proposition
2.3
applies. In particular, for the corresponding analytic continuation of
H_{y}
, the segment joining
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
to
\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(u,t)
lies inside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
whenever
|u|\leq 2\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}
, and so the first-derivative bound in
(iii)
gives

|H_{y}(u,t)|\leq\frac{9}{4}\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}\mathrm{R}_{2}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|.

Here the factor
9\mathrm{R}_{2}/8
bounds the distance from
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
to any point of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, while the extra factor
2
converts
\Sigma(t)^{2}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)
using (
102
). Thus Cauchy’s estimate on the circle
|u|=\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}
gives the following fully explicit coefficient bound. Define

\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{H}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{9}{4}\mathrm{M}_{1}\mathrm{M}_{2}\mathrm{R}_{2},\qquad\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max\left(1,\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}^{-1},\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{H}}\right)^{2}.

Then, since
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{r}_{\mathrm{H}}^{-n}\leq\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}
for every
n\geq 1
, we have

|G_{n,y}(t)|\leq n!\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|,\qquad\text{for all $n\geq 3$, $t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}$ and $y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}$.}

(122)

We now insert this coefficient bound into the complete Bell polynomial defining
B_{n,y}(t)
. First we record which indices can actually contribute. By definition,
\widetilde{G}_{i,y}(t)=0
for
i=1,2
. In addition, the parity cancellation proved above gives
G_{i,y}(t)=0
for every odd
i
in the infinite-
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
case, and for every odd
i\leq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1
in the finite case. Therefore a term in the Bell expansion for
B_{n,y}(t)
can be nonzero only if every index with
j_{i}>0
belongs to

A_{n,y}(t)=\begin{cases}\{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}:\text{$i\geq 4$ and $i$ is even}\},&\text{if $\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty$,}\\
\{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}:\text{$i\geq 4$ and either $i$ is even or $i\geq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+3$ is odd}\},&\text{if $\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty$.}\end{cases}

Thus the factors with
i\notin A_{n,y}(t)
either vanish, if
j_{i}>0
, or contribute
1
, if
j_{i}=0
. For the remaining active indices we apply the bound for
G_{i,y}(t)
with
i\geq 3
. The factorial
i!
in this bound cancels the denominator
(i!)^{j_{i}}
in the Bell polynomial, and the constraint
\sum_{i=1}^{n}ij_{i}=n
in the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
gives

\prod_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{ij_{i}}=\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}ij_{i}}\leq\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}.

Consequently, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,

|B_{n,y}(t)|\leq n!\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j_{i}!}\right)\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|^{\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}j_{i}}.

It remains only to estimate the number of active factors appearing in each nonzero Bell term. We increase
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, using the lower bound (
102
) and
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
from
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, so that

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\geq 1,\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}.

This allows us to replace a smaller power of
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)
by a larger one. If
n
is even, then every active index is at least
4
, so, for each
\underline{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
,

4\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}j_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}ij_{i}=n,\quad\text{which implies}\quad\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}j_{i}\leq\frac{n}{4}.

If
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
,
n
is odd, and
n\geq 2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+3
, then any nonzero term must contain at least one active odd index; otherwise the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{n}ij_{i}
would be even. The smallest possible active odd index is
2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+3
. After selecting one such odd index, every remaining active index is at least
4
, and hence

n=\sum_{i=1}^{n}ij_{i}\geq(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+3)+4\left(\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}j_{i}-1\right)=2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)-1+4\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}j_{i},

and therefore

\sum_{i\in A_{n,y}(t)}j_{i}\leq\frac{n-2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1}{4}.

We now make the remaining combinatorial constant explicit. Since
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is the full partition index set for the complete Bell polynomial, the sum over
\mathcal{R}_{n}
is the coefficient of
z^{n}
in the exponential generating product

\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j_{i}!}=[z^{n}]\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j_{i}=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{ij_{i}}}{j_{i}!}=[z^{n}]\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}z^{i}\right)=[z^{n}]\exp\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right).

Expanding the last expression once more gives the closed form

\sum_{\underline{j}\in\mathcal{R}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j_{i}!}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}\binom{n-1}{k-1},\qquad n\geq 1.

We therefore define the explicit constant

\mathrm{B}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}n!\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}\binom{n-1}{k-1},\qquad n\geq 1.

This constant is independent of
t
and
y
. Moreover, using
\binom{n-1}{k-1}\leq n^{k-1}/(k-1)!
and then setting
m=k-1
, we have

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k!}\binom{n-1}{k-1}\leq\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^{m}}{(m!)^{2}}\leq\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2\sqrt{n})^{2m}}{(2m)!}\leq\mathrm{e}^{2\sqrt{n}},

and hence
\mathrm{B}_{n}\leq n!\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{n}\mathrm{e}^{2\sqrt{n}}
. Thus the dependence of the Bell coefficient bound on
n
is fully explicit. Combining this with the two preceding active-index estimates gives

\displaystyle|B_{n,y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{B}_{n}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|^{\frac{n}{4}},\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all even $n\geq 1$,}

(123)

\displaystyle|B_{n,y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{B}_{n}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right|^{\frac{n-2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1}{4}},\qquad

\displaystyle\text{if $\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty$ and $n>2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1$ is odd.}

Step 4c: Rescaling, main terms, Gaussian moments, and local error bounds.

Continuing first in the finite-
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
case, set

\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\theta_{y}(t)\sqrt{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/6}.

We now rescale by the quadratic scale. Making the change of variables

u=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2}z

in (
114
), and then renaming
z
back to
u
, gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)}{\lambda_{y(t)}2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)}^{\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)}\Bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}\mathrm{i}^{n-k+1}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)}{(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!}\right)^{k}\mathfrak{c}_{n,k,y}(t)\frac{u^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)k+n-k}}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)k+n-k+1}{2}}}\Bigg)

(124)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}B_{m,y}(t)\frac{u^{m}}{m!\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{m/2}}\Bigg)\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}u.

(The case
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
has already been handled in Step 4a.) The Taylor series is evaluated at the original local parameter
\theta=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2}u
. Since
|u|\leq\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)
is equivalent to
|\theta|\leq\theta_{y}(t)
, the expansion remains inside the fixed holomorphic neighbourhood used in Step 4b. By the lower bound (
102
),
\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/6}\geq 2^{-1/6}\Sigma(t)^{1/3}
. Thus, for every fixed
\mathrm{L}\geq 1
, choosing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that
2^{-1/6}\Sigma(t)^{1/3}\geq\mathrm{L}
ensures that the rescaled integration interval contains
[-\mathrm{L},\mathrm{L}]
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

The estimates below repeatedly use the two facts established above: the definition
\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/6}
and the lower curvature bound
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{2}/2
from (
102
). First, define the explicit Gaussian moment constants, for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

\mathrm{G}_{r}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|u|^{r}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}u=2^{\frac{r+1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{r+1}{2}\right),\quad\text{and set}\quad\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{B}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\mathrm{e}^{3}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}.

We claim that, after increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, the Bell factor in (
124
) satisfies

\left|\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}B_{m,y}(t)\frac{u^{m}}{m!\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{m/2}}-1\right|\leq\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{B}}u^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2},\qquad|u|\leq\mathrm{A}_{y}(t),

(125)

and, on the same range,

\left|\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}B_{m,y}(t)\frac{u^{m}}{m!\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{m/2}}\right|\leq 2.

(126)

To prove (
125
), first note that
B_{1,y}(t)=B_{2,y}(t)=0
because
\widetilde{G}_{1,y}(t)=\widetilde{G}_{2,y}(t)=0
. For
m\geq 3
, combine the vanishing of the inadmissible odd Bell coefficients with (
123
). If
m
is even, (
123
) gives the power
m/4
. If
m
is odd and
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
with
m>2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)+1
, then the exponent in (
123
) is at most
m/4
; in all other odd cases,
B_{m,y}(t)=0
. Thus, once
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\geq 1
, the single bound
|B_{m,y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{B}_{m}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{m/4}
holds for every
m\geq 3
. The explicit formula for
\mathrm{B}_{m}
gives
\mathrm{B}_{m}/m!\leq\mathrm{e}(\mathrm{e}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}})^{m}
, because
\mathrm{e}^{2\sqrt{m}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{m+1}
for
m\geq 1
. Hence, for
|u|\leq\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/6}
,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}B_{m,y}(t)\frac{u^{m}}{m!\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{m/2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}\sum_{m=3}^{\infty}\left(\mathrm{e}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}|u|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/4}\right)^{m}.

Since
|u|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/4}\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/12}
, we increase
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that
\mathrm{e}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/12}\leq 1/2
. Enlarging the resulting constant, the same geometric-series estimate gives

\mathrm{e}\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\left(\mathrm{e}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}|u|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/4}\right)^{m}\leq 2\mathrm{e}^{3}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{2}u^{2}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2},

which proves (
125
). Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
again so that
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/6}\leq 1
, and using
|u|\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/6}
, gives (
126
).

We also need an explicit bound for the deviation of the local contour from the vertical tangent. Write

\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)=\mathrm{i}\theta+\chi_{y}(\theta,t),

where
\chi_{y}(\theta,t)=\mathfrak{a}_{y}(t)\theta^{2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)}/(2\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t))!
if
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)<\infty
, and
\chi_{y}(\theta,t)=0
if
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)=\infty
. Since
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{SD}}
, the contour-deviation estimate (
101
) from Proposition
2.8
gives, whenever
|\theta|\leq\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1}
,

|\chi_{y}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|^{2},\qquad|\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|.

We increase the common threshold so that
\theta_{y}(t)\leq\min(1,\mathrm{M}_{2}^{-1})
. Then, for each fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the product coming from the parametrisation satisfies

\left|\left(\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right)^{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\theta}\Phi_{y}^{\mathrm{SD}}(\theta,t)-\mathrm{i}^{n+1}\theta^{n}\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{poly},n}|\theta|^{n+1},

(127)

where

\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{poly},n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}\left(1+n(1+\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}})(2+\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}})^{n}\right).

Indeed, subtract and add
(\mathrm{i}\theta)^{n}(\mathrm{i}+\chi_{y}^{(1)}(\theta,t))
. The term containing
\chi_{y}^{(1)}
is bounded by
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}}|\theta|^{n+1}
. For the remaining term, the elementary inequality
|a^{n}-b^{n}|\leq n|a-b|(|a|+|b|)^{n-1}
, applied with
a=\mathrm{i}\theta+\chi_{y}(\theta,t)
and
b=\mathrm{i}\theta
, together with
|\mathrm{i}\theta+\chi_{y}(\theta,t)|\leq(1+\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{SD}})|\theta|
, gives the displayed bound.

We next identify the lowest-order term for even
n
. The contribution obtained by keeping the vertical factor
\mathrm{i}^{n+1}\theta^{n}
and the zeroth Bell coefficient is

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{main,even}}_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{i}^{n}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)}{\lambda_{y(t)}2\pi\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\int_{-\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)}^{\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)}u^{n}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}u.

If
n
is odd, this term is identically zero because the integration interval is symmetric and
u^{n}\exp(-u^{2}/2)
is odd. This observation avoids any need for a uniform bound on
\mathfrak{N}_{y}(t)
: all odd contributions are treated as errors forced either by the Bell tail (
125
) or by the contour correction (
127
). Combining (
125
), (
126
), and (
127
), and then using the Gaussian moments
\mathrm{G}_{n+1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{n+2}
, gives, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\left|\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)-\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{main,even}}_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{rem},n}}{\lambda_{y(t)}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+2}{2}}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right),

(128)

where the explicit constant is

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{rem},n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{G}_{n+2}+2\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{poly},n}\mathrm{G}_{n+1}\right).

To see (
128
), decompose the difference from the leading vertical Gaussian term as

\textnormal{vertical factor}\times\textnormal{Bell tail}\quad+\quad\textnormal{contour correction}\times\textnormal{full Bell factor}.

The first product is controlled by (
125
): the vertical part multiplied by the Bell tail contributes at most

\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathrm{G}_{n+2}}{2\pi\lambda_{y(t)}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+2}{2}}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right).

The contour-correction part is bounded by (
127
); after the change of variables
\theta=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2}u
, the extra power
|\theta|^{n+1}\mathrm{d}\theta
gives
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-(n+2)/2}|u|^{n+1}\mathrm{d}u
, and (
126
) supplies the factor
2
. We can now evaluate the even leading term using Gaussian moments. After increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, we have
\mathrm{A}_{y}(t)\geq 1
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. For
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, set

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail},p}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\sum_{\ell=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\ell}\ell!.

If
\mathrm{A}\geq 1
, then, for
u=\mathrm{A}+v
with
v\geq 0
, using
(\mathrm{A}+v)^{p}\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\ell}\mathrm{A}^{p-\ell}v^{\ell}\leq\mathrm{A}^{p}\sum_{\ell=0}^{p}\binom{p}{\ell}v^{\ell}
, and using
\exp(-(\mathrm{A}+v)^{2}/2)\leq\exp(-\mathrm{A}^{2}/2)\exp(-v)
, we get

2\int_{\mathrm{A}}^{\infty}u^{p}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\mathrm{d}u\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail},p}\mathrm{A}^{p}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{A}^{2}}{2}\right).

For each fixed even
n
, define a further lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}^{\mathrm{tail}}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail},n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{n/6}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right)\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}^{\mathrm{tail}},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}.

This is possible because
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{2}/2
and
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
. Thus, for even
n
, the truncation error in replacing the finite Gaussian moment by the full Gaussian moment contributes at most

\frac{1}{2\pi\lambda_{y(t)}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+2}{2}}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right).

Combining this truncation estimate with (
128
), and defining

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{rem},n}+\frac{1}{2\pi},

gives, for even
n
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)-\frac{\mathrm{i}^{n}(n-1)!!}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)\right|

(129)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},n}}{\lambda_{y(t)}\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+2}{2}}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right).

For the special case
n=0
, the additional truncation threshold is absorbed into the common
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, because the resulting estimate is used below to lower-bound the normalising probability.
If
n
is odd, then
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{main,even}}_{n,y}(t)=0
, and (
128
) gives directly

\left|\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{rem},n}}{\lambda_{y(t)}\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{\frac{n+2}{2}}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right).

(130)

In particular, taking
n=0
in (
129
), we obtain

\left|\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)-\frac{1}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},0}}{\lambda_{y(t)}\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right).

(131)

Step 5: exponential smallness on the complementary arc.

We next estimate the complementary contour integral
I_{2,x,y}(t)
. We apply Lemma
2.10
with
\vartheta=\theta_{y}(t)
. Its hypotheses are all in force:
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
by assumption;
\theta_{\star}\in(0,\theta_{\mathrm{C}})
by construction; Corollary
2.9
applies because
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{C}}
; and Step 1 imposed
\theta_{y}(t)<\phi_{\star}=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\sin\theta_{\star}
. Hence the phase bound (
107
) holds. Moreover
\Gamma_{2}^{(\theta_{y}(t))}(t)
agrees with the complementary contour
\Gamma_{2,y}(t)
from Step 2 up to the two cutoff endpoints, which do not affect the contour integral; the endpoint-reduction estimate is applied on the corresponding closure.
Since
\theta_{y}(t)<\phi_{\star}
, both splice points
\Phi_{y}(\pm\theta_{y}(t),t,\theta_{\star})
lie on the local segment where (
116
)–(
120
) holds. Fix
\sigma\in\{-1,1\}
. The coefficient bound (
122
), together with the threshold condition
\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}\theta_{y}(t)\leq 1/2
, gives the traceable tail bound

\left|\sum_{m=3}^{\infty}G_{m,y}(t)\frac{(\sigma\theta_{y}(t))^{m}}{m!}\right|\leq 2\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{3}\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta_{y}(t)^{3}.

Since
\theta_{y}(t)=\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/3}
, we can rewrite the right-hand side as

2\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{3}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/3}\cdot\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta_{y}(t)^{2}.

Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that

2\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{3}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/3}\leq\frac{1}{4},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t},

we obtain, for this same fixed
\sigma
,

\mathfrak{Re}\left[H_{y}(\sigma\theta_{y}(t),t)\right]\leq-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta_{y}(t)^{2}+2\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{H}}^{3}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta_{y}(t)^{3}\leq-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\theta_{y}(t)^{2}.

Therefore

\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(\sigma\theta_{y}(t),t,\theta_{\star}),t)\right]\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}.

Since the right-hand side is independent of
\sigma
, the endpoint maximum satisfies the same estimate:

\max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(\sigma\theta_{y}(t),t,\theta_{\star}),t)\right]\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}.

We now make the contour and polynomial prefactor constants explicit. The parameter interval for
\Gamma_{y}(t)
has length

2\mathrm{U}_{\Gamma},\qquad\mathrm{U}_{\Gamma}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\phi_{\star}}{\theta_{\star}}\pi.

On the local part the derivative of the parametrisation is bounded by
\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}}
, while on the circular part the change of variables in Corollary
2.9
gives the bound
\theta_{\star}/\sin(\theta_{\star})=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\theta_{\star}/\phi_{\star}
. Thus we set

\mathrm{A}_{\Gamma}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max\left(\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{SD}},\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{C}}\theta_{\star}}{\phi_{\star}}\right),\qquad\mathrm{L}_{\Gamma}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\mathrm{U}_{\Gamma}\mathrm{A}_{\Gamma},\qquad\mathrm{R}_{\Gamma}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{R}_{2}}{8}+\mathrm{U}_{\Gamma}\mathrm{A}_{\Gamma}.

Since
|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{R}_{2}/8
, every point of
\Gamma_{y}(t)
has modulus at most
\mathrm{R}_{\Gamma}
, and the length of
\Gamma_{y}(t)
is at most
\mathrm{L}_{\Gamma}
. Also, by
(A1)
,
\lambda_{k}\geq\mathfrak{L}
for every
k
, and hence

|\rho_{x}(w)|=\prod_{k=0}^{x-1}\left|1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{k}}\right|\leq\left(1+\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\Gamma}}{\mathfrak{L}}\right)^{x},\qquad|w|\leq\mathrm{R}_{\Gamma}.

Therefore, with

\mathrm{C}_{x,\Gamma}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathrm{L}_{\Gamma}}{2\pi\mathfrak{L}}\left(1+\frac{\mathrm{R}_{\Gamma}}{\mathfrak{L}}\right)^{x},\qquad x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

the prefactor and contour length in the definition of
I_{2,x,y}(t)
are bounded explicitly, uniformly over
t
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
:

\left|I_{2,x,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{x,\Gamma}\exp\left(\max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}\mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\Phi_{y}(\sigma\theta_{y}(t),t,\theta_{\star}),t)\right]\right).

(132)

Combining the previous displays yields the pointwise estimate

\left|I_{2,x,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{x,\Gamma}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right).

In anticipation of normalising this pointwise bound by
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]
, define the statement constant

\mathrm{C}_{x}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{x,\Gamma},\qquad x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(133)

Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
so that

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1}{8}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right),

for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, and then using the lower curvature estimate (
102
), we get

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{8}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}},

because
\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}=1/(8\,2^{1/3})
. The only missing ingredient for the normalised form is a lower bound on
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]
, which we prove next from the
n=0
local integral.

Step 6: from local integrals to
\eta_{n,y}(t)
and
\Sigma(t)
.

The denominator in the definition of
\eta_{n,y}(t)
is the full transition probability from the origin, not only the local Gaussian integral. We therefore first prove an explicit lower bound for this denominator. Let

\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}\right)^{-1}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)

denote the leading term in (
131
). Dividing the error term in (
131
) by
\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)
gives

\left|\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)-\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)\right|\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},0}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/2}\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t).

Similarly, the pointwise estimate from Step 5 and the upper rate bound
\lambda_{y(t)}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{1}
from
(A1)
give

|I_{2,0,y}(t)|\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{0,\Gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right)\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t).

Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, using (
102
) and
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
, we may assume that both coefficients on the right-hand sides of the preceding two displays are at most
1/4
, uniformly for
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Hence

|\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)|\geq\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t),\qquad\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t),

(134)

and also

\left|I_{2,0,y}(t)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)\right|,\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}},\quad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}.

(135)

The second inequality in (
134
) follows because
\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)>0
is real,
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]
is a probability, and the identity
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]=\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)+I_{2,0,y}(t)
together with the two one-quarter bounds gives
\left|\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]-\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)/2
. The domination (
135
) is stronger than what is needed for (
113
), but it records explicitly that the complementary contour cannot change the leading local mass. Combining (
134
) with the pointwise estimate for
I_{2,x,y}(t)
in Step 5 gives, for every fixed
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\frac{|I_{2,x,y}(t)|}{\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}\leq 2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{C}_{x,\Gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/3}\right).

The final threshold condition imposed at the end of Step 5 then turns this into the normalised complementary-contour estimate

\left|I_{2,x,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}}\mathbb{P}_{0}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right],

(136)

with
\mathrm{C}_{x}
exactly as defined in (
133
). We now pass from local integrals to the coefficients
\eta_{n,y}(t)
. For even
n
, set
\mathrm{Q}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(n-1)!!
, with the convention
\mathrm{Q}_{0}=(-1)!!=1
. The leading term in (
129
) can then be written as

(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{Q}_{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t).

Dividing the local error estimate (
129
) by
\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)
gives the explicit relative error

\left|\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)-(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{Q}_{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)\right|\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t).

Since
\eta_{n,y}(t)=\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)/\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]
, we separate the numerator error from the denominator normalisation. With

\mathrm{M}_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{Q}_{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n}{2}},

the triangle inequality gives

\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)-\mathrm{M}_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\frac{|\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)-\mathrm{M}_{n,y}(t)\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)|}{\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}+|\mathrm{M}_{n,y}(t)|\left|\frac{\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)}{\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}-1\right|.

The first term is bounded by the local estimate above and (
134
). The second term is bounded by writing
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]=\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)+(\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)-\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t))+I_{2,0,y}(t)
and using the two denominator errors displayed before (
134
). Therefore

\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)-(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{Q}_{n}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\right|\leq\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(0)}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}+\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(1)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n}{2}},

(137)

where the explicit constants are

\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(0)}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},n}+\mathrm{Q}_{n}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{loc},0}\right),\qquad\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(1)}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{Q}_{n}\mathrm{C}_{0,\Gamma}.

Indeed, the first term in
\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(0)}
comes from the numerator error in (
129
); the second comes from replacing
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]
by
\mathcal{L}_{0,y}(t)
in the denominator; and
\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(1)}
is the contribution of the complementary contour
I_{2,0,y}(t)
, after using the exponential absorption from Step 5.

It remains to replace the exact curvature
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)
by the deterministic scale
\Sigma(t)^{2}
. By
(iv)
from Proposition
2.3
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+\left|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\right|\right)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

The stationary-point smallness estimate (
93
) gives
|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|\leq 2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}
after increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
to dominate the lower time in Proposition
2.4
. Hence

\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}-1\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(1+2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\right)\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
once more, we may assume that the right-hand side is at most
1/2
. On this range the ratio
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)/\Sigma(t)^{2}
lies in
[1/2,3/2]
, and the mean-value theorem applied to
r\mapsto r^{-n/2}
gives

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n}{2}}-\Sigma(t)^{-n}\right|\leq n2^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(1+2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\right)\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{-n}.

Multiplying this curvature-replacement bound by
\mathrm{Q}_{n}=(n-1)!!
gives the first contribution to the even constant in (
138
).
Also, (
102
) gives
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-(n+1)/2}\leq 2^{(n+1)/2}\Sigma(t)^{-n-1}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-n/2}\leq 2^{n/2}\Sigma(t)^{-n}
. Since
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
by Definition
1.12
, we have
\gamma(t)^{1/2}\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/4}
; after increasing the
n
-dependent lower time so that
\Sigma(t)\geq 1
, this implies
\Sigma(t)^{-1}\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
. Increasing this lower time once more so that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}}\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
, the even estimate (
137
) becomes the even part of (
109
). We denote by
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}
the maximum of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
,
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}^{\mathrm{tail}}
when
n
is even, and the finitely many additional
n
-dependent lower times used in this paragraph. If
n
is odd, the leading Gaussian contribution is absent because the symmetric integral of
u^{n}\exp(-u^{2}/2)
is zero. The local estimate (
130
), divided by the denominator lower bound in (
134
), gives directly

\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-\frac{n+1}{2}},\qquad\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{\mathrm{odd}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\sqrt{2\pi}\,\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{rem},n}.

Applying (
102
) yields
\left|\eta_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq 2^{(n+1)/2}\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{\mathrm{odd}}\Sigma(t)^{-n-1}
, which is the odd part of (
109
); for odd
n
we take
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}
to be the maximum of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
and the lower times used in the denominator and local-remainder estimates above. Collecting the constants used in the even and odd cases, the constant in the statement may be taken to be

\mathrm{C}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}n2^{\frac{n}{2}}(n-1)!!\,\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(1+2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\right)+2^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(0)}+2^{\frac{n}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{(1)},&\text{if $n$ is even,}\\
2^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\mathrm{D}_{\eta,n}^{\mathrm{odd}},&\text{if $n$ is odd.}\end{cases}

(138)

Step 7: assembling (
108
).

Finally, the exact decomposition above gives

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{x}\frac{\rho_{x}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{n!}\eta_{n,y}(t)\right)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]+I_{2,x,y}(t).

Dividing by
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]
and applying (
136
) gives (
108
) with the explicit constant
\mathrm{C}_{x}
from (
133
).

On the thresholds.

The proof enlarges a starting lower time finitely many times, always using bounds uniform on
\{y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
.
The
x
-dependent expansion (
108
) uses the common threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, whose cumulative requirements are:
(i) applicability of Corollary
2.9
and the endpoint-window inputs from Propositions
2.3
and
2.4
, including
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
and
\theta_{y}(t)<\phi_{\star}
;
(ii) containment of the local contour segment in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
and the polydisk/Cauchy regime for
H_{y}(\cdot,t)
from Step 4;
(iii) the
n=0
local bound needed for the denominator lower estimate;
(iv) the complementary-contour regime for Lemma
2.10
at cutoff
\vartheta=\theta_{y}(t)
, the endpoint reduction (
107
), and the successive comparisons leading to (
136
);
(v) the domination (
135
) used to normalise the complementary-contour error.
Any deterministic time satisfying (i)–(v) may be taken as
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
. For the coefficient estimate (
109
), a fixed
n
may require the additional finite-time conditions from the Gaussian truncation and curvature-replacement steps; their maximum with
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
is the threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}
appearing in the statement.
∎

To see (
108
) concretely, it is useful to examine the homogeneous unit-rate process, where the transition probabilities are explicit. In this case the transition ratio can be expanded directly in the same Taylor basis as the decoupling sum. Example
2.12
shows how the saddle coordinate, the Taylor coefficients of
\rho_{x}
, and the even–odd hierarchy in (
109
) fit together in the simplest possible model.

Example 2.12

(Homogeneous unit rates: the decoupling expansion in Poisson form)
:

Work in continuous time with
\lambda_{n}\equiv 1
, fix
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and restrict to integer times
t\geq 1
. Then
\mathscr{Z}(t)=t
and
\Sigma(t)^{2}=t+1
. Let
y
be a path with
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. After increasing the lower time in a way depending only on the fixed value of
x
, the endpoint window gives
y(t)\geq x
. The stationary equation gives

\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)=1-\frac{y(t)+1}{t},\qquad\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)=\frac{\sqrt{t+1}}{t}(y(t)+1-t),\qquad\rho_{x}(w)=(1-w)^{x}.

Since
\mathscr{X}(t)-x
has distribution
\mathrm{Poisson}(t)
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
, the Poisson masses cancel and

\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}{\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}=\frac{y(t)!}{(y(t)-x)!t^{x}}=\prod_{j=0}^{x-1}\frac{y(t)-j}{t}.

(139)

We now write this exact ratio in the Taylor basis appearing in (
108
). Since

\frac{\rho_{x}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{n!}=(-1)^{n}\binom{x}{n}\left(\frac{y(t)+1}{t}\right)^{x-n},

(140)

coefficient extraction gives

\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}{\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]}=\sum_{n=0}^{x}(-1)^{n}\binom{x}{n}\left(\frac{y(t)+1}{t}\right)^{x-n}\!\!\!\!\tilde{\eta}_{n,y}(t),

(141)

where

\tilde{\eta}_{n,y}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}n![\zeta^{n}]\left\{\exp\left(\frac{y(t)+1}{t}\zeta\right)\left(1-\frac{\zeta}{t}\right)^{y(t)}\right\}=\frac{n!}{t^{n}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\binom{y(t)}{\ell}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}(y(t)+1)^{n-\ell}}{(n-\ell)!}.

(142)

To verify (
141
), multiply its right-hand side by
s^{x}/x!
and sum over
x
. The Taylor factors in (
140
) give

\exp\left(\frac{y(t)+1}{t}s\right)\sum_{n\geq 0}\tilde{\eta}_{n,y}(t)\frac{(-s)^{n}}{n!}=\exp\left(\frac{y(t)+1}{t}s\right)\exp\left(-\frac{y(t)+1}{t}s\right)\left(1+\frac{s}{t}\right)^{y(t)}=\left(1+\frac{s}{t}\right)^{y(t)},

which is the exponential generating function of the falling-factorial ratio in (
139
). The first coefficients already display the mechanism behind (
109
):

\tilde{\eta}_{0,y}(t)=1,\qquad\tilde{\eta}_{1,y}(t)=\frac{1}{t},\qquad\tilde{\eta}_{2,y}(t)=\frac{1-y(t)}{t^{2}}

(143)

and

\tilde{\eta}_{3,y}(t)=\frac{1-5y(t)}{t^{3}},\qquad\tilde{\eta}_{4,y}(t)=\frac{3y(t)^{2}-20y(t)+1}{t^{4}}.

In the central case
y(t)=t
, these identities compare directly with the Gaussian powers appearing in (
109
):

\left|\tilde{\eta}_{2,y}(t)+\Sigma(t)^{-2}\right|=\frac{1}{t^{2}(t+1)},\qquad\left|\tilde{\eta}_{4,y}(t)-3\Sigma(t)^{-4}\right|\leq\frac{69}{t^{3}},\qquad t\geq 1.

The odd coefficients displayed above are smaller than the corresponding even Gaussian scale in this central example. For comparison with the local coefficients in (
111
), the homogeneous local contour can also be written explicitly. On the local steepest-descent circle, set

w(\phi)=1-\frac{y(t)+1}{t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi},\qquad\phi_{t}=\arcsin\left(\frac{t}{y(t)+1}\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{-1/3}\right),

after the lower time has been chosen so that the argument of the arcsine is at most one. The change of variables in (
111
), restricted to the symmetric arc
|\phi|\leq\phi_{t}
, gives

\eta_{n,y}(t)=\frac{y(t)!\,(y(t)+1)^{n-y(t)}}{2\pi t^{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}J_{y(t)-k}\big(y(t)+1,\phi_{t}\big),

where

J_{r}(A,\phi)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\int_{-\phi}^{\phi}\exp\!\left(A\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\psi}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}r\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\psi=\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{A^{q}}{q!}\,\mathrm{S}_{q-r}(\phi),\qquad\mathrm{S}_{\ell}(\phi)=\begin{cases}2\sin(\ell\phi)/\ell,&\ell\neq 0,\\
2\phi,&\ell=0.\end{cases}

Thus the only distinction between the local coefficients
\eta_{n,y}(t)
and the full-ratio coefficients
\tilde{\eta}_{n,y}(t)
is the angular range of integration. Indeed, since
J_{r}(A,\pi)=2\pi A^{r}/r!
for
r\geq 0
, for each fixed
n
there is a lower time after which
n\leq y(t)
, and replacing
\phi_{t}
by
\pi
in the preceding display gives exactly (
142
). For instance, at
t=1000
and
y(t)=1000
, the rounded values are

\begin{array}[]{c|cc}n&\eta_{n,y}(t)&\tilde{\eta}_{n,y}(t)\\
\hline\cr 0&0.99846&1\\
1&1.00975\times 10^{-3}&1.00000\times 10^{-3}\\
2&-9.80590\times 10^{-4}&-9.99000\times 10^{-4}\\
3&-4.90769\times 10^{-6}&-4.99900\times 10^{-6}\\
4&2.75659\times 10^{-6}&2.98000\times 10^{-6}\end{array}

The exact coefficients
\tilde{\eta}_{n,y}(t)
in (
142
) come from the full Poisson transition ratio, whereas
\eta_{n,y}(t)
is defined through the local contour integral in Step 3 of Proposition
2.11
.

The same local estimates also give a kind of local limit theorem which, although not used directly in the proof, could be of independent interest.

Corollary 2.13

(Stationary-point local limit)
:

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
