. For every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there exist
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{K}_{x}>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
and every path
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfying
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the minimizer in Definition
1.18
is unique and equals
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, with

\left|\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]-\frac{\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi}\,\Sigma(t)}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\right)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{K}_{x}\gamma(t)^{1/2}}{\Sigma(t)}.

(144)

Here
\mathrm{K}_{x}
depends only on
x
and the fixed structural constants from Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
2.3
, and in particular is independent of the choice of
y
once
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. We construct
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
by starting from the threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
in Proposition
2.11
and increasing it finitely many times; these increases may depend on this fixed
x
, but remain independent of
y
because all endpoint estimates invoked below are uniform on
\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Let
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
, and let
y\in\mathscr{P}
satisfy
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. The proof of Proposition
2.11
gives the exact decomposition

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{x}\frac{\rho_{x}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{n!}\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)+I_{2,x,y}(t).

We first isolate the exact saddle approximation. Since (
93
) and
\gamma(t)\to 0
put
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
in a fixed compact subset of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
after increasing the lower time, the finitely many derivatives
\rho_{x}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))
,
0\leq n\leq x
, are bounded by a constant depending only on
x
. The
n=0
estimate (
131
), together with the lower rate bound
\lambda_{y(t)}\geq\mathfrak{L}
from
(A1)
and the curvature lower bound (
102
), gives

\left|\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)-\frac{\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{K}_{x,0}}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}.

Here we have also used
\exp(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t))\leq 1
. To justify this last inequality, note that
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(0,t)=0
,
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
by (
77
), and (
102
) together with
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
makes
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)
non-negative on the real segment between
0
and
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
after increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
. This segment lies inside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
, so the logarithmic branches in Definition
1.17
are real-valued along it. Thus the real function
w\mapsto\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)
is convex on that segment and has a stationary point at
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, so
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\leq\mathfrak{F}_{y}(0,t)=0
. It remains to control the terms with
n\geq 1
and the complementary contour. For each fixed
1\leq n\leq x
, the local estimates (
129
) and (
130
), together with the coefficient bounds preceding them, imply

\left|\mathcal{I}_{n,y}(t)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{K}_{x,n}}{\Sigma(t)^{2}},

after increasing the lower time, uniformly over
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Indeed, every such term contains at least one additional inverse power of
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)^{1/2}
compared with
\mathcal{I}_{0,y}(t)
, and (
102
) converts this into at least one additional inverse power of
\Sigma(t)
. Also, (
136
), the trivial bound
\mathbb{P}_{0}[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)]\leq 1
, and the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}}\leq\Sigma(t)^{-2}
after increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
show that
|I_{2,x,y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{tail}}\Sigma(t)^{-2}
. Summing the finitely many bounds for
0\leq n\leq x
yields the exact-saddle estimate

\left|\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=y(t)\right]-\frac{\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{ex}}}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}.

(145)

We now replace the exact saddle height and curvature by the stationary-point coordinate. On the present time range, (
77
) gives
\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)=-\Sigma(t)\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(0,t)=0
and
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=0
, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives the exact identity

\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)^{2}=-\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}r\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(r\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}r.

The real segment from
0
to
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
lies inside
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
after the same lower-time increase used above. Applying
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
on this segment and using (
93
) gives a constant
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}>0
, independent of
t
and
y
, such that

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}.

Similarly,
(iv)
and (
93
) imply

\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}-1\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{curv}}\gamma(t)^{1/2},

for a constant
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{curv}}>0
independent of
t
and
y
. Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
so that the right-hand side is at most
1/2
, the mean-value theorem applied to
r\mapsto r^{-1/2}
on
[1/2,3/2]
gives

\left|\frac{\Sigma(t)}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}}-1\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{curv}}^{\prime}\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

The phase-height estimate above still contains the factor
\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}
, which can be absorbed into a uniform upper bound after the Gaussian decay is retained. Writing
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)=-\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}/2+\mathcal{E}_{y}(t)
, we have
|\mathcal{E}_{y}(t)|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}
. After increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
so that
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1/4
, the elementary estimate
|\mathrm{e}^{z}-1|\leq|z|\mathrm{e}^{|z|}
yields

\left|\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\right).

Since
\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}}r^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-r^{2}/4}<\infty
, the prefactor
\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\exp(-\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}/4)
is bounded uniformly in
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
and
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
, and therefore there exists
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}^{\prime}>0
, independent of
t
and
y
, such that

\left|\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{ph}}^{\prime}\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

The polynomial
\rho_{x}
is bounded on the fixed compact set containing all
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, and
(A1)
gives
\lambda_{y(t)}\geq\mathfrak{L}
. Combining the preceding display with the curvature comparison gives

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi\,\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t),t)\right)-\frac{\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{y}(t))}{\lambda_{y(t)}\sqrt{2\pi}\,\Sigma(t)}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)^{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{G}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}}{\Sigma(t)}.

Finally, the triangle inequality combines (
145
), which bounds the difference from the exact saddle approximation by
\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{ex}}\Sigma(t)^{-2}
, with the Gaussian-coordinate comparison above, which bounds the replacement of that saddle approximation by
\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{G}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}/\Sigma(t)
. By Definition
1.12
,
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
, so
\Sigma(t)^{-2}\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{-1}
whenever
\Sigma(t)\geq 1
. Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc},x}
once more, we may assume
\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{ex}}\Sigma(t)^{-2}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{G}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}/\Sigma(t)
, because
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
by
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
. Summing the two displays with the enlarged constant
\mathrm{K}_{x}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{G}}+\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{ex}}
proves (
144
).
∎

3
The process associated with the stationary point

3.1
Large Deviations

This section supplies the probabilistic localization input needed to apply the deterministic endpoint-window expansion from Section
2.5
to random terminal states. The phase estimates from Section
2
, the stationary-point construction from Section
2.3
, the contour geometry from Section
2.4
, and the one-particle decoupling theorem from Section
2.5
are all conditional on endpoint control. The purpose here is to show that, under
(A1)
and
(A2)
, with probability one there is a finite random time after which the process remains in the finite-time endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
from Definition
2.2
. Consequently, the deterministic expansion may be applied to random endpoints with the complementary event contributing only a vanishing tail.

The argument has two levels. Proposition
3.1
provides the exponential observables from which the one-time estimate is derived. The quantitative one-time bound is then obtained in Proposition
3.2
while Proposition
3.3
then upgrades this one-time input to a path tail localization statement.

Proposition 3.1
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
t\in\mathscr{T}
, the following identity holds for every
w\in\mathbb{C}
in the continuous-time regime and for every
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{L}+\mathfrak{U}^{-1}}(\mathfrak{L})
in the discrete-time regime:

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\right]=\begin{cases}\rho_{x}(w)e^{-tw},&\textnormal{if }\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\rho_{x}(w)\displaystyle\prod_{n=0}^{t-1}\dfrac{(1-\alpha_{n}w)^{1-\tau_{n}}}{(1+\beta_{n}w)^{\tau_{n}}},&\textnormal{if }\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}.\end{cases}

(146)

In the discrete-time formula the powers are ordinary integer powers, since
\tau_{n}\in\{0,1\}
, and the empty product at
t=0
is equal to one.

Proof.

We prove the identity directly from the transition mechanisms in Definition
1.1
and Definition
1.2
. The only algebraic input needed throughout the proof is the one-step recursion following immediately from the definition of the characteristic polynomials in (
9
),

\rho_{y+1}(w)=\rho_{y}(w)\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{y}}\right),\qquad y\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(147)

We first consider the continuous-time regime. The generator
\mathsf{L}
of the pure-birth chain in Definition
1.1
acts on a test function
f
by
\mathsf{L}f(y)=\lambda_{y}(f(y+1)-f(y))
. Substituting
f(y)=\rho_{y}(w)
and using (
147
) gives the exact eigenfunction relation

(\mathsf{L}\rho_{\cdot}(w))(y)=\lambda_{y}\left(\rho_{y+1}(w)-\rho_{y}(w)\right)=-w\rho_{y}(w).

Since the function
y\mapsto\rho_{y}(w)
is not bounded for arbitrary complex
w
, we justify the preceding generator computation by the standard stopping argument. Let
N(t)
be the number of jumps made by the process up to time
t
. By the upper rate bound in
(A1)
,
N(t)
is stochastically dominated by a Poisson random variable with mean
\mathfrak{B}_{1}t
. On the event
\{N(t)=k\}
the process is at most at
x+k
, and the lower rate bound in
(A1)
together with (
147
) gives

|\rho_{x+k}(w)|\leq|\rho_{x}(w)|\left(1+\frac{|w|}{\mathfrak{L}}\right)^{k}.

The right-hand side has finite expectation under the dominating Poisson law for every fixed
t
and
w
. Therefore the stopped Dynkin formula for the process killed when it leaves
\{x,\ldots,x+m\}
may be passed to the limit
m\rightarrow\infty
by dominated convergence. Thus the function
h(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{x}[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)]
is finite and satisfies the integral form of the backward equation, equivalently

h^{\prime}(t)=-wh(t),\qquad h(0)=\rho_{x}(w).

Solving this scalar differential equation gives
h(t)=\rho_{x}(w)e^{-tw}
, proving (
146
) in the continuous-time regime. We now turn to the discrete-time regime. In the Bernoulli case
\tau_{t}=0
, conditioning on
\mathscr{X}(t)=y
and using (
147
) gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t+1)}(w)\mid\mathscr{X}(t)=y\right]=(1-\lambda_{y}\alpha_{t})\rho_{y}(w)+\lambda_{y}\alpha_{t}\rho_{y+1}(w)=(1-\alpha_{t}w)\rho_{y}(w).

This proves the one-step identity in the Bernoulli case. In the geometric case
\tau_{t}=1
, again condition on
\mathscr{X}(t)=y
. For
k\geq 0
, set

A_{k}(w)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\prod_{i=y}^{y+k-1}\frac{\beta_{t}(\lambda_{i}-w)}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{i}},

with the empty product
A_{0}(w)=1
. We first check that the tail of this product vanishes. Since
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{L}+\mathfrak{U}^{-1}}(\mathfrak{L})
, for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
the triangle inequality and the lower bound
\lambda_{i}\geq\mathfrak{L}
give

|\lambda_{i}-w|\leq|\lambda_{i}-\mathfrak{L}|+|w-\mathfrak{L}|<\lambda_{i}+\mathfrak{U}^{-1}.

By the upper bound
\beta_{t}<\mathfrak{U}
from
(A1)
, this implies
|\lambda_{i}-w|<\lambda_{i}+\beta_{t}^{-1}
, or equivalently

\left|\frac{\beta_{t}(\lambda_{i}-w)}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{i}}\right|<1.

The map
\lambda\mapsto|\beta_{t}(\lambda-w)|(1+\beta_{t}\lambda)^{-1}
is continuous on the compact interval
[\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{B}_{1}]
and is strictly smaller than one at every point of that interval. Hence there exists
\mathrm{q}(w,t)<1
such that

\left|\frac{\beta_{t}(\lambda_{i}-w)}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{i}}\right|\leq\mathrm{q}(w,t),\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

Consequently,
|A_{k}(w)|\leq\mathrm{q}(w,t)^{k}
and therefore
\lim_{k\to\infty}A_{k}(w)=0
. The reason for introducing
A_{k}(w)
is that it makes the geometric sum telescope. Indeed, for every
k\geq 0
,

A_{k}(w)-A_{k+1}(w)=A_{k}(w)\left(1-\frac{\beta_{t}(\lambda_{y+k}-w)}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{y+k}}\right)=A_{k}(w)\frac{1+\beta_{t}w}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{y+k}}.

Combining the geometric transition probabilities from Definition
1.2
with (
147
), we obtain, for every
m\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\mathbb{P}\left[\mathscr{X}(t+1)=y+k\mid\mathscr{X}(t)=y\right]\rho_{y+k}(w)

\displaystyle=\rho_{y}(w)\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{A_{k}(w)}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{y+k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{y}(w)}{1+\beta_{t}w}\left(1-A_{m+1}(w)\right).

Passing to the limit as
m
tends to infinity is justified by the bound
|A_{k}(w)|\leq\mathrm{q}(w,t)^{k}
, and gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t+1)}(w)\mid\mathscr{X}(t)=y\right]=\frac{\rho_{y}(w)}{1+\beta_{t}w}.

Thus, in both discrete one-step regimes,

\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t+1)}(w)\mid\mathscr{X}(t)\right]=\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\frac{(1-\alpha_{t}w)^{1-\tau_{t}}}{(1+\beta_{t}w)^{\tau_{t}}}.

Iterating this identity by the tower property, starting from
\mathscr{X}(0)=x
, gives the discrete-time part of (
146
). This completes the proof.
∎

For vector endpoints we use the coordinatewise endpoint window defined as follows,

\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}:m_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\textnormal{ for every }i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\right\}.

Proposition 3.2

(One-time tail localization)
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. Then there exist constants
\kappa,c>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there exists
\mathrm{M}_{x}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\left|\mathscr{X}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right|\geq\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-c\Sigma(t)},\qquad t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}.

(148)

In particular,

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-c\Sigma(t)},\qquad t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}.

(149)

Proof.

We prove the estimate by applying Markov’s inequality to the eigenfunction from Proposition
3.1
. The proof has four steps. First we choose the two deterministic boundary paths at distance
\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}
from
\mathscr{Z}(t)
and choose a deterministic lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
so that, for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
, the corresponding endpoints lie in the wider window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Second we turn the upper and lower tails into bounds involving the phase
\mathfrak{F}_{y}
. Third we use the new first-derivative estimate in
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
to prove that the two boundary phases have stationary points on the correct sides of the origin. Finally we evaluate the phase at those stationary points and obtain an exponentially small bound. Throughout the proof,
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
is increased finitely many times; the final value is the threshold appearing in the statement.

Step 1: The intrinsic boundary paths.

Let
\mathrm{T}_{1}
be the threshold from
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
, and let
\mathrm{C}_{1},\mathrm{C}_{2},\mathrm{T}_{3}
be the constants and threshold from
(iv)
of the same proposition. The sign-sensitive coefficient in the boundary estimates below is
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
, because the lower bound at the upper endpoint and the upper bound at the lower endpoint both use the
\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
branch of the first display in
(ii)
. Define

\mathrm{A}_{\star}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}2\mathrm{C}_{2}+1,\qquad\kappa\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}4\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{A}_{\star}.

The value
\kappa=4\mathfrak{B}_{1}\mathrm{A}_{\star}
is chosen so that the deterministic displacement of the endpoint dominates the centring error allowed in
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
; this is used explicitly in (
152
)–(
153
).

By Definition
1.12
,
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
and
\gamma(t)\downarrow 0
. Also,
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
gives

\mathrm{C}_{1}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{3}.

(150)

Consequently, since
\gamma(t)
can be made arbitrarily small and
\Sigma(t)
arbitrarily large after increasing the deterministic lower time, we may assume throughout the proof that

\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{Z}(t),\qquad\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq 1,\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}\leq\mathrm{A}_{\star}\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

(151)

The first inequality ensures that the lower boundary remains in
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, while the last two inequalities allow the integer-rounding errors and the additive constant
3\Lambda/2
in
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
to be absorbed into the intrinsic window.

For
t\in\mathscr{T}
, set

\mathrm{Z}_{+}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathscr{Z}(t)+\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2},\qquad\mathrm{Z}_{-}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathscr{Z}(t)-\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2},

and define the integer boundary paths

\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\lceil\mathrm{Z}_{+}(t)\right\rceil,\qquad\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\lfloor\mathrm{Z}_{-}(t)\right\rfloor.

For the estimates below we use these displayed values only after the final lower time; before that time we choose arbitrary non-negative integer values, so that
\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}\in\mathscr{P}
.
After increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
so that (
151
) holds for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
, the first inequality in (
151
) gives
\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\geq 0
. Moreover,

\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\geq\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2},\qquad\mathscr{Z}(t)-\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\geq\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2},

(152)

and, using
\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq 1
,

|\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)|\leq(\kappa+1)\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

(153)

We include in the final choice of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
the deterministic condition
(\kappa+1)\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1
for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
, which is possible because
\gamma(t)\to 0
. Then (
153
) gives

|\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)|\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2},

so both endpoints
\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)
and
\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)
belong to
\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Thus the two boundary endpoints satisfy exactly the finite-time hypothesis of Proposition
2.4
. No separate qualitative path argument is needed at this point: the stationary-point construction in that proposition is now uniform over all endpoints in
\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

Step 2: Markov bounds from the eigenfunction.

Since
\mathscr{X}(t)
and
\mathscr{Z}(t)
are integer-valued, the event in the statement is contained in the union of the two one-sided events

\left\{\left|\mathscr{X}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right|\geq\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\mathscr{X}(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)\right\}\cup\left\{\mathscr{X}(t)\leq\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\right\}.

(154)

This inclusion is all that is needed. We now bound each event by choosing the sign of the spectral parameter so that
m\mapsto\rho_{m}(w)
is monotone in the correct direction. For the spectral parameters below set

\mathcal{D}_{-}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}(-\infty,0),&\textnormal{in continuous time},\\
(-\mathfrak{U}^{-1},0),&\textnormal{in discrete time},\end{cases}\qquad\mathcal{D}_{+}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(0,\mathfrak{L}).

Thus both choices lie in the spectral domain of Proposition
3.1
.

Recall from (
9
) that

\rho_{m+1}(w)=\rho_{m}(w)\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{m}}\right).

If
w\in\mathcal{D}_{-}
, then every factor
1-w/\lambda_{m}
is at least one, so
m\mapsto\rho_{m}(w)
is increasing and positive. Therefore

\left\{\mathscr{X}(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)\right\}=\left\{\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\geq\rho_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)}(w)\right\},\qquad w\in\mathcal{D}_{-}.

If
w\in\mathcal{D}_{+}
, then
(A1)
gives
0<1-w/\lambda_{m}\leq 1
for every
m
, so
m\mapsto\rho_{m}(w)
is decreasing and positive. Hence

\left\{\mathscr{X}(t)\leq\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\right\}=\left\{\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\geq\rho_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)}(w)\right\},\qquad w\in\mathcal{D}_{+}.

Markov’s inequality then gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)}(w)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\right],

\displaystyle w\in\mathcal{D}_{-},

(155)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)\leq\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)}(w)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\right],

\displaystyle w\in\mathcal{D}_{+}.

(156)

Using Proposition
3.1
and the branch-free product form of
\exp(\mathfrak{F}_{y})
from (
56
), the right-hand side can be rewritten exactly. The only small point is that
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)
contains the spatial product up to
y(t)
, whereas
\rho_{y(t)}(w)
contains the product only up to
y(t)-1
. This accounts for the remaining factor:

\rho_{y(t)}(w)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w)\right]=\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{y(t)}}\right)\rho_{x}(w)\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{y}(w,t)\right).

(157)

Taking
y=\mathscr{Z}_{+}
in (
157
) for the upper tail and
y=\mathscr{Z}_{-}
for the lower tail, (
155
)–(
156
) become

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)}}\right)\rho_{x}(w)\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}}(w,t)\right),

\displaystyle w\in\mathcal{D}_{-},

(158)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)\leq\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{w}{\lambda_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)}}\right)\rho_{x}(w)\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}}(w,t)\right),

\displaystyle w\in\mathcal{D}_{+}.

(159)

Step 3: Sign and size of the first derivative at the two boundaries.

The upper boundary satisfies
\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)\geq\mathscr{Z}(t)
, so
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
uses
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{l}}(t)=\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
in the lower bound. Combining that lower bound with (
152
), (
150
), and (
151
), we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}}^{(1)}(0,t)

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\left(\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right)-2\gamma(t)\mathscr{Z}(t)-\frac{3\Lambda}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\kappa-2\mathrm{C}_{2}\right)\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}-\frac{3\Lambda}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{A}_{\star}\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

(160)

For the lower boundary,
\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)<\mathscr{Z}(t)
, and the upper bound in
(ii)
uses
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{u}}(t)=\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}
. Combining this upper bound with the lower-boundary inequality in (
152
), the upper scale comparison in (
150
), and the last inequality in (
151
) gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}}^{(1)}(0,t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\left(\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right)+2\gamma(t)\mathscr{Z}(t)+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}^{-1}\kappa-2\mathrm{C}_{2}\right)\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}+\frac{3\Lambda}{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\mathrm{A}_{\star}\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

(161)

This is where the sign information in
(ii)
enters the proof: the phase at the upper endpoint has positive first derivative at the origin, while the phase at the lower endpoint has negative first derivative at the origin.

Because
\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t),\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
, the uniform endpoint version of the strengthened second-derivative estimate in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
applies to both phases. We add the condition
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1/2
to the final choice of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
. Then, for all
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
,

\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(0,t)\leq\frac{3}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}.

(162)

Indeed, applying
(iv)
at
w=0
gives

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(0,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}.

The constants are uniform over the two boundary endpoints because both endpoints lie in
\mathscr{C}_{t}
.

Step 4: Evaluation at the stationary points.

For readability, write

\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{u}_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}}(t),\qquad\mathrm{u}_{-}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{u}_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}}(t).

Since the two boundary endpoints belong to
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, Proposition
2.4
supplies real stationary points
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
after the deterministic lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
. Increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
to dominate
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
, these stationary points are defined for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
and obey

\left|\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)+\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\!\left(\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right)^{\!2}

by (
78
). Write

a_{\pm}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(0,t)}.

By (
160
), (
161
), and (
162
),
a_{+}(t)>0
and
a_{-}(t)<0
for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
. The complementary inequalities in
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
, together with (
153
) and (
150
), also give a deterministic constant
\mathrm{D}_{\partial}>0
such that

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|\leq\mathrm{D}_{\partial}\left(\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}+1\right)

for both signs. Hence (
162
) gives
|a_{\pm}(t)|\leq\mathrm{D}_{a}(\gamma(t)+\Sigma(t)^{-2})
for a deterministic constant
\mathrm{D}_{a}>0
, and so
a_{\pm}(t)\to 0
. We add to the final choice of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
the two conditions
2\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}|a_{\pm}(t)|<1
and
|a_{\pm}(t)|<\mathrm{R}_{-}/3
for both signs. Then the explicit bound gives
|\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)+a_{\pm}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}a_{\pm}(t)^{2}<|a_{\pm}(t)|/2
, so
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
and
-a_{\pm}(t)
have the same sign. The same inequality also gives
|\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)|\leq|a_{\pm}(t)|+\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}a_{\pm}(t)^{2}<\mathrm{R}_{-}/2
. Therefore

\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)<0<\mathrm{u}_{-}(t),\qquad|\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)|,|\mathrm{u}_{-}(t)|<\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{R}_{-},

(163)

where
\mathrm{R}_{-}
is the holomorphic radius from
(i)
of Proposition
2.3
. Since
\mathrm{R}_{-}\leq\mathfrak{U}^{-1}
and
\mathrm{R}_{-}\leq\mathfrak{L}
, (
163
) puts
\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)
in
\mathcal{D}_{-}
and
\mathrm{u}_{-}(t)
in
\mathcal{D}_{+}
. Substituting these choices into (
158
)–(
159
) and using (
154
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\left|\mathscr{X}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right|\geq\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}\right]

(164)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)}{\lambda_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)}}\right)\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{+}(t))\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}}(\mathrm{u}_{+}(t),t)\right)

\displaystyle+\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{u}_{-}(t)}{\lambda_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)}}\right)\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{-}(t))\exp\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}}(\mathrm{u}_{-}(t),t)\right).

We now estimate the two exponential terms. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}(0,t)=0
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t),t)=0
, Taylor’s theorem on the real segment between
0
and
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
gives

0=\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}(\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t),t)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(\mathrm{v}_{\pm}(t),t)\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)^{2},

(165)

for some real
\mathrm{v}_{\pm}(t)
between
0
and
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
. By (
163
), the whole segment lies in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}/2}(0)
. Applying the strengthened second-derivative estimate in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
along this segment gives

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(w,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|w|\right)\Sigma(t)^{2}

for every real
w
between
0
and
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
. We add the deterministic condition
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)|)\leq 1/2
for both signs to the final choice of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
, which is possible because
\gamma(t)\to 0
and
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)\to 0
. Therefore, for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
,

\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(w,t)\leq 2\Sigma(t)^{2}

(166)

for every real
w
between
0
and
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
.

The same segment estimate applied to the derivative gives a lower bound on the distance of the stationary point from the origin. Since

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t),t)-\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(1)}(0,t)=\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}^{(2)}(\widetilde{\mathrm{v}}_{\pm}(t),t)\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)

for some
\widetilde{\mathrm{v}}_{\pm}(t)
between
0
and
\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)
, (
160
), (
161
), and (
166
) imply

|\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t)|\geq\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\star}}{2}\gamma(t).

(167)

Substituting (
166
) and (
167
) into (
165
), we obtain

\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{Z}_{\pm}}(\mathrm{u}_{\pm}(t),t)\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\star}^{2}}{16}\gamma(t)^{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\star}^{2}}{16}\Sigma(t),

(168)

where the final inequality uses
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
, again from Definition
1.12
.

It remains only to control the non-exponential factors in (
164
). The bound (
163
) places
\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)
and
\mathrm{u}_{-}(t)
in a fixed compact subset of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{-}}(0)
. Since
x
is fixed,
\rho_{x}
is a fixed polynomial, and
(A1)
gives
\lambda_{n}\geq\mathfrak{L}
, there exists
\mathrm{M}_{x}>0
such that

\left|1-\frac{\mathrm{u}_{+}(t)}{\lambda_{\mathscr{Z}_{+}(t)}}\right||\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{+}(t))|+\left|1-\frac{\mathrm{u}_{-}(t)}{\lambda_{\mathscr{Z}_{-}(t)}}\right||\rho_{x}(\mathrm{u}_{-}(t))|\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}

(169)

for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
. Combining (
164
), (
168
), and (
169
) proves (
148
), with
c=\mathrm{A}_{\star}^{2}/16
and with
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
chosen to dominate all thresholds introduced above. We now increase
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
once more, if necessary, so that
\kappa\gamma(t)\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}
, which is possible because
\gamma(t)\to 0
. If
\mathscr{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}
, then by the definition of the endpoint window
\left|\mathscr{X}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right|>\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
, and hence
\left|\mathscr{X}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)\right|\geq\kappa\gamma(t)\Sigma(t)^{2}
. Thus (
149
) follows directly from (
148
), which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.3

(Almost-sure eventual endpoint-window localization)
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. For every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\exists\,T_{0}\in\mathscr{T}\textnormal{ such that }\mathscr{X}(s)\in\mathscr{C}_{s}\textnormal{ for every }s\geq T_{0}\right]=1.

Proof.

Proposition
3.2
gives an exponentially small estimate at one fixed time, whereas the event of interest concerns the whole future tail of the path. In discrete time the upgrade is a direct summation over the future time indices. In continuous time there is one extra issue: a path could, in principle, cross the endpoint window between two deterministic sampling times. We therefore introduce a fine deterministic mesh and separately bound the probability that the birth process makes too many jumps inside one mesh interval.

Step 1: A fixed intrinsic window.

Let
\kappa,c>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}\in\mathscr{T}
be the constants and lower time supplied by Proposition
3.2
. Fix

\mathrm{K}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}8\kappa.

For
u\in\mathscr{T}
, introduce the two one-time events

\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(u)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\left|\mathscr{X}(u)-\mathscr{Z}(u)\right|\geq\mathrm{K}\gamma(u)\Sigma(u)^{2}\right\},\qquad\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(u)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\left|\mathscr{X}(u)-\mathscr{Z}(u)\right|\geq\kappa\gamma(u)\Sigma(u)^{2}\right\}.

Since
\mathrm{K}>\kappa
, the inclusion
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(u)\subseteq\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(u)
holds for every
u
. Hence, for each fixed starting point
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, Proposition
3.2
gives a constant
\mathrm{M}_{x}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(u)\right]\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(u)\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-c\Sigma(u)},\qquad u\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}.

(170)

Since
\gamma(u)\to 0
, we may increase
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}
so that
\mathrm{K}\gamma(u)^{1/2}\leq 1
for all
u\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}
. For such
u
, the inclusion

\left\{\mathscr{X}(u)\notin\mathscr{C}_{u}\right\}\subseteq\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(u)

(171)

holds, because leaving
\mathscr{C}_{u}
means that
|\mathscr{X}(u)-\mathscr{Z}(u)|>\gamma(u)^{1/2}\Sigma(u)^{2}
, and the latter threshold is at least
\mathrm{K}\gamma(u)\Sigma(u)^{2}
.
We shall repeatedly use a deterministic lower growth bound for
\Sigma
. Set

\mathrm{a}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\min(1,\mathfrak{U}^{-1},\mathfrak{L})}{\Lambda}.

In continuous time, Definition
1.11
gives
\mathrm{z}(u)=u/\Lambda
. In discrete time, the same definition gives
\mathrm{z}(u)=\Lambda^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{u-1}(\alpha_{r}(1-\tau_{r})+\beta_{r}\tau_{r})
, and the lower bounds on
\alpha_{r}
and
\beta_{r}
in
(A1)
imply
\mathrm{z}(u)\geq\min(\mathfrak{U}^{-1},\mathfrak{L})u/\Lambda
. Therefore, in both regimes,
\mathrm{z}(u)\geq\mathrm{a}_{0}u
. Since
\mathscr{Z}(u)=\lfloor\mathrm{z}(u)\rceil
differs from
\mathrm{z}(u)
by at most
1/2
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{z}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that

\mathscr{Z}(u)\geq\frac{\mathrm{a}_{0}}{2}u,\qquad u>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{z}}.

(172)

Combining this with the upper comparison
\mathscr{Z}(u)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(u)^{2}
from
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, we obtain

\Sigma(u)\geq\mathrm{c}_{0}u^{1/2},\qquad u>\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{z}},\mathrm{T}_{3}),

(173)

where
\mathrm{c}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(\mathrm{a}_{0}/(2\mathrm{C}_{2}))^{1/2}
and
\mathrm{T}_{3}
is the lower time in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
.

Step 2: The discrete-time upgrade.

Assume first that
\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. For
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, let

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\bigcup_{s=t}^{\infty}\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(s).

The event
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)
says that the path exits the
\mathrm{K}\gamma\Sigma^{2}
window at least once after time
t
. By (
171
), the event that
\mathscr{X}(s)
leaves
\mathscr{C}_{s}
for some
s\geq t
is contained in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)
. By Boole’s inequality, (
170
), and (
173
), for every
t>\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{z}},\mathrm{T}_{3})
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right]\leq\sum_{s=t}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(s)\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\sum_{s=t}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c\Sigma(s)}\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\sum_{s=t}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathrm{c}_{0}s^{1/2}}.

The series
\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathrm{c}_{0}s^{1/2}}
converges by comparison with an integral of the same stretched-exponential form, and hence the tail displayed above tends to zero as
t\to\infty
. Since the events
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)
decrease as
t
increases, continuity of probability from above gives

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\bigcap_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right]=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right]=0.

Thus the probability that the path leaves
\mathscr{C}_{s}
at some discrete time
s\geq t
tends to zero as
t\to\infty
. The almost-sure statement of the proposition then follows in the discrete-time regime from continuity of probability from above applied to the decreasing events
\{\exists\,s\geq t:\mathscr{X}(s)\notin\mathscr{C}_{s}\}
.

Step 3: The continuous-time mesh.

It remains to prove the same statement when
\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}
. For
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
define

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\exists\,s\geq t\text{ such that }\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(s)\text{ occurs}\right\}.

Fix
\sigma\in(0,1)
, set
r_{0}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}0
, and define

r_{n+1}-r_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{B}_{1}(n+1)^{\sigma}},\qquad n\geq 0.

Because
\sigma<1
, the sequence
r_{n}
tends to infinity, and the intervals

\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}[t+r_{n},t+r_{n+1}],\qquad q_{n}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}t+r_{n+1},

cover the whole half-line
[t,\infty)
. We also set

\mathrm{L}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\lceil\Sigma(t)\rceil,\qquad\mathcal{N}_{n}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)}\left|\mathscr{X}(s_{2})-\mathscr{X}(s_{1})\right|<\mathrm{L}(t)\right\}.

The event
\mathcal{N}_{n}(t)
is the good-oscillation event on the interval
\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
. We next choose a deterministic lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{mesh}}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{mesh}}
, a violation of the
\mathrm{K}
-window somewhere inside
\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
either forces a violation of the
\kappa
-window at the right endpoint
q_{n}(t)
or else the good-oscillation event fails.

The deterministic centre moves by a bounded amount over every mesh interval. Indeed, in continuous time
\mathrm{z}(u)=u/\Lambda
, and therefore, for any
s\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
,

\left|\mathscr{Z}(q_{n}(t))-\mathscr{Z}(s)\right|\leq\left|\mathrm{z}(q_{n}(t))-\mathrm{z}(s)\right|+1\leq\frac{1}{\Lambda\mathfrak{B}_{1}}+1\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{Z}}.

(174)

Here the additional
1
accounts for the two nearest-integer rounding errors in
\mathscr{Z}=\lfloor\mathrm{z}\rceil
. Since, in continuous time,
\Sigma(u)^{2}=\sum_{r=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(u)}\lambda_{r}^{-2}
, (
174
) and the lower spatial-rate bound in
(A1)
imply

0\leq\Sigma(q_{n}(t))^{2}-\Sigma(s)^{2}\leq\left(\lceil\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{Z}}\rceil+1\right)\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{D}_{\Sigma}.

(175)

The left inequality uses the monotonicity of
\mathscr{Z}
, and the right inequality uses the fact that at most
\lceil\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{Z}}\rceil+1
additional summands can be inserted between
\mathscr{Z}(s)
and
\mathscr{Z}(q_{n}(t))
. Since
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
by (
173
), there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that, whenever
t>\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma}
,
n\geq 0
, and
s\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
,

\Sigma(s)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(q_{n}(t))^{2}.

(176)

Moreover,
\gamma
is non-increasing by Definition
1.12
, because it is the maximum of two tail suprema over
[t,\infty)
. Hence, for
s\leq q_{n}(t)
, (
176
) gives

\mathrm{K}\gamma(s)\Sigma(s)^{2}\geq 4\kappa\gamma(q_{n}(t))\Sigma(q_{n}(t))^{2}.

(177)

We shall also use that the deterministic and path oscillation errors are smaller than one copy of the
\kappa
-window. By Definition
1.12
,
\gamma(u)\geq\Sigma(u)^{-1/2}
; since
\Sigma(q_{n}(t))\geq\Sigma(t)
in continuous time, increasing
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{mesh}}
if necessary gives, uniformly in
n
and for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{mesh}}
,

\mathrm{L}(t)+\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{Z}}\leq\kappa\gamma(q_{n}(t))\Sigma(q_{n}(t))^{2}.

(178)

Now suppose that
\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{K}}(s)
occurs for some
s\in\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
and that
\mathcal{N}_{n}(t)
also occurs. Combining the triangle inequality with (
174
), (
177
), and (
178
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{X}(q_{n}(t))-\mathscr{Z}(q_{n}(t))\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\mathscr{X}(s)-\mathscr{Z}(s)\right|-\left|\mathscr{X}(q_{n}(t))-\mathscr{X}(s)\right|-\left|\mathscr{Z}(q_{n}(t))-\mathscr{Z}(s)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{K}\gamma(s)\Sigma(s)^{2}-\mathrm{L}(t)-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{Z}}

\displaystyle\geq 3\kappa\gamma(q_{n}(t))\Sigma(q_{n}(t))^{2}.

In particular,
\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(q_{n}(t))
occurs. Thus, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{mesh}}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\subseteq\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(q_{n}(t))\cup\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}_{n}(t)^{c}.

(179)

Step 4: Summing the continuous-time bounds.

We estimate the two terms in (
179
). First, by (
170
),

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(q_{n}(t))\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-c\Sigma(q_{n}(t))}

(180)

for every
t\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}
, since
q_{n}(t)\geq t
for every
n\geq 0
. For each fixed
n
, the summand in (
180
) tends to zero as
t\to\infty
, because
q_{n}(t)\to\infty
and
\Sigma(u)\to\infty
by (
173
). To justify passing the limit through the infinite sum, note that (
173
) gives

\mathrm{e}^{-c\Sigma(q_{n}(t))}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-c\mathrm{c}_{0}r_{n+1}^{1/2}}

for every
t
beyond the lower scale threshold in (
173
), since
q_{n}(t)=t+r_{n+1}\geq r_{n+1}
. Also,

r_{n+1}=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}j^{-\sigma}\geq\frac{(n+2)^{1-\sigma}-1}{\mathfrak{B}_{1}(1-\sigma)},

by comparison of the sum with the integral of
u^{-\sigma}
. Hence
\sum_{n\geq 0}\exp\{-c\mathrm{c}_{0}r_{n+1}^{1/2}\}<\infty
, since the displayed lower bound implies a stretched-exponential bound in
n
after a finite index depending only on
\sigma
and
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
. The dominated convergence theorem for series therefore gives

\lim_{t\to\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathfrak{I}_{\kappa}(q_{n}(t))\right]=0.

(181)

It remains to bound the bad-oscillation term. On
\mathcal{N}_{n}(t)^{c}
, the non-decreasing birth process makes at least
\mathrm{L}(t)
jumps during
\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
. By the upper rate bound
\lambda_{r}<\mathfrak{B}_{1}
in
(A1)
, the jump count on
\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
is stochastically dominated by a Poisson random variable with rate
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
. One way to see this is to construct a rate-
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
Poisson clock and accept a proposed jump from site
r
with probability
\lambda_{r}/\mathfrak{B}_{1}
; the accepted process has the required birth rates and is bounded above by the proposing Poisson process. Thus the number of jumps in
\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)
is dominated by a Poisson random variable with mean

\mu_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{B}_{1}|\mathcal{J}_{n}(t)|=(n+1)^{-\sigma}.

Since
\mu_{n}\leq 1
, the Poisson tail is bounded by

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathrm{Poisson}(\mu_{n})\geq\mathrm{L}(t)\right]=\mathrm{e}^{-\mu_{n}}\sum_{\ell=\mathrm{L}(t)}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{n}^{\ell}}{\ell!}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{L}(t)+j}}{\mathrm{L}(t)!j!}=\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{n}}\frac{\mu_{n}^{\mathrm{L}(t)}}{\mathrm{L}(t)!}\leq\mathrm{e}\,\frac{(n+1)^{-\sigma\mathrm{L}(t)}}{\mathrm{L}(t)!}.

Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{osc}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\sigma\mathrm{L}(t)>1
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{osc}}
. For such
t
, summing this estimate over
n
gives

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathcal{N}_{n}(t)^{c}\right]\leq\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathrm{L}(t)!}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)^{-\sigma\mathrm{L}(t)}=\frac{\mathrm{e}\,\zeta(\sigma\mathrm{L}(t))}{\mathrm{L}(t)!},

(182)

and the right-hand side tends to zero as
t\to\infty
, because
\mathrm{L}(t)=\lceil\Sigma(t)\rceil\to\infty
while
\zeta(\sigma\mathrm{L}(t))\to 1
. Combining (
179
), (
181
), and (
182
), we obtain

\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right]=0.

The events
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)
decrease as
t
increases through the integers, so continuity from above gives

\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\bigcap_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right]=0.

Consequently, with probability one, there is a random finite time after which

\left|\mathscr{X}(s)-\mathscr{Z}(s)\right|<\mathrm{K}\gamma(s)\Sigma(s)^{2}

for every
s
in the continuous time set. Since
\gamma(s)\to 0
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{K}\gamma(s)^{1/2}\leq 1
for every
s>\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C}}
; hence
\mathrm{K}\gamma(s)\Sigma(s)^{2}\leq\gamma(s)^{1/2}\Sigma(s)^{2}
for every
s>\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C}}
. Therefore
\mathscr{X}(s)\in\mathscr{C}_{s}
after the maximum of the random finite time above and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C}}
, which proves the proposition in the continuous-time regime.
∎

Figure 13
:
Schematic illustration of Proposition
3.3
. With probability one, there is a finite random time
T_{0}
after which the one-particle path remains in the endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
around the deterministic centre
\mathscr{Z}(t)
. The blue staircase boundaries reflect the integer-valued endpoint window in the discrete spatial coordinate.

3.2
Martingale control

This subsection provides the moment input that is used to lift the one-particle estimates of Section
2.5
to the multi-particle determinant analysis. The remaining content of Section
3
has three components: martingale/moment control for the stationary-point coordinates (Proposition
3.4
), the Gaussian limit for the stationary-point process (Proposition
3.5
), and the determinant expansion of the Karlin–McGregor semigroup (Proposition
3.10
). Throughout, the endpoint restriction is encoded by the coordinatewise window
\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
introduced before Proposition
3.2
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Assume
(A1)
and fix
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
. For every
p\geq 2
and every
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, there exists
\mathrm{C}_{p,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\left|\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\right|^{p}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,\mathbf{x}}(1+t)^{p/2},\qquad t\in\mathscr{T}.

(183)

If in addition
(A2)
holds, then for every
p\geq 2
there exist
\mathrm{T}_{p,N}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{p,N,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that

\sup_{t\geq\mathrm{T}_{p,N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[|\mathbf{U}(t)|^{p}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,N,\mathbf{x}}.

(184)

Proof.

The proof has two parts. First we prove a moment bound for the centered inverse-rate coordinate of one particle. This is the probabilistic input behind (
183
). Second, under
(A2)
, we use the endpoint-window stationary-point estimates to transfer that moment control to
\mathbf{U}(t)
on
\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
.

Step 1: The inverse-rate martingale.
Set

H(m)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{m-1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}},\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

We first prove the required moment estimate for one coordinate process
X
started from a fixed point
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. In continuous time, the generator from Definition
1.1
is
\mathsf{L}f(m)=\lambda_{m}(f(m+1)-f(m))
. Since
H
is unbounded, we localize it: for
K\in\mathbb{N}
with
K>x
let
H_{K}(m)=H(m\wedge K)
and
\tau_{K}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X(t)\geq K\}
. Dynkin’s formula for the bounded test function
H_{K}

[
53
, Chapter 4]
gives a martingale, and before
\tau_{K}
the generator satisfies

(\mathsf{L}H)(m)=\lambda_{m}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{m}}\right)=1.

Hence
H(X(t\wedge\tau_{K}))-H(X(0))-(t\wedge\tau_{K})
is a martingale. The upper rate bound in
(A1)
gives
\lambda_{m}<\mathfrak{B}_{1}
for every state
m
, so the process can be constructed by thinning a rate-
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
Poisson clock: when the dominating clock rings at state
m
, accept the birth with probability
\lambda_{m}/\mathfrak{B}_{1}
. This construction gives non-explosion and
\tau_{K}\uparrow\infty
almost surely. For each fixed deterministic time,
H(X(t\wedge\tau_{K}))\leq H(x)+\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}(t)
, where
\Pi_{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}(t)
is the number of rings of the dominating Poisson clock up to time
t
. Thus the stopped martingale variables are dominated in absolute value by
2H(x)+\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\Pi_{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}(t)+t
. Hence the stopped martingales are uniformly integrable on each finite time interval, and letting
K\rightarrow\infty
gives that
H(X(t))-H(x)-t
is a true martingale. Its jumps have size at most
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
, and its predictable quadratic variation is

\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{X(s)}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{X(s)}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\lambda_{X(s)}}\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{t}{\mathfrak{L}}.

For the moment estimate we use the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality for càdlàg martingales in
[
88
, Chapter IV, Theorem 48]
. Applied at a fixed time, this theorem controls the martingale by its optional quadratic variation. In the present pure-birth case,

[H(X(\cdot))-\cdot,H(X(\cdot))-\cdot]_{t}=\sum_{0<s\leq t}\left(H(X(s))-H(X(s-))\right)^{2}.

Each summand is bounded by
\mathfrak{L}^{-2}
, and the number of jumps up to time
t
is bounded by the same rate-
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
Poisson clock used above. Therefore the optional quadratic variation is bounded by
\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\Pi_{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}(t)
under this coupling. Since
\mathbb{E}[\Pi_{\mathfrak{B}_{1}}(t)^{p/2}]\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,\mathfrak{B}_{1}}(1+t)^{p/2}
for every
p\geq 2
, the càdlàg BDG inequality applied to
H(X(t))-H(x)-t
gives the required
p
th moment bound for the martingale part. Absorbing the fixed term
H(x)
by the triangle inequality, there exists
\mathrm{C}_{p,x}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\left|H(X(t))-t\right|^{p}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,x}(1+t)^{p/2},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(185)

In discrete time, let
X
be one coordinate process from Definition
1.2
, with natural filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
. If
\tau_{t}=0
, then the Bernoulli jump probability is
\alpha_{t}\lambda_{X(t)}
, and therefore

\mathbb{E}\!\left[H(X(t+1))-H(X(t))\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\alpha_{t}.

If
\tau_{t}=1
and
X(t)=m
, then the tail-sum formula for the non-negative increment
H(X(t+1))-H(X(t))
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[H(X(t+1))-H(X(t))\mid X(t)=m\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\prod_{r=m}^{n}\frac{\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{t}\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{r=m}^{n-1}\frac{\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}-\prod_{r=m}^{n}\frac{\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}\right).

The product at the upper endpoint tends to zero because
(A1)
gives

\frac{\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}{1+\beta_{t}\lambda_{r}}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{U}\mathfrak{B}_{1}}{1+\mathfrak{U}\mathfrak{B}_{1}}<1.

Thus the telescoping sum equals
\beta_{t}
, and the process

H(X(t))-\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{s})\alpha_{s}+\tau_{s}\beta_{s}\big)

is a martingale. Its one-step martingale differences have uniformly bounded conditional
p
th moments. Indeed, a Bernoulli update makes at most one spatial jump, while in a geometric update the probability of at least
r
jumps is bounded by
q_{\mathrm{geo}}^{r}
with
q_{\mathrm{geo}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{U}\mathfrak{B}_{1}/(1+\mathfrak{U}\mathfrak{B}_{1})<1
; multiplying by the factor
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
converting spatial jumps into
H
-increments gives a finite bound for the conditional
p
th moment of
H(X(t+1))-H(X(t))
, depending only on
p
and the constants in
(A1)
. The deterministic centring term subtracted in the martingale difference is either
\alpha_{t}
or
\beta_{t}
, both bounded above by constants from
(A1)
, so the martingale differences themselves have conditional
p
th moments bounded by a deterministic constant
\mathrm{D}_{p}
. Let

\Delta_{s}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}H(X(s+1))-H(X(s))-\big((1-\tau_{s})\alpha_{s}+\tau_{s}\beta_{s}\big).

Burkholder’s discrete martingale inequality
[
60
, Chapter 2, Theorem 2.10]
gives

\mathbb{E}\left|\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\Delta_{s}\right|^{p}\leq\mathrm{C}_{p}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\Delta_{s}^{2}\right)^{p/2}.

For
t\geq 1
, we use
(\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}a_{s}^{2})^{p/2}\leq t^{p/2-1}\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}|a_{s}|^{p}
, while
t=0
is absorbed into the fixed initial value. The uniform conditional moment bound on the
\Delta_{s}
therefore gives a bound of order
(1+t)^{p/2}
for the centred martingale. Absorbing
H(x)
by the triangle inequality gives a constant
\mathrm{C}_{p,x}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}_{x}\!\left[\left|H(X(t))-\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{s})\alpha_{s}+\tau_{s}\beta_{s}\big)\right|^{p}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,x}(1+t)^{p/2},\qquad t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

(186)

Combining (
185
) and (
186
), and applying the resulting one-coordinate estimate to
X=\mathscr{X}_{i}
, we get, in both time regimes,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\left|H(\mathscr{X}_{i}(t))-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{s})\alpha_{s}+\tau_{s}\beta_{s}\big)\right|^{p}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,\mathbf{x}}(1+t)^{p/2}.

(187)

By the explicit formula for the first derivative of the phase in Definition
1.17
,

\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)=H(\mathscr{X}_{i}(t))-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{s})\alpha_{s}+\tau_{s}\beta_{s}\big)+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)}}.

The final term is bounded by
\mathfrak{L}^{-1}
under
(A1)
. Therefore (
187
) implies (
183
), after increasing
\mathrm{C}_{p,\mathbf{x}}
.

Step 2: Moment bounds for the stationary-point coordinates.

Assume now
(A2)
. On the endpoint event
\{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
, the stationary-point construction in Proposition
2.4
is uniform in the terminal value. Let
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{U}}\in\mathscr{T}
be chosen to dominate
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
and the threshold in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
at which
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1/2
. Then the endpoint second-derivative comparison gives
\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{2}/2
whenever
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{U}}
and
\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. The same comparison also gives
\Sigma(t)^{2}/\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\leq 1+2\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}
, because
(1-r)^{-1}\leq 1+2r
for
0\leq r\leq 1/2
. Multiplying (
78
) by
\Sigma(t)
and using these two consequences of the second-derivative comparison gives

|\mathscr{U}_{i}(t)|\leq\frac{\left|\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\right|}{\Sigma(t)}+\frac{4\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\left|\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\right|^{2}}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}+\frac{2\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\left|\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\right|}{\Sigma(t)}

(188)

on this event. This deterministic estimate transfers the martingale bound to the stationary-point coordinate. We next compare
\Sigma(t)^{2}
with time. The deterministic centre estimate (
61
), proved from Definition
1.11
and
(A1)
, gives a threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{Z}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathscr{Z}(t)\geq\mathfrak{A}t/2
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{Z}}
. Combining this with the upper comparison
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
from
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, there exist constants
\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma}\in\mathscr{T}
such that

\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}(1+t),\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma}.

Increase the lower time once more so that
\gamma(t)\leq 1
. Taking the
p
th power in (
188
), using
(a+b+c)^{p}\leq 3^{p-1}(a^{p}+b^{p}+c^{p})
, and applying (
183
) with exponents
p
and
2p
, gives

\sup_{t\geq\mathrm{T}_{p}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[|\mathscr{U}_{i}(t)|^{p}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}}\right]<\infty

for a deterministic threshold
\mathrm{T}_{p}\in\mathscr{T}
. Indeed, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{p}
, the first and third terms in (
188
) are controlled by

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\left|\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\right|^{p}\right]}{\Sigma(t)^{p}}\leq\mathrm{C}_{p,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{-p/2},

where we used
\gamma(t)\leq 1
for the third term. The quadratic term is controlled by

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\left|\mathfrak{F}^{(1)}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(0,t)\right|^{2p}\right]}{\Sigma(t)^{3p}}\leq\mathrm{C}_{2p,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{-3p/2}(1+t)^{-p/2}\leq\mathrm{C}_{2p,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{-3p/2}.

These two estimates use (
183
) and the lower bound
\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}(1+t)
, and therefore give a bound independent of
t
. Finally,

|\mathbf{U}(t)|^{p}\leq N^{p-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\mathscr{U}_{i}(t)|^{p},

and the event
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}
is contained in each coordinate event
\{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
. Summing the one-coordinate bounds over
i
proves (
184
), after choosing
\mathrm{C}_{p,N,\mathbf{x}}
larger than the resulting finite sum.
∎

Proposition 3.5

(Quantitative Gaussian limit on the endpoint window)
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. Fix
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
. Let
H:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}
be continuous and suppose that there are constants
\mathrm{C}_{H}>0
and
q\geq 0
such that

|H(\mathbf{v})|\leq\mathrm{C}_{H}(1+|\mathbf{v}|^{q}),\qquad|H(\mathbf{v})-H(\mathbf{w})|\leq\mathrm{C}_{H}(1+\mathrm{R}^{q})|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}|,

whenever
\max(|\mathbf{v}|,|\mathbf{w}|)\leq\mathrm{R}
and
\mathrm{R}\geq 1
; the same constant
\mathrm{C}_{H}
may be used in both bounds after enlarging it if necessary. There exist
\mathrm{T}_{H,\mathbf{x}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{H,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that, with

\mathrm{R}_{H,\mathbf{x}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{C}_{H,\mathbf{x}}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4},

(189)

for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{H,\mathbf{x}}
(in particular,
\gamma(t)<1
throughout),

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[H\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}H(\mathbf{v})\,\exp\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{v}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\right|\leq\mathrm{R}_{H,\mathbf{x}}(t).

(190)

Proof.

The strategy is to reduce the multivariate test-function bound to a one-particle characteristic-function estimate on a compact frequency window, identify the relevant exponent exactly on the endpoint event
\mathcal{A}_{t}=\{\mathscr{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
, control the residual error in
L^{2}
via the moment input from Proposition
3.4
, and finally pass from the characteristic-function to the test-function by first cutting off the polynomially growing observable and then applying a compact-frequency smoothing kernel with finite first moment.

Step 1: Reduction to a one-particle characteristic-function estimate.
Fix
\mathrm{R}>0
and
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N})\in[-\mathrm{R},\mathrm{R}]^{N}
. By independence of the coordinates, and because
\mathscr{U}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(t)
depends only on
\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{U}(t)\rangle\big)\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{x_{i}}\left[\exp\big(\mathrm{i}u_{i}\,\mathscr{U}(t)\big)\right],

(191)

where, in the
i
-th factor,
\mathscr{U}(t)
denotes the globally defined one-dimensional random variable
\mathscr{U}_{\mathscr{X}}(t)
for a one-particle process started from
x_{i}
. For
t
beyond the deterministic lower time used below, this equals
-\Sigma(t)\,\mathrm{u}_{\mathscr{X}}(t)
on the endpoint event by Proposition
2.4
. We prove that, for every fixed starting point
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there are constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},x)\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},x)>0
such that

\sup_{|u|\leq\mathrm{R}}\left|\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\exp\big(\mathrm{i}u\,\mathscr{U}(t)\big)\right]-\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},x)\gamma(t)^{1/2},\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},x).

(192)

Step 2: Eigenfunction identity at the imaginary spectral value.
Fix
u
with
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}
and define
w(u,t)=-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}
. Since
w(u,t)
is purely imaginary,
|w(u,t)-\mathfrak{L}|^{2}=\mathfrak{L}^{2}+u^{2}/\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq\mathfrak{L}^{2}+\mathrm{R}^{2}/\Sigma(t)^{2}
, and
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
by
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
. Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{disc}}\in\mathscr{T}
so that
\mathrm{R}^{2}/\Sigma(t)^{2}<2\mathfrak{L}/\mathfrak{U}+\mathfrak{U}^{-2}
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{disc}}
; then
w(u,t)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{L}+\mathfrak{U}^{-1}}(\mathfrak{L})
uniformly in
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}
. Hence Proposition
3.1
applies in both time regimes and yields, for every
t\in\mathscr{T}
with
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{disc}}
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w(u,t))\right]=\rho_{x}(w(u,t))\\
\times\exp\Bigg(-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t\,w(u,t)+\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\Big((1-\tau_{n})\log\left(1-\alpha_{n}w(u,t)\right)-\tau_{n}\log\left(1+\beta_{n}w(u,t)\right)\Big)\Bigg).

(193)

Step 3: Exact exponent identity on the endpoint event.
Let

\mathcal{A}_{t}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\{\mathscr{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\}.

By Proposition
3.2
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{L}}\in\mathscr{T}
and a constant
\mathrm{M}_{x}>0
such that, for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{L}}
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathrm{c}})\leq\mathrm{M}_{x}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}\Sigma(t)}.

The contribution of
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathrm{c}}
is deferred to Step 5, where it is controlled by Cauchy–Schwarz together with the square-integrability estimate (
213
); no separate pointwise bound on
\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(w(u,t))
is needed at this stage. All endpoint-window estimates below are applied only on the event
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, and their lower times are deterministic, because they come from the endpoint versions of Proposition
2.3
and from the endpoint-uniform construction in Proposition
2.4
. Using the explicit form
\rho_{x}(w)=\prod_{k=0}^{x-1}(1-w/\lambda_{k})
from (
9
) together with the absolutely convergent series
\log(1+z)=\sum_{m\geq 1}(-1)^{m+1}z^{m}/m
on
|z|<1
, applied with
z=\mathrm{i}u/(\lambda_{k}\Sigma(t))
, we obtain

\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\right)=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{X}(t)-1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}+\frac{u^{2}}{2\Sigma(t)^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{X}(t)-1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}+\Xi_{1}(u,t)\right),

(194)

where

\Xi_{1}(u,t)=-\sum_{m=3}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mathrm{i}u)^{m}}{m\,\Sigma(t)^{m}}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{X}(t)-1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{m}}.

(195)

By
(A1)
,
\lambda_{n}>\mathfrak{L}
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Therefore, whenever
|u|\leq\mathfrak{L}\Sigma(t)/2
, the geometric-series bound applied to the tail in the preceding display gives

\left|\Xi_{1}(u,t)\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{L}^{-3}\frac{|u|^{3}\mathscr{X}(t)}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}.

(196)

Indeed, after factoring out the
m=3
term, each remaining summand is bounded by the corresponding power of
|u|/(\mathfrak{L}\Sigma(t))\leq 1/2
. On
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, the endpoint condition gives
|\mathscr{X}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t)|\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
. Choose the deterministic lower time so that
\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq\mathrm{C}_{1}
and the lower comparison
\mathscr{Z}(t)\geq\mathrm{C}_{1}\Sigma(t)^{2}
from
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
is valid. Then
\mathscr{X}(t)\leq 2\mathscr{Z}(t)
on
\mathcal{A}_{t}
. The upper comparison
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
from the same proposition therefore gives, uniformly for
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}
and every
t
beyond this deterministic lower time,

\left|\Xi_{1}(u,t)\right|\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\leq 4\mathfrak{L}^{-3}\mathrm{C}_{2}\frac{|u|^{3}}{\Sigma(t)},

which is bounded by a constant depending only on
\mathrm{R}
times
\Sigma(t)^{-1}
. Next, define

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\exp\left(\begin{aligned} &\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}t\,w(u,t)\\
&\quad-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\left((1-\tau_{n})\log\left(1-\alpha_{n}w(u,t)\right)-\tau_{n}\log\left(1+\beta_{n}w(u,t)\right)\right)\end{aligned}\right),

so that (
193
) can be rewritten as

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(w(u,t)\right)\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)\right]=\rho_{x}\left(w(u,t)\right).

(197)

Expanding
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)
at
w=0
by the same absolutely convergent logarithmic series yields

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)=\exp\left(\begin{aligned} &-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\,t\\
&\quad-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\tau_{n}\beta_{n}\big)\\
&\quad-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\frac{u^{2}}{2\Sigma(t)^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}^{2}-\tau_{n}\beta_{n}^{2}\big)+\Xi_{2}(u,t)\end{aligned}\right),

(198)

where

\Xi_{2}(u,t)=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{m=3}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m\,\Sigma(t)^{m}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\Big((1-\tau_{n})(-\mathrm{i}u\alpha_{n})^{m}-\tau_{n}(\mathrm{i}u\beta_{n})^{m}\Big).

(199)

By the temporal bounds in
(A1)
,
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\leq\mathfrak{U}
. Hence, whenever
|u|\leq\Sigma(t)/(2\mathfrak{U})
, the same geometric-series estimate gives

\left|\Xi_{2}(u,t)\right|\leq 2\mathfrak{U}^{3}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\frac{|u|^{3}t}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}.

(200)

As in the preceding moment estimate,
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
together with
(A1)
gives constants
\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}(1+t)
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma}
. Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{R},2}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{R}\leq\Sigma(t)/(2\mathfrak{U})
for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{R},2}
. Therefore, for every
t>\max(\mathrm{T}_{\Sigma},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{R},2})
and every
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}
,

\left|\Xi_{2}(u,t)\right|\leq\frac{2\mathfrak{U}^{3}|u|^{3}}{\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}\Sigma(t)},

which is bounded by a constant depending only on
\mathrm{R}
times
\Sigma(t)^{-1}
.

Step 4: Identification of
\mathscr{U}(t)
and the explicit error bound.
Throughout this step,
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(j)}(0,t)
denotes the
j
th phase derivative
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(j)}(0,t)
of Definition
1.17
evaluated along the random terminal value
y=\mathscr{X}
. By Definition
1.17
we have

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)=-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}\,t+\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\big((1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}+\tau_{n}\beta_{n}\big),

(201)

and

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{y(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\big(\tau_{n}\beta_{n}^{2}-(1-\tau_{n})\alpha_{n}^{2}\big).

(202)

On
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, the terminal endpoint belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Therefore the endpoint estimate in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
gives the concrete comparison

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(2)}(0,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\Sigma(t)^{2}

(203)

for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}
on
\mathcal{A}_{t}
. Combining this comparison with the endpoint stationary-point construction from Proposition
2.4
, we have
\mathscr{U}(t)=-\Sigma(t)\,\mathrm{u}_{\mathscr{X}}(t)
on
\mathcal{A}_{t}
. Taking
t
beyond the deterministic maximum of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
and
\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}
, (
78
) combined with
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
applied at
w=0
gives, on
\mathcal{A}_{t}
,

\left|\mathscr{U}(t)-\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\Sigma(t)}\right|\leq\frac{4\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|^{2}}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}+\frac{2\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|}{\Sigma(t)}.

(204)

Combining (
194
) and (
198
) gives an exact exponent identity. The only mismatch between the sums in (
194
) and the derivatives in Definition
1.17
is the terminal spatial summand. Thus, on
\mathcal{A}_{t}
and for every
t
beyond the deterministic lower time fixed in the preceding paragraph,

\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(w(u,t)\right)\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)+\frac{u^{2}}{2\Sigma(t)^{2}}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(2)}(0,t)+\mathcal{E}_{1,t}(u)\right),

(205)

where the error term is not implicit but is the explicit quantity

\mathcal{E}_{1,t}(u)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)\lambda_{\mathscr{X}(t)}}-\frac{u^{2}}{2\Sigma(t)^{2}\lambda_{\mathscr{X}(t)}^{2}}+\Xi_{1}(u,t)+\Xi_{2}(u,t).

(206)

By
(A1)
, the two terminal summands satisfy

\frac{|u|}{\Sigma(t)\lambda_{\mathscr{X}(t)}}+\frac{u^{2}}{2\Sigma(t)^{2}\lambda_{\mathscr{X}(t)}^{2}}\leq\frac{|u|}{\mathfrak{L}\Sigma(t)}+\frac{u^{2}}{2\mathfrak{L}^{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}}.

Together with (
196
) and (
200
), this bounds
\mathcal{E}_{1,t}(u)
explicitly. Replacing
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)/\Sigma(t)
by
\mathscr{U}(t)
and replacing
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(2)}(0,t)/\Sigma(t)^{2}
by
1
gives

\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(w(u,t)\right)\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)=\exp\left(\mathrm{i}u\,\mathscr{U}(t)+\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)\right),

(207)

where

\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathcal{E}_{1,t}(u)+\mathrm{i}u\left(\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\Sigma(t)}-\mathscr{U}(t)\right)+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(2)}(0,t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}-1\right).

(208)

The preceding estimates give the following completely explicit bound on the endpoint-window event:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|u|}{\mathfrak{L}\Sigma(t)}+\frac{u^{2}}{2\mathfrak{L}^{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}}+4\mathfrak{L}^{-3}\mathrm{C}_{2}\frac{|u|^{3}}{\Sigma(t)}+\frac{2\mathfrak{U}^{3}|u|^{3}}{\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}\Sigma(t)}+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad+|u|\left(\frac{4\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|^{2}}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}+\frac{2\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|}{\Sigma(t)}\right).

(209)

Step 5:
L^{2}
bound on
\mathcal{E}_{2,t}
and on the endpoint complement.
We now check the probabilistic size of the explicit error uniformly for
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}
. Let
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{FD},p}
denote the constant in (
183
). Squaring (
3.2
), using
(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{m})^{2}\leq m(a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{m}^{2})
, and applying (
183
) with
p=2
and
p=4
, gives a constant
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{R},x)>0
and a deterministic lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{R},x)\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{R},x)
,

\sup_{|u|\leq\mathrm{R}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[|\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\right]\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{R},x)\gamma(t).

(210)

Indeed, the deterministic terms in (
3.2
) have squares bounded by constants depending only on
\mathrm{R}
times
\Sigma(t)^{-2}
or
\gamma(t)
, and
\Sigma(t)^{-2}\leq\gamma(t)
after increasing the lower time. The term involving
|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)|^{2}/\Sigma(t)^{3}
is controlled by

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\frac{|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)|^{4}}{\Sigma(t)^{6}}\right]\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{FD},4}(1+t)^{2}}{\Sigma(t)^{6}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{FD},4}}{\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{FD},4}}{\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{2}}\gamma(t),

while the term involving
\gamma(t)^{1/2}|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(1)}(0,t)|/\Sigma(t)
is controlled by

\gamma(t)\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{FD},2}(1+t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{FD},2}}{\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}}\gamma(t).

The complement estimate at the beginning of the proof and the inequality
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}\Sigma(t)/2}\leq\gamma(t)^{1/2}
, imposed by increasing the deterministic lower time, give constants
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(x)>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{L},\gamma}(x)\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{L},\gamma}(x)
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathrm{c}})^{1/2}\leq\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(x)\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

(211)

Step 6: Uniform
L^{2}
bound on
\mathrm{Z}_{t}(u)
and the one-particle CF estimate.
We now justify the substitution of (
207
) inside the expectation without assuming an exponential moment for
\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)
. Set

\mathrm{Z}_{t}(u)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\right)\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t).

We first record a uniform square-integrability bound for this random variable. By
(A1)
and the inequality
\log(1+v)\geq v/2
for
0\leq v\leq 1
, for every fixed
a>0
there exists
\mathrm{T}_{a}\in\mathscr{T}
such that

\exp\left(a\frac{\mathscr{X}(t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}\right)\leq\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(-\frac{2\mathfrak{B}_{1}a}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}\right),\qquad t>\mathrm{T}_{a}.

Indeed, choose
\mathrm{T}_{a}
so that
2\mathfrak{B}_{1}a/(\mathfrak{L}\Sigma(t)^{2})\leq 1
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{a}
. Then each factor in
\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}(-2\mathfrak{B}_{1}a/\Sigma(t)^{2})
is at least
\exp(a/\Sigma(t)^{2})
, because
\lambda_{n}\leq\mathfrak{B}_{1}
and
\lambda_{n}\geq\mathfrak{L}
. In the discrete-time regime we also require
\mathrm{T}_{a}
to make the real spectral value
-2\mathfrak{B}_{1}a/\Sigma(t)^{2}
lie in the ball allowed in Proposition
3.1
. Applying Proposition
3.1
at this real value, and using the deterministic comparison

t\leq\mathrm{C}_{t,\Sigma}\Sigma(t)^{2}

for a constant
\mathrm{C}_{t,\Sigma}>0
supplied by
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
together with
(A1)
, gives

\sup_{t>\mathrm{T}_{a}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\exp\left(a\frac{\mathscr{X}(t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}\right)\right]<\infty.

(212)

The last implication is uniform for
t>\mathrm{T}_{a}
: the fixed polynomial
\rho_{x}
is bounded on the compact set of spectral values reached after this threshold. In continuous time the temporal factor is bounded by

\exp(2\mathfrak{B}_{1}a\,\mathrm{C}_{t,\Sigma}).

In discrete time, with
\mathrm{T}_{a}
also chosen so that
\mathfrak{U}(2\mathfrak{B}_{1}a)/\Sigma(t)^{2}\leq 1/2
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{a}
, the inequalities
\log(1+v)\leq v
and
-\log(1-v)\leq 2v
give the bound

\exp(4\mathfrak{U}\mathfrak{B}_{1}a\,\mathrm{C}_{t,\Sigma}).

On the other hand, for the purely imaginary spectral value
-\mathrm{i}u/\Sigma(t)
, the elementary inequality
\log(1+v)\leq v
for
v\geq 0
gives

\left|\rho_{\mathscr{X}(t)}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\right)\right|^{2}\leq\exp\left(\frac{u^{2}}{\mathfrak{L}^{2}}\frac{\mathscr{X}(t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}\right),

and the temporal factor satisfies
|\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{time}}(u,t)|^{2}\leq\exp(\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{R}})
for a finite constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{R}}
depending only on
\mathrm{R}
and the constants in
(A1)
. In continuous time this modulus is equal to one. In discrete time, the bound follows by applying
\mathfrak{Re}\log(1+\mathrm{i}v)=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+v^{2})\leq v^{2}/2
to the factors with
v=\alpha_{n}u/\Sigma(t)
and
v=\beta_{n}u/\Sigma(t)
, followed by
t\leq\mathrm{C}_{t,\Sigma}\Sigma(t)^{2}
. Combining this deterministic temporal bound with (
212
), for
a=\mathrm{R}^{2}/\mathfrak{L}^{2}
, yields

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{R}}\\
|u|\leq\mathrm{R}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[|\mathrm{Z}_{t}(u)|^{2}\right]<\infty

(213)

for a threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{R}}\in\mathscr{T}
.

On
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, equation (
207
) gives

\mathrm{Z}_{t}(u)=\exp\left(\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\exp\big(\mathrm{i}u\,\mathscr{U}(t)\big)\exp(\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)).

Let
\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{Z}}(\mathrm{R},x)
be a finite constant larger than the square-root of the supremum in (
213
). On
\mathcal{A}_{t}\cap\{|\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)|\leq 1\}
, the inequality
|\mathrm{e}^{z}-1|\leq\mathrm{e}|z|
gives

\left|\mathrm{Z}_{t}(u)-\exp\left(\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\exp\big(\mathrm{i}u\,\mathscr{U}(t)\big)\right|\leq\mathrm{e}^{1+\mathrm{R}^{2}/2}|\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)|.

On
\mathcal{A}_{t}\cap\{|\mathcal{E}_{2,t}(u)|>1\}
we use Cauchy’s inequality, (
213
), and (
210
). Hence (
210
), (
211
), and (
213
) give a constant
\mathrm{K}_{\rho}(\mathrm{R},x)>0
and a deterministic lower time
\mathrm{T}_{\rho}(\mathrm{R},x)\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\rho}(\mathrm{R},x)
,

\sup_{|u|\leq\mathrm{R}}\left|\rho_{x}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\right)-\exp\left(\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\exp\big(\mathrm{i}u\,\mathscr{U}(t)\big)\right]\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{\rho}(\mathrm{R},x)\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

(214)

Since
\rho_{x}
is a fixed polynomial with
\rho_{x}(0)=1
, there are constants
\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{R}}>0
and
\mathrm{T}_{x,\mathrm{R}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{x,\mathrm{R}}
,

\sup_{|u|\leq\mathrm{R}}\left|\rho_{x}\left(-\frac{\mathrm{i}u}{\Sigma(t)}\right)-1\right|\leq\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{R}}\Sigma(t)^{-1}\leq\mathrm{K}_{x,\mathrm{R}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

Combining this estimate with (
214
), and taking
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},x)
to dominate the lower times in (
214
) and in the last displayed inequality, proves (
192
).

Step 7: From characteristic functions to general test functions.
Consequently, there exist constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})
and every
\mathbf{u}\in[-\mathrm{R},\mathrm{R}]^{N}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\langle\mathbf{u},\mathbf{U}(t)\rangle\big)\right]-\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})\gamma(t)^{1/2}.

(215)

This follows from the product identity at the beginning of the proof and the elementary telescoping bound for finite products whose factors are bounded by one.
Moreover, following the constants in the estimates above gives the explicit growth bound

\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}\exp\{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{R}^{2}\}(1+\mathrm{R})^{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}},\qquad\mathrm{R}\geq 1.

(216)

The exponential factor comes from the square-integrability estimate (
213
), where the auxiliary real spectral value is proportional to
\mathrm{R}^{2}/\Sigma(t)^{2}
; all remaining factors in (
3.2
) are polynomial in
\mathrm{R}
.

We now pass from characteristic functions to the observable
H
. Since
H
is allowed to grow polynomially, we first localize it in physical space and only then apply Fourier smoothing. This order is needed because the Fourier inversion step below is applied to a compactly supported function, while the finite first moment of the smoothing kernel converts the Lipschitz regularity of the cutoff observable into an explicit
\mathrm{R}^{-1}
smoothing error.

Choose

\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(\frac{\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})}{8\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}}\right)^{1/2},\qquad\mathrm{A}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{1/(4(q+1))},

after increasing the lower time so that
\gamma(t)<\mathrm{e}^{-8\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}}
and
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)\geq 1
. Let
\chi_{\mathrm{A}}
be a Lipschitz cutoff which equals one on
