|\mathbf{v}|\leq\mathrm{A}
, vanishes on
|\mathbf{v}|\geq 2\mathrm{A}
, takes values in
[0,1]
, and has Lipschitz constant at most
2/\mathrm{A}
. Set
H_{\mathrm{A}}=H\chi_{\mathrm{A}}
. The growth and Lipschitz assumptions on
H
imply, for
\mathrm{A}\geq 1
,

\|H_{\mathrm{A}}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathrm{C}_{N,q}\mathrm{C}_{H}\mathrm{A}^{q},\qquad\operatorname{Lip}(H_{\mathrm{A}})\leq\mathrm{C}_{N,q}\mathrm{C}_{H}\mathrm{A}^{q+1},\qquad\operatorname{supp}(H_{\mathrm{A}})\subseteq\{\mathbf{v}:|\mathbf{v}|\leq 2\mathrm{A}\}.

(217)

Indeed, the supremum bound follows from
|H(\mathbf{v})|\leq\mathrm{C}_{H}(1+|\mathbf{v}|^{q})
on the support of
\chi_{\mathrm{A}}
. For the Lipschitz bound, the product rule gives two contributions: the first is bounded by
\operatorname{Lip}(H)
on
|\mathbf{v}|\leq 2\mathrm{A}
, and the second is bounded by
\|H\|_{L^{\infty}(|\mathbf{v}|\leq 2\mathrm{A})}\operatorname{Lip}(\chi_{\mathrm{A}})
. Both are bounded by the middle expression in (
217
), after absorbing constants depending only on
N
and
q
.

We next record the smoothing estimate in the precise form used here. Let
\kappa:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the normalized fourth-power sinc kernel

\kappa(r)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{3}{8\pi}\left(\frac{\sin(r/4)}{r/4}\right)^{4},

with the value at
r=0
defined by continuity. With the Fourier convention
\widehat{g}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-ir\xi}g(r)\,\mathrm{d}r
, we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\kappa(r)\,\mathrm{d}r=1
, the Fourier transform of
\kappa
is supported in
[-1,1]
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|r|\kappa(r)\,\mathrm{d}r<\infty
, because
\kappa(r)
decays like
|r|^{-4}
. For
\mathrm{R}\geq 1
define
\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}(r)=\mathrm{R}\kappa(\mathrm{R}r)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}(\mathbf{v})=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}(v_{j})
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}
has total mass one, its Fourier transform is supported in
[-\mathrm{R},\mathrm{R}]^{N}
, and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|\mathbf{v}|\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}(\mathbf{v})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\leq\mathrm{C}_{N}\mathrm{R}^{-1}.

(218)

The last estimate follows by the triangle inequality
|\mathbf{v}|\leq|v_{1}|+\cdots+|v_{N}|
and the one-dimensional scaling identity
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|r|\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r=\mathrm{R}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|r|\kappa(r)\,\mathrm{d}r
.

Let
\mu_{t}
be the law of
\mathbf{U}(t)
and let
\nu
be the standard Gaussian law on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. If
f
is bounded, Lipschitz and supported in
\{|\mathbf{v}|\leq\mathrm{B}\}
, then (
218
) gives

\left|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}-\int f*\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\right|+\left|\int f\,\mathrm{d}\nu-\int f*\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{N}\operatorname{Lip}(f)\mathrm{R}^{-1}.

(219)

For the smoothed difference, Fourier inversion applies because
f
is compactly supported and
\widehat{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}}
is compactly supported. Writing
\phi_{t}
and
\phi_{\nu}
for the characteristic functions of
\mu_{t}
and
\nu
, respectively, we obtain

\left|\int f*\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}-\int f*\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{N}(1+\mathrm{B})^{N}\|f\|_{\infty}\mathrm{R}^{N}\sup_{\xi\in[-\mathrm{R},\mathrm{R}]^{N}}|\phi_{t}(\xi)-\phi_{\nu}(\xi)|.

(220)

Indeed,
|\widehat{f}(\xi)|\leq\|f\|_{1}\leq\mathrm{C}_{N}(1+\mathrm{B})^{N}\|f\|_{\infty}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{R}}}
is bounded by a constant depending only on
N
, and the integration region has volume
(2\mathrm{R})^{N}
. Combining (
219
) and (
220
), then using (
215
), gives the compact-support smoothing bound

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[f(\mathbf{U}(t))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}\!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}f(\mathbf{v})\exp\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{v}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\right|

(221)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{sm},N}\left(\operatorname{Lip}(f)\mathrm{R}^{-1}+(1+\mathrm{B})^{N}\|f\|_{\infty}\mathrm{R}^{N}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})\gamma(t)^{1/2}\right),

for every
\mathrm{R}\geq 1
, every compactly supported bounded Lipschitz
f
with support contained in
\{|\mathbf{v}|\leq\mathrm{B}\}
, and every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R},\mathbf{x})
.

Although (
215
) was first proved for fixed
\mathrm{R}
, the preceding estimates also apply with
\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)
after increasing the deterministic lower time once more. Indeed, Definition
1.12
gives
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
, and hence, after increasing the deterministic lower time,
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{x}}\sqrt{1+\log\Sigma(t)}
. This logarithmic growth is dominated by every positive power of
\Sigma(t)
: for each fixed
a\geq 0
,
b>0
and
c>0
appearing in Steps 2–6, we increase the deterministic lower time so that
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)^{a}\Sigma(t)^{-b}\leq c
for every later
t
.

Applying (
221
) to
f=H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}
, with
\mathrm{B}=2\mathrm{A}(t)
and
\mathrm{R}=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)
, gives

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}(\mathbf{U}(t))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}\!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}(\mathbf{v})\exp\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{v}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\right|

(222)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}\left(\mathrm{A}(t)^{q+1}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)^{-1}+(1+\mathrm{A}(t))^{N}\mathrm{A}(t)^{q}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)^{N}\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{cf}}(\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t),\mathbf{x})\gamma(t)^{1/2}\right).

The first term on the right-hand side of (
222
) has the required logarithmic decay. Indeed, by the definitions of
\mathrm{A}(t)
and
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)
, after increasing the lower time if necessary,

\mathrm{A}(t)^{q+1}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)^{-1}\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{x}}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4}.

For the second term, (
216
) and the definition of
\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)
give

\gamma(t)^{1/2}\exp\{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)^{2}\}\leq\gamma(t)^{3/8}.

All remaining factors in the second term of (
222
) are powers of
1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})
. Hence there is a constant
\mathrm{m}_{N,q,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that this second term is bounded, up to the constant
\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}
, by
\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{\mathrm{m}_{N,q,\mathbf{x}}}\gamma(t)^{3/8}
. The elementary exponential-polynomial comparison gives a number
\mathrm{r}_{N,q,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that
r^{\mathrm{m}_{N,q,\mathbf{x}}}\mathrm{e}^{-3r/8}\leq r^{-1/4}
for every
r\geq\mathrm{r}_{N,q,\mathbf{x}}
. Increasing the lower time so that
1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\geq\mathrm{r}_{N,q,\mathbf{x}}
, we get

(1+\mathrm{A}(t))^{N}\mathrm{A}(t)^{q}\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t)^{N}(1+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{F}}(t))^{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cf},\mathbf{x}}}\gamma(t)^{3/8}\leq\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4}.

Substituting these two estimates into (
222
) yields

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}(\mathbf{U}(t))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}\!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}(\mathbf{v})\exp\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{v}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4}.

(223)

It remains to remove the cutoff and insert the endpoint event. Choose
p=4(q+1)
in Proposition
3.4
. The endpoint moment bound (
184
) and the Gaussian
p
-moment give

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[|H(\mathbf{U}(t))|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathbf{U}(t)|>\mathrm{A}(t)\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]+\int_{|\mathbf{v}|>\mathrm{A}(t)}|H(\mathbf{v})|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-|\mathbf{v}|^{2}/2}}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}\leq\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4}.

(224)

Finally, on the endpoint complement, applying Proposition
3.2
to each coordinate and using the finite union bound
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
gives constants
\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{L},\mathbf{x}}>0
and
\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{L}}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{\mathrm{L},\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{L}}\Sigma(t)}

after increasing the lower time. Combining this with the cutoff bound gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[|H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}(\mathbf{U}(t))|\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{q/(4(q+1))}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{L}}\Sigma(t)}.

(225)

The last estimate uses the coordinatewise endpoint-window bound from Proposition
3.2
(combined with the finite union bound above) and the cutoff bound
|H_{\mathrm{A}(t)}|\leq\mathrm{C}_{q}\mathrm{C}_{H}\mathrm{A}(t)^{q}
. By Definition
1.12
,
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
, so
1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\leq 1+\frac{1}{2}\log\Sigma(t)
after increasing the lower time. The exponential factor
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{L}}\Sigma(t)}
therefore dominates the displayed logarithmic power, and we increase the lower time so that

\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{q/(4(q+1))}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{L}}\Sigma(t)}\leq\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4}

for every later
t
. Writing
\mathcal{D}_{t}(H)
for the difference inside the absolute value in (
190
), the triangle inequality combined with (
223
), (
224
), and (
225
) gives

\left|\mathcal{D}_{t}(H)\right|\leq\underbrace{\eqref{eq:truncated-H-rate}}_{\text{cutoff main term}}+\underbrace{\eqref{eq:H-tail-rate}}_{\text{tail $|\mathbf{U}|>\mathrm{A}(t)$}}+\underbrace{\eqref{eq:H-endpoint-complement-rate}}_{\text{endpoint complement}}\leq 3\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}\left(1+\log(\gamma(t)^{-1})\right)^{-1/4},

which is (
190
) with the explicit error in (
189
) after setting
\mathrm{C}_{H,\mathbf{x}}=3\mathrm{C}_{q,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{H}
and
\mathrm{T}_{H,\mathbf{x}}
equal to the maximum of all deterministic lower times introduced above.
∎

Recall the continuous Weyl chamber
\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}
from Definition
1.8
.

Proposition 3.6

(Endpoint ordering on
\mathscr{C}_{t}
)
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. There exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W}}
,

\left\{\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N},\ \mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\textnormal{ for every }i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\right\}=\left\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N},\ \mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}\textnormal{ for every }i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\right\}.

(226)

Proof.

On the endpoint window
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the ordering of the stationary-point coordinates is the same as the ordering of the terminal particle positions. We prove this directly from the stationary-point equation, rather than by comparing two asymptotic expansions. By Definition
1.17
, the phase
\mathfrak{F}_{y}(\cdot,t)
depends on
y
only through the terminal value
y(t)
, and hence so does the stationary point
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
via Definition
1.18
.

Choose
r\in(0,\mathrm{R}_{2})
small enough that
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}r\leq 1/4
, where
\mathrm{R}_{2}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}
are the constants in Proposition
2.3
. By the calibration in (
87
), the quadratic comparison (
78
), and the Newton-scale estimate (
84
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 1,\quad\text{and}\quad|\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(0,t)}\right|\leq 2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2},

uniformly over
y\in\mathscr{P}
with
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
and
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
; here the factor
2
on the second line uses the calibration on the first line to absorb the quadratic remainder in (
78
). The threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
already absorbs the auxiliary times
\mathrm{T}_{2,\mathscr{C}}
,
\mathrm{T}_{\mathscr{C},1}
, and
\mathrm{T}_{4,\mathscr{C}}
from Proposition
2.3
. Since
\gamma(t)\to 0
by
(A2)
and
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W},1}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
2\mathrm{B}_{\mathrm{A}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}<r
, and hence
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)\in(-r,r)
, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W},1}
and every such
y
. For the second derivative we use the strengthened estimate in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
: for
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
and
w\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{R}_{2}}(0)
,

\left|\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)-\Sigma(t)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|w|\right)\Sigma(t)^{2}.

Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W},2}\in\mathscr{T}
so that
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq 1/4
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W},2}
. With our choice of
r
, this gives
\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+|w|)\leq 1/2
whenever
|w|\leq r
, and therefore

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(2)}(w,t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}>0,\qquad y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t},\quad w\in[-r,r],

(227)

for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W},1},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{W},2})
. Thus, for each path
y\in\mathscr{P}
with
y(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
, the map
w\mapsto\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(w,t)
is strictly increasing on the interval
[-r,r]
, and its unique zero
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
lies in
(-r,r)
.

Take two paths
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
with
y(t),y^{\prime}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
and
y(t)<y^{\prime}(t)
. For every real
w\in[-r,r]
, the explicit formula for the phase in Definition
1.17
gives

\mathfrak{F}_{y^{\prime}}^{(1)}(w,t)-\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(w,t)=\sum_{\ell=y(t)+1}^{y^{\prime}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\ell}-w}.

(228)

The right-hand side is strictly positive: for real
w\in[-r,r]
, the choice of
r
together with
\mathrm{R}_{2}=\mathrm{R}_{-}/2=\min(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{U}^{-1})/2
from Proposition
2.3

(i)
and
(iii)
gives

\lambda_{\ell}-w\geq\mathfrak{L}-r>\mathfrak{L}-\mathrm{R}_{2}\geq\mathfrak{L}/2>0,

using
\lambda_{\ell}\geq\mathfrak{L}
from
(A1)
. Substituting
w=\mathrm{u}_{y^{\prime}}(t)
into (
228
) and using the stationary-point identity
\mathfrak{F}_{y^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y^{\prime}}(t),t)=0
gives

\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\mathrm{u}_{y^{\prime}}(t),t)<0.

Since
\mathfrak{F}_{y}^{(1)}(\cdot,t)
is strictly increasing on
[-r,r]
and vanishes at
\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
, this strict inequality forces
\mathrm{u}_{y^{\prime}}(t)<\mathrm{u}_{y}(t)
. Since
\Sigma(t)>0
, multiplying by
-\Sigma(t)
reverses the inequality, giving

y(t)<y^{\prime}(t)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)<\mathscr{U}_{y^{\prime}}(t).

(229)

If
y(t)=y^{\prime}(t)
, then by Definition
1.17
the two phases coincide, and hence so do the two stationary points. Combining the strict implication (
229
), the equality case
y(t)=y^{\prime}(t)\Rightarrow\mathscr{U}_{y}(t)=\mathscr{U}_{y^{\prime}}(t)
, and the symmetric case obtained by swapping
y
and
y^{\prime}
, we conclude that the strict order of
y(t),y^{\prime}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
is equivalent to that of
\mathscr{U}_{y}(t),\mathscr{U}_{y^{\prime}}(t)
. Applying this pairwise to
\mathscr{X}_{1}(t),\ldots,\mathscr{X}_{N}(t)
proves the displayed equality of events.
∎

3.3
Asymptotic expansion of the Karlin–McGregor semigroup

We first recall two lemmas. The following is implicit in
[
4
,
5
]
as a consequence of probabilistic intertwining considerations. It can also be proven directly using the LGV formula
[
56
]
.

Lemma 3.7

(Positivity of
\mathfrak{h}_{N}
)
.

Assume
(A1)
. Then for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}
,

\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})>0.

The following, in continuous time, is simply the well-known Karlin-McGregor formula
[
67
]
while in discrete time is a straightforward consequence of the LGV formula
[
56
]
along with the composition of the Markov kernels.

Lemma 3.8

(Karlin-McGregor semigroup)
.

Fix
r\in\mathscr{T}
and let
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}
. For every
s\in\mathscr{T}
with
s\geq r
, the time-inhomogeneous killed transition kernel satisfies

\mathbb{P}_{r,\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left(\mathbf{X}(s)=\mathbf{y},\,\mathcal{T}^{(r)}>s\right)=\det\left(\mathbb{P}_{r,x_{i}}\!\left(\mathscr{X}(s)=y_{j}\right)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},

(230)

where the single-particle laws
\mathbb{P}_{r,x_{i}}
are obtained from Definitions
1.1
–
1.2
by starting from
x_{i}
at absolute time
r
.

We will now convert the one-particle expansion into an
N
-particle determinant expansion. The next lemma is a Cauchy-Binet-type decomposition tailored to our coefficients and isolates the index structure that drives the leading asymptotics.

Lemma 3.9
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
, let
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}}
, and let
y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}\in\mathscr{P}
satisfy
y_{j}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
for every
j\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. Then

\displaystyle\det\left(\sum_{n=0}^{x_{i}}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t))}{n!}\eta_{n,y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\det\left((-1)^{k_{i}}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{j}+k_{j})}(0)}{n_{j}!k_{j}!}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}

(231)

\displaystyle\times\det\left(\mathscr{U}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}\eta_{n_{i},y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\Sigma(t)^{-\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}},

Proof.

We start from the determinant on the left-hand side of (
231
) and expand it by the Leibniz formula. This gives

\det\left(\sum_{n=0}^{x_{i}}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t))}{n!}\eta_{n,y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{N}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{x_{i}}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{y_{\sigma(i)}}(t))}{n!}\eta_{n,y_{\sigma(i)}}(t)\right).

(232)

All rearrangements below are finite. Indeed, for each fixed
x_{i}
, the polynomial
\rho_{x_{i}}
has degree
x_{i}
, so
\rho_{x_{i}}^{(r)}\equiv 0
whenever
r>x_{i}
. Thus extending sums over
n
and
k
to
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
only adds zero terms. Expanding the product in (
232
) therefore gives

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{N}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{i})}(\mathrm{u}_{y_{\sigma(i)}}(t))}{n_{i}!}\eta_{n_{i},y_{\sigma(i)}}(t).

(233)

Next we Taylor expand each derivative of
\rho_{x_{i}}
at the origin. Since
\rho_{x_{i}}
is a polynomial, Taylor’s formula is exact and finite:

\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{i})}(\mathrm{u}_{y_{\sigma(i)}}(t))=\sum_{k_{i}=0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{i}+k_{i})}(0)}{k_{i}!}\mathrm{u}_{y_{\sigma(i)}}(t)^{k_{i}}.

Substituting this identity into (
233
) gives the following finite expression:

\sum_{\sigma\in S_{N}}\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{i}+k_{i})}(0)}{n_{i}!k_{i}!}\mathrm{u}_{y_{\sigma(i)}}(t)^{k_{i}}\eta_{n_{i},y_{\sigma(i)}}(t).

For fixed
(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})
, the sum over
\sigma
is exactly the determinant expansion in the terminal index
j
. Hence the preceding display equals

\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{i}+k_{i})}(0)}{n_{i}!k_{i}!}\det\left(\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}\eta_{n_{i},y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(234)

The remaining step is a symmetrisation in the pair-index
(n_{i},k_{i})
. Because
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
is invariant under simultaneous permutations of the pairs
(n_{i},k_{i})
, the value of (
234
) is unchanged if we average over all such permutations. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{i}+k_{i})}(0)}{n_{i}!k_{i}!}\det\left(\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}\eta_{n_{i},y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\sum_{\mu\in S_{N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{\mu(i)}+k_{\mu(i)})}(0)}{n_{\mu(i)}!k_{\mu(i)}!}\det\left(\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{\mu(i)}}\eta_{n_{\mu(i)},y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(235)

In the determinant in (
235
), the row indexed by
i
is the row associated with the pair
(n_{\mu(i)},k_{\mu(i)})
. Reordering these rows back to the order
(n_{i},k_{i})
contributes the factor
\textnormal{sgn}(\mu)
. Therefore the inner sum over
\mu
is the Leibniz expansion of the determinant

\det\left(\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{j}+k_{j})}(0)}{n_{j}!k_{j}!}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N},

and (
235
) becomes

\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\det\left(\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{j}+k_{j})}(0)}{n_{j}!k_{j}!}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left(\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}\eta_{n_{i},y_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(236)

Finally, by the definition of the stationary-point coordinate,
\mathscr{U}_{y_{j}}(t)=-\Sigma(t)\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t)
. Hence

\mathrm{u}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}=(-1)^{k_{i}}\Sigma(t)^{-k_{i}}\mathscr{U}_{y_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}.

In the second determinant in (
236
), this factor is constant along row
i
. Pulling the factors
\Sigma(t)^{-k_{i}}
out of the rows gives the global factor
\Sigma(t)^{-\sum_{i}k_{i}}
, while the product of the signs
\prod_{i}(-1)^{k_{i}}
may equivalently be inserted into the rows of the first determinant. This gives exactly (
231
).
∎

The next proposition lifts the one-particle estimates from Proposition
2.11
to the killed
N
-particle semigroup. The starting point is the killed determinant formula of Lemma
3.8
into its entries we insert the one-particle expansion of Section
2.5
. We then use Lemma
3.9
to reorganize the result into a finite sum indexed by pairs
(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
.

The main issue is to identify which index families contribute at order
\Sigma(t)^{-N(N-1)/2}
. The cancellation mechanism comes from the antisymmetry of the first determinant, while the parity information is supplied by (
109
). The final expectation is evaluated by combining the moment bounds of Proposition
3.4
with the quantitative Gaussian estimate from Proposition
3.5
. The moment bound is the ingredient that places the polynomially growing observable
\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))F(\mathbf{U}(t))
within the admissible test-function class of that proposition.

For the quantitative statement below define

\mathrm{R}_{\eta}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(\gamma(t)^{1/2}+4\left(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2\right)\mathrm{C}_{2}\gamma(t)^{1/2}+6\Lambda\Sigma(t)^{-2}\right)+\sqrt{2}\,\Sigma(t)^{-1}+\mathrm{e}^{-\Sigma(t)^{2/3}/(8\,2^{1/3})}.

(237)

We compare this coefficient-error scale with the single Gaussian error scale
\psi_{\mathrm{G}}
from (
10
). Set

\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\left(1+4\left(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2\right)\mathrm{C}_{2}+6\Lambda\right)+\sqrt{2}+1.

(238)

By Definition
1.12
,
\gamma(t)\to 0
and
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
. Hence, after increasing the lower time so that
\gamma(t)<1
,
\gamma(t)^{1/2}\leq\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
,
\Sigma(t)^{-1}\leq\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
,
\Sigma(t)^{-2}\leq\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
, and
\exp(-\Sigma(t)^{2/3}/(8\,2^{1/3}))\leq\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
. Substituting these four comparisons into (
237
) gives

\mathrm{R}_{\eta}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)

(239)

for every
t
beyond this enlarged lower time.

Proposition 3.10

(Karlin–McGregor asymptotics)
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}
and let
F:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded and Lipschitz. Then there exist
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F(\mathbf{U}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\,\Sigma(t)^{-\frac{N}{2}(N-1)}}-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}F(\mathbf{u})\,\Delta(\mathbf{u})\,\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

(240)

Proof.

Outline of strategy:
First we split the terminal sum into endpoints lying in
\mathscr{C}_{t}
and its complement; the complement is controlled by the endpoint-window estimate in Proposition
3.2
applied coordinatewise. On the endpoint window, Proposition
2.11
gives a uniform one-particle expansion, so the Karlin–McGregor determinant can be expanded by Lemma
3.9
. The determinant cancellation and parity estimates then identify the unique leading family. The remaining expectation is evaluated using the Gaussian limit in Proposition
3.5
and the moment bounds in Proposition
3.4
.

Step 1: Restriction to the endpoint window and determinant expansion.
Set

\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}=1/(8\,2^{1/3})
. Recall that
\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
denotes the coordinatewise endpoint window from the beginning of this section. Applying Proposition
3.2
to each coordinate and using the finite union bound
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}\}
, there exist constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}\in\mathscr{T}
,
\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{loc}}>0
and
\mathrm{M}_{\mathbf{x}}>0
such that, for
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\right]\leq\mathrm{M}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{loc}}\Sigma(t)}.

(241)

Since
F
is bounded, the part of the expectation in (
240
) coming from
\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
is bounded in absolute value by
\|F\|_{\infty}
times the right-hand side of (
241
). Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}\geq\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}
so that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{loc}}\Sigma(t)}\leq\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}-1}
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}}
, which is possible because
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
. Then

\|F\|_{\infty}\mathrm{M}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\mathrm{loc}}\Sigma(t)}\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}-1},

(242)

for a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail}}>0
depending only on
N
,
\mathbf{x}
,
F
, and the constants in Proposition
3.2
.

Now fix
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}\cap\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
. For each component
m_{j}
, choose any path
y_{j}\in\mathscr{P}
with
y_{j}(t)=m_{j}
. Since the phase and all coefficients in Proposition
2.11
depend on this auxiliary path only through its terminal value at time
t
, we use the following local shorthand in this proof: for any endpoint
m\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,
\mathfrak{F}_{m}(\cdot,t)
,
\mathrm{u}_{m}(t)
and
\eta_{n,m}(t)
denote the corresponding objects obtained from any path with terminal value
m
at time
t
. By Lemma
3.8
(applied at
r=0
),

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathbf{X}(t)=\mathbf{m},\mathcal{T}>t\right]=\det\left(\mathbb{P}_{x_{i}}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=m_{j}\right]\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}.

(243)

Applying (
108
) from Proposition
2.11
to each matrix entry, with the auxiliary path
y_{j}
attached to the terminal endpoint
m_{j}
, gives

\mathbb{P}_{x_{i}}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=m_{j}\right]=\left(\sum_{n=0}^{x_{i}}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n)}(\mathrm{u}_{m_{j}}(t))}{n!}\eta_{n,m_{j}}(t)+\mathrm{E}_{i,j}(t)\right)\mathbb{P}_{0}\left[\mathscr{X}(t)=m_{j}\right],

where

|\mathrm{E}_{i,j}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}_{x_{i}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}}.

This is exactly the uniform complementary-contour error in (
108
). The stationary points
\mathrm{u}_{m_{j}}(t)
lie in a fixed compact subset of the holomorphic neighbourhood, the polynomial derivatives
\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n)}
involve only finitely many
n
because
x_{i}
is fixed, and the coefficient bounds in (
109
) bound the finitely many
\eta_{n,m_{j}}(t)
uniformly over
m_{j}\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
after the lower time is chosen to dominate the corresponding finite set of coefficient thresholds. Here and below,
\mathbb{P}_{\underline{0}}^{N}
denotes the independent
N
-particle product law with initial vector
\underline{0}=(0,\ldots,0)
; it is not a killed or conditioned law. Hence determinant multilinearity gives a constant
\mathrm{C}_{N,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that

\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[\mathbf{X}(t)=\mathbf{m},\mathcal{T}>t]}{\mathbb{P}_{\underline{0}}^{N}[\mathbf{X}(t)=\mathbf{m}]}-\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\Psi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m}}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{N,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}},

(244)

where

\Psi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\det\left((-1)^{k_{i}}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(n_{j}+k_{j})}(0)}{n_{j}!k_{j}!}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\det\left(\mathscr{U}_{m_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}\eta_{n_{i},m_{j}}(t)\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\Sigma(t)^{-\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}}.

The sum is finite: if
n_{j}+k_{j}>x_{i}
for every row index
i
in some column, then the corresponding column of the first determinant is zero. Therefore a non-zero term must satisfy
n_{j}+k_{j}\leq\max_{i}x_{i}
for every column index
j
.
Choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{err}}\in\mathscr{T}
so that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{err}}
,

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}_{\varphi}\Sigma(t)^{2/3}}\leq\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}-1}.

This is possible because
\Sigma(t)\to\infty
, and it ensures that the accumulated determinant-expansion error in (
244
), after multiplication by the bounded function
F
and summation against
\mathbb{P}_{\underline{0}}^{N}
, is absorbed into the same remainder scale as (
242
).

For
\mathbf{m}\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
write
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{m}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}(\mathscr{U}_{m_{1}}(t),\ldots,\mathscr{U}_{m_{N}}(t))
. By Proposition
3.6
, there exists
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{ord}}\in\mathscr{T}
such that, for all
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{ord}}
, the ordering event for endpoints in
\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
is equivalent to the ordering event for the corresponding stationary-point coordinates. Summing (
244
) against
F(\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{m}}(t))\mathbb{P}_{\underline{0}}^{N}[\mathbf{X}(t)=\mathbf{m}]
over
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}\cap\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
, and using (
242
), gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[F(\mathbf{U}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\underline{0}}^{N}\left[\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}}\Psi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{X}(t)}(t)F(\mathbf{U}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N},\,\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]

(245)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{KM},1}(t),

with

|\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{KM},1}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM},1}\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}-1},

(246)

for every
t>\max(\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{tail}},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{ord}},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec}},\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{err}})
, for a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM},1}>0
depending only on
N
,
\mathbf{x}
,
F
, and the constants already displayed above.

Step 2: Uniform bounds for each index family.
Recall the explicit coefficient-error scale
\mathrm{R}_{\eta}(t)
from (
237
); after increasing the lower time, (
239
) gives the simpler bound
\mathrm{R}_{\eta}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
. Since the sum in (
245
) is finite and every non-zero term has
n_{j}+k_{j}\leq\max_{i}x_{i}
, we choose this lower time to dominate
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{dec},n}
for every
0\leq n\leq\max_{i}x_{i}
. The expression inside the first parentheses in (
237
) is exactly the endpoint error
\gamma(t)^{1/2}
together with the local stationary-point bound

|\mathrm{u}_{m}(t)|\leq 4\left(\mathfrak{L}^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{1}^{-1}+2\right)\mathrm{C}_{2}\gamma(t)^{1/2}+6\Lambda\Sigma(t)^{-2},

which follows by combining the endpoint first-derivative estimate in
(ii)
of Proposition
2.3
, the scale comparison
\mathscr{Z}(t)\leq\mathrm{C}_{2}\Sigma(t)^{2}
, the denominator lower bound
\mathfrak{F}_{m}^{(2)}(0,t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{2}/2
, and the quadratic estimate (
78
). Therefore, through
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, the first term controls

\left|\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{m}^{(2)}(\mathrm{u}_{m}(t),t)}{\Sigma(t)^{2}}-1\right|.

The middle term is supplied by the lower curvature bound (
102
); the final term is the complementary-contour contribution. Therefore (
109
) implies that, for each fixed even
n
and every
m\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
,

\left|\eta_{n,m}(t)-c_{n}\Sigma(t)^{-n}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{n}\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\Sigma(t)^{-n},\qquad c_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\
(-1)^{n/2}(n-1)!!,&n\geq 2,\end{cases}

(247)

while for each fixed odd
n
,

|\eta_{n,m}(t)|\leq\mathrm{C}_{n}\Sigma(t)^{-n-1}.

(248)

Applying the Leibniz formula to the second determinant in
\Psi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{X}(t)}(t)
and using (
247
)–(
248
) row by row, we obtain, for each fixed pair
(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})
, a polynomial
\mathrm{P}_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}}
with non-negative coefficients and a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{x}}>0
such that on
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}
,

\left|\Psi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{X}(t)}(t)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{P}_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k}}(|\mathbf{U}(t)|)\Sigma(t)^{-\alpha(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})},

(249)

where

\alpha(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(k_{i}+n_{i}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\text{$n_{i}$ is odd}\}}\right).

Choose
p
larger than the degrees of the finitely many polynomials that occur. Then Proposition
3.4
gives constants
\mathrm{T}_{p,N}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{p,N,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that the expectations of these polynomial factors, multiplied by
\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}
, are bounded by
\mathrm{C}_{p,N,\mathbf{x}}
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{p,N}
. This is the form needed here, because the summation in (
245
) is already restricted to
\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}
.

Step 3: Identification of the leading index family.

If two of the integers
n_{i}+k_{i}
coincide, then two columns of the first determinant in
\Psi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m}}(t)
coincide, and hence that term vanishes. Thus only pairs with distinct non-negative integers
n_{i}+k_{i}
can contribute. For such pairs,

\sum_{i=1}^{N}(n_{i}+k_{i})\geq 0+1+\cdots+(N-1)=\mathrm{d}_{N}.

Since the parity contribution in
\alpha(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})
is non-negative, every non-zero index family satisfies
\alpha(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})\geq\mathrm{d}_{N}
. If
\alpha(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})\geq\mathrm{d}_{N}+1
, then (
249
) and the moment bound imply that the total contribution of all such pairs is bounded by

\mathrm{C}_{N,F,\mathbf{x}}\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}-1}.

(250)

Equality
\alpha(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{k})=\mathrm{d}_{N}
can occur only if
\{n_{i}+k_{i}:i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket\}=\{0,1,\ldots,N-1\}
and every
n_{i}
is even. Among these equality cases, the determinant with the leading part of (
247
) inserted has rows proportional to
\mathscr{U}_{m_{j}}(t)^{k_{i}}
. Hence, if two of the
k_{i}
coincide, this leading determinant is zero; all remaining terms in the row-by-row expansion contain at least one coefficient error from (
247
) and are therefore bounded, after taking expectation and using Proposition
3.4
, by
\mathrm{C}_{N,F,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}
. Thus the only equality cases that can contribute to the limiting constant must have distinct
k_{i}
. If some
n_{i}
were a positive even integer in such a case, then

\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(n_{i}+k_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{N}n_{i}<\mathrm{d}_{N}.

But
N
distinct non-negative integers have sum at least
\mathrm{d}_{N}
. Therefore, if the
k_{i}
are distinct, all
n_{i}
must be zero. Hence the only index family that contributes to the limiting constant is

\mathcal{M}_{N}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{k}_{\sigma}):\sigma\in S_{N},\ k_{\sigma,i}=\sigma(i)-1\right\}.

For the leading family, (
247
) with
n=0
gives
|\eta_{0,m}(t)-1|\leq\mathrm{C}_{0}\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
uniformly over
m\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
. Expanding the determinant by the Leibniz formula therefore yields, for every
\sigma\in S_{N}
,

\Psi_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{k}_{\sigma},\mathbf{X}(t)}(t)=A_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\det\left(\mathscr{U}_{j}(t)^{\sigma(i)-1}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}+\mathrm{R}_{\sigma}(t),

(251)

Here
\mathscr{U}_{j}(t)
denotes the
j
th component of the random vector
\mathbf{U}(t)
, and the remainder satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\underline{0}}^{N}\left[|\mathrm{R}_{\sigma}(t)|\,|F(\mathbf{U}(t))|\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N},\,\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{N,F,\mathbf{x}}\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}.

Combining this bound with (
250
), and using
\Sigma(t)^{-1}\leq\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
after increasing the lower time, shows that every pair outside
\mathcal{M}_{N}
contributes at most

\mathrm{C}_{N,F,\mathbf{x}}\left(1+\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\right)\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}

(252)

to (
245
).

Step 4: Evaluation of the leading family.

For
\sigma\in S_{N}
, pulling out row signs and then permuting columns gives

A_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})=\det\left((-1)^{\sigma(i)-1}\frac{\rho_{x_{i}}^{(\sigma(j)-1)}(0)}{(\sigma(j)-1)!}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}=\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\,\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}).

Similarly,

\det\left(\mathscr{U}_{j}(t)^{\sigma(i)-1}\right)_{i,j=1}^{N}=\textnormal{sgn}(\sigma)\,\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t)).

The signs cancel. Since
|\mathcal{M}_{N}|=N!
, substituting (
251
) and (
252
) into (
245
), and then using (
239
) together with
\Sigma(t)^{-1}\leq\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
after increasing the lower time, gives

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[F(\mathbf{U}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]-\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\underline{0}}^{N}\left[\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))F(\mathbf{U}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N},\,\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\right|

(253)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}_{N,F,\mathbf{x}}\left(1+\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}}\right)\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}.

Step 5: Identification of the remaining expectation.

Define the observable

H_{F}(\mathbf{u})\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\Delta(\mathbf{u})F(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}\}}.

This is exactly the function to which we apply Proposition
3.5
. Since
F
is bounded and Lipschitz,
H_{F}
satisfies the hypotheses of that proposition. We spell this out because the indicator of the Weyl chamber appears in the definition of
H_{F}
. The Vandermonde determinant is a polynomial of degree
\mathrm{d}_{N}
, and therefore, on every ball of radius
\mathrm{R}\geq 1
, both
\Delta
and its gradient are bounded by constants depending only on
N
times
\mathrm{R}^{\mathrm{d}_{N}}
. If both points lie in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}
, the local Lipschitz bound for
H_{F}
follows from the product rule and the Lipschitz bound for
F
; if both points lie outside
\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}
, both values of
H_{F}
are zero. If one point lies in
\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}
and the other does not, choose a point on the line segment between them that lies on the boundary; at this point
\Delta=0
, and applying the same gradient bound for
\Delta
on each of the two subsegments gives the required Lipschitz estimate across the boundary. Thus there are constants
\mathrm{C}_{H_{F}}>0
and
q_{F}=\mathrm{d}_{N}+1
such that
H_{F}
satisfies the growth and local Lipschitz assumptions of Proposition
3.5
. Therefore, for every
t
beyond the lower time supplied by that proposition,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\underline{0}}^{N}\left[\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))F(\mathbf{U}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N},\,\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\right.

(254)

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}F(\mathbf{u})\,\Delta(\mathbf{u})\,\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{H_{F},\underline{0}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

Dividing (
253
) by
\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}
and combining the result with (
254
) gives (
240
) after increasing
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})
so that it dominates
\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\mathrm{C}_{N,F,\mathbf{x}}(1+\mathrm{C}_{\eta,\mathrm{G}})+\mathrm{C}_{H_{F},\underline{0}}
.
∎

4
Proof of main results

4.1
Proof of Theorem
1.13

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.

Applying Proposition
3.10
with
F\equiv 1
gives a quantitative estimate. In this case the observable in Proposition
3.5
is
H_{1}(\mathbf{u})=\Delta(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}\}}
, and therefore, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[\mathcal{T}>t]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}\Delta(\mathbf{u})\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

It remains only to identify the deterministic integral. By the normalization of the ordered
\mathrm{GOE}_{N}
eigenvalue density in Definition
1.19
,

\frac{N!}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(3/2)}{\Gamma(1+i/2)}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}\Delta(\mathbf{u})\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}=1,

and from this we readily obtain that Theorem
1.13
holds with
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}=\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}=\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})
.
∎

4.2
Proof of Corollary
1.14

The argument combines the finite-horizon conditioning identity (
7
) with the collision-tail asymptotics from Theorem
1.13
to construct the limiting law and identify the Doob
h
-transform. The only deterministic point requiring care is that, in the discrete-time regime, the survival factor after time
t
is computed in the environment shifted by
t
. We isolate this comparison first.

Lemma 4.1

(Shifted deterministic scales)
.

Fix
t\in\mathscr{T}
. In the discrete-time regime, set

\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\alpha_{t+n},\qquad\widetilde{\beta}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\beta_{t+n},\qquad\widetilde{\tau}_{n}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\tau_{t+n},\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

while in the continuous-time regime there is no shift in the parameters. Define the shifted centre and shifted fluctuation scale by

\mathrm{z}_{t}(s)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}\frac{s-t}{\Lambda}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\frac{1}{\Lambda}\sum_{n=t}^{s-1}\big(\alpha_{n}(1-\tau_{n})+\beta_{n}\tau_{n}\big),\qquad\mathscr{Z}_{t}(s)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\lfloor\mathrm{z}_{t}(s)\rceil,

and

\Sigma_{t}(s)^{2}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}_{t}(s)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\sum_{n=t}^{s-1}\big(\alpha_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})-\beta_{n}^{2}\tau_{n}\big).

The corresponding shifted error scale is

\gamma_{t}(s)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max\left(\sup_{r\geq s}\left|\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}_{t}(r)+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}_{t}(r)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\right|,\sup_{r\geq s}\Sigma_{t}(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right),\qquad\psi_{\mathrm{G},t}(s)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left(1+\log(\gamma_{t}(s)^{-1})\right)^{-\frac{1}{4}},

whenever the lower time has been chosen so that
\gamma_{t}(s)<1
. Then the shifted model satisfies
(A1)
and
(A2)
with the same structural constants as the original model. Moreover, there exist a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}>0
, depending only on the constants in
(A1)
and on
\Lambda
, and a threshold
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{sh}}(t)\in\mathscr{T}
, depending on the fixed shift
t
, such that, for every
s>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{sh}}(t)
,

\left|\Sigma_{t}(s)^{2}-\Sigma(s)^{2}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}(1+t).

(255)

Consequently, after increasing the lower time depending on
t
,

\left|\frac{\Sigma_{t}(s)}{\Sigma(s)}-1\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}(1+t)}{\Sigma(s)^{2}}.

(256)

In particular,
\Sigma_{t}(s)/\Sigma(s)\rightarrow 1
and
\gamma_{t}(s)\rightarrow 0
as
s\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

The shifted model has the same spatial rates
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
. In discrete time,
(A1)
is preserved because it is uniform in the temporal index, and
(A2)
is unchanged because it involves only the spatial reciprocal-rate averages. The continuous-time case is immediate, since there is no temporal environment to shift.

We compare the deterministic centres before rounding. Set

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{sh}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{\max(1,\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-1},\mathfrak{U})}{\Lambda}.

By the definitions of
\mathrm{z}
and
\mathrm{z}_{t}
, and by the temporal bounds in
(A1)
,

\left|\mathrm{z}(s)-\mathrm{z}_{t}(s)\right|\leq\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{R}_{+}\}}\frac{t}{\Lambda}+\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathscr{T}=\mathbb{Z}_{+}\}}\frac{1}{\Lambda}\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\big(\alpha_{n}(1-\tau_{n})+\beta_{n}\tau_{n}\big)\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{sh}}t.

Since both
\mathscr{Z}(s)
and
\mathscr{Z}_{t}(s)
are nearest-integer roundings, the two rounding errors contribute at most one in total, and hence

\left|\mathscr{Z}(s)-\mathscr{Z}_{t}(s)\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{sh}}t+1.

Therefore the spatial parts of
\Sigma(s)^{2}
and
\Sigma_{t}(s)^{2}
differ by at most

\left|\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}(s)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}-\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{Z}_{t}(s)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\right|\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\left|\mathscr{Z}(s)-\mathscr{Z}_{t}(s)\right|\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-2}\big(\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{sh}}t+1\big),

where the first inequality uses
\lambda_{n}^{-2}\leq\mathfrak{L}^{-2}
from
(A1)
. Similarly, in the discrete-time regime,

\left|\sum_{n=0}^{s-1}\big(\alpha_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})-\beta_{n}^{2}\tau_{n}\big)-\sum_{n=t}^{s-1}\big(\alpha_{n}^{2}(1-\tau_{n})-\beta_{n}^{2}\tau_{n}\big)\right|\leq\sum_{n=0}^{t-1}\left(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2}\right)\leq\bigl(\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2}\bigr)t,

again by
(A1)
. Taking

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\mathfrak{L}^{-2}(\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{sh}}+1)+\mathfrak{B}_{2}^{-2}+\mathfrak{U}^{2}

gives (
255
).

Dividing (
255
) by
\Sigma(s)^{2}
gives the corresponding bound for the squared scales. Increase the lower time so that
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}(1+t)\Sigma(s)^{-2}\leq 1/2
. Then both scales are positive and the elementary inequality
|\sqrt{1+r}-1|\leq|r|
, valid for
r\geq-1/2
, gives (
256
). Since
\Sigma(s)\to\infty
by
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, this proves
\Sigma_{t}(s)/\Sigma(s)\to 1
.

It remains only to check the shifted logarithmic scale. The first term in
\gamma_{t}(s)
tends to zero by
(A2)
, since
\mathscr{Z}_{t}(r)\to\infty
uniformly as
r\to\infty
with
r\geq s
. The second term tends to zero because (
256
) gives
\Sigma_{t}(s)\geq\Sigma(s)/2
after increasing the lower time, while
\Sigma(s)\to\infty
. Hence
\gamma_{t}(s)\to 0
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.14
.

We first identify the ratio of survival probabilities which appears in the finite-horizon conditioning formula (
7
). Use the shifted notation from Lemma
4.1
. The proof of Theorem
1.13
uses only the structural constants in
(A1)
–
(A2)
, the convergence control of the reciprocal-rate averages, and the initial point. By Lemma
4.1
, the shifted model has the same structural constants, the same reciprocal-rate convergence, and
\gamma_{t}(s)\to 0
. Thus Theorem
1.13
applies to the shifted model; in this shifted application the deterministic scale and Gaussian error scale are precisely
\Sigma_{t}(s)
and
\psi_{\mathrm{G},t}(s)
.
Applying Theorem
1.13
to the shifted model gives constants
\mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{col},t,\mathbf{y}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},t,\mathbf{y}}>0
such that, for every
s>\mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{col},t,\mathbf{y}}
,

\left|\frac{H_{t,s}(\mathbf{y})}{\mathrm{A}_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{y})\,\Sigma_{t}(s)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-1\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},t,\mathbf{y}}}{\mathrm{A}_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{y})}\psi_{\mathrm{G},t}(s).

(257)

Applying Theorem
1.13
at the initial time
0
gives constants
\mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}>0
such that, for every
s>\mathrm{S}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}
,

\left|\frac{H_{0,s}(\mathbf{x})}{\mathrm{A}_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\,\Sigma(s)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-1\right|\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}}{\mathrm{A}_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(s).

Set
\mathrm{C}_{N,\mathrm{pow}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\max\{1,\mathrm{d}_{N}2^{\mathrm{d}_{N}+1}\}
. By Lemma
4.1
, after increasing the lower time so that the right-hand side of (
256
) is at most
1/2
, the mean-value theorem on the interval
[1/2,3/2]
gives

\left|\left(\frac{\Sigma(s)}{\Sigma_{t}(s)}\right)^{\mathrm{d}_{N}}-1\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{N,\mathrm{pow}}\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}(1+t)}{\Sigma(s)^{2}}.

Combining the two collision-tail estimates with this scale comparison, and increasing the lower time so that the two normalized collision-tail errors are at most
1/2
, gives, for every
s
larger than the maximum of the deterministic thresholds introduced in this paragraph,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{H_{t,s}(\mathbf{y})}{H_{0,s}(\mathbf{x})}\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{y})}-1\right|

(258)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 3\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},t,\mathbf{y}}}{\mathrm{A}_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{y})}\psi_{\mathrm{G},t}(s)+3\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{col},\mathbf{x}}}{\mathrm{A}_{N}\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(s)+\mathrm{C}_{N,\mathrm{pow}}\frac{\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A1}}(1+t)}{\Sigma(s)^{2}}.

The right-hand side in (
258
) tends to zero as
s\to\infty
.

Now fix
T\in\mathscr{T}
. It follows from
[
4
,
5
]
, and can also be readily shown from the results of this paper, that,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{X}(T))\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}>T}\right]=\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x}).

Hence, the following measure, with
\mathscr{B}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
,

\mathscr{B}\mapsto\frac{1}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathfrak{h}_{N}\!\left(\mathbf{X}(T)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{B}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>T\}}\right],\qquad\mathscr{B}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,T},

(259)

is a probability measure. To take the limit, we first work on cylinder events whose final endpoint is prescribed:

\mathscr{A}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\left\{\mathbf{X}(t_{1})=\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}(t_{m})=\mathbf{y}_{m}\right\}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,T},\qquad 0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}=T.

If
\mathbf{y}_{m}\notin\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}
, then both the finite-horizon conditioned probability and the Doob-transform expression below are zero because the factor
\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>T\}}
forces the terminal state to remain ordered. We therefore assume
\mathbf{y}_{m}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}
in the calculation below. By (
7
),

\mathsf{P}_{0,\mathbf{x}}^{N,(s)}(\mathscr{A})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\frac{H_{T,s}(\mathbf{X}(T))}{H_{0,s}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{A}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>T\}}\right].

On the event
\mathscr{A}
, we have
\mathbf{X}(T)=\mathbf{y}_{m}
, and therefore (
258
), with
t=T
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}_{m}
, gives

\lim_{s\to\infty}\mathsf{P}_{0,\mathbf{x}}^{N,(s)}(\mathscr{A})=\frac{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{y}_{m})}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathscr{A},\,\mathcal{T}>T\right].

(260)

The cylinder events of this form are a
\pi
-system generating the finite-time path sigma-field
\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
. Their limiting probabilities are consistent as
T
varies, because they are pointwise limits of the consistent finite-horizon conditioned laws. Hence they define a limiting measure on each
\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
, and these finite-time measures are themselves consistent.

The right-hand side of (
260
) is the value on
\mathscr{A}
of the probability measure in (
259
). Hence the two probability measures agree on the generating
\pi
-system, and the
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem gives, for every
\mathscr{B}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
,

\mathsf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}(\mathscr{B})=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathfrak{h}_{N}\!\left(\mathbf{X}(T)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{B}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>T\}}\right].

Extending from indicators to bounded
\mathcal{F}_{0,T}
-measurable functionals by linearity and bounded monotone approximation yields

\mathsf{E}^{N}_{\mathbf{x}}\left[F\left(\hat{\mathbf{X}}(t):t\leq T\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[\mathfrak{h}_{N}\!\left(\mathbf{X}(T)\right)F\left(\mathbf{X}(t):t\leq T\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>T\}}\right],

thus completing the proof.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
1.20

Proposition 4.2

(Survival-conditioned stationary-point limit)
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A2)
. Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{d}}^{N}
, and let
F:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded and Lipschitz. Define

\mathrm{L}_{F}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}F(\mathbf{u})\,\Delta(\mathbf{u})\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u},\qquad\mathrm{L}_{0}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{N!}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(1+i/2)}{\Gamma(3/2)}.

Then there exist constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\middle|\mathcal{T}>t\right]-\frac{N!}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(3/2)}{\Gamma(1+i/2)}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}F(\mathbf{u})\,\Delta(\mathbf{u})\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\right|

(261)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

In particular, the conditional law of
\mathbf{U}(t)
given
\{\mathcal{T}>t\}
is controlled quantitatively by the ordered eigenvalue law of
\mathrm{GOE}_{N}
with the single explicit error scale in (
261
).

Proof.

Fix bounded Lipschitz
F:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
. Since
\{\mathcal{T}>t\}
has strictly positive probability for every finite
t
, we may write

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\middle|\mathcal{T}>t\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\mathcal{T}>t\right]}.

(262)

We now analyze the numerator and denominator after dividing by their common scale.
The constant
\mathrm{L}_{0}
is strictly positive, since every factor in the finite product defining it is positive. Applying Proposition
3.10
to the test function
F
gives, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})
,

\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-\mathrm{L}_{F}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

(263)

Applying the same proposition to the constant test function
1
and then using the evaluation of the Gaussian integral from Theorem
1.13
gives, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})
,

\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\mathcal{T}>t\right]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-\mathrm{L}_{0}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

(264)

By (
10
),
\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\to 0
. Hence we may choose
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}\in\mathscr{T}
larger than the lower times in (
263
) and (
264
), and satisfying
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\leq\mathrm{L}_{0}/2
for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}
. For those
t
, the normalized denominator in (
262
) is at least
\mathrm{L}_{0}/2
, and the elementary identity for ratios gives

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\middle|\mathcal{T}>t\right]-\frac{\mathrm{L}_{F}}{\mathrm{L}_{0}}\right|\\
\leq\frac{2}{\mathrm{L}_{0}}\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-\mathrm{L}_{F}\right|+\frac{2|\mathrm{L}_{F}|}{\mathrm{L}_{0}^{2}}\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[\mathcal{T}>t\right]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\mathrm{d}_{N}}}-\mathrm{L}_{0}\right|.

Substituting the bounds (
263
) and (
264
) into this ratio estimate gives

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F\!\left(\mathbf{U}(t)\right)\middle|\mathcal{T}>t\right]-\frac{\mathrm{L}_{F}}{\mathrm{L}_{0}}\right|\leq\left(\frac{2\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})}{\mathrm{L}_{0}}+\frac{2|\mathrm{L}_{F}|\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})}{\mathrm{L}_{0}^{2}}\right)\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

Thus (
261
) holds with
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{GOE},F,\mathbf{x}}=2\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(F,\mathbf{x})/\mathrm{L}_{0}+2|\mathrm{L}_{F}|\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})/\mathrm{L}_{0}^{2}
. Finally, the identity
\mathrm{L}_{F}/\mathrm{L}_{0}
equals the displayed
\mathrm{GOE}_{N}
integral in (
261
), because
\mathrm{L}_{0}^{-1}=N!\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma(3/2)/\Gamma(1+i/2)
. This proves the proposition.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.20
.

Fix
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
. By Proposition
3.3
, applied to the one-particle path
\mathscr{X}_{i}
, there is an almost surely finite random time after which
\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}
for all later
t
. Increasing this random time if necessary so that
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{A}}
, Proposition
2.4
applies directly to the realized terminal endpoint
\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)
at every later time. Hence, after this almost surely finite random time, the minimizer in Definition
1.18
is unique and equals
\mathrm{u}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(t)
, so
\mathscr{U}_{i}(t)=-\Sigma(t)\mathrm{u}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}(t)
. This proves the finite-random-time uniqueness assertion for each coordinate.

Now let
F:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded and Lipschitz. The function
F
satisfies the growth and local Lipschitz hypotheses of Proposition
3.5
, with polynomial exponent
q=0
after increasing the local Lipschitz constant to dominate the uniform bound. Therefore (
190
) gives a bound of the form
\mathrm{C}_{F,\mathbf{x}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
for the expectation restricted to
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}
. The omitted endpoint complement contributes at most
\|F\|_{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}]
, and the one-time endpoint-window estimate from Proposition
3.2
(applied coordinatewise and combined with the finite union bound, as in the final step of Proposition
3.5
) bounds this probability by another constant multiple of
\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
after increasing the lower time. Absorbing these two constants into
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{free},F,\mathbf{x}}
proves the free quantitative bound. For real-valued bounded Lipschitz
F
, the survival-conditioned statement is precisely Proposition
4.2
; its limiting integral is the ordered
\mathrm{GOE}_{N}
eigenvalue density from Definition
1.19
. Combining these conclusions proves the theorem.
∎

4.4
Proof of Corollary
1.21

Proof of Corollary
1.21
.

We first prove the quantitative comparison between the stationary-point coordinates and the centered inverse-rate coordinates on the endpoint window. This is the only additional estimate needed to transfer Theorem
1.20
from
\mathbf{U}(t)
to
\mathbf{V}(t)
. Since the comparison is also used inside the leading term of the Karlin–McGregor expansion, we prove it for an arbitrary deterministic initial vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
and take expectations with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}
throughout this proof.

For one coordinate, applying (
58
) to the random endpoint path
y=\mathscr{X}_{i}
gives the exact identity

\frac{1}{\Sigma(t)}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\mathscr{X}_{i}(t)}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}-\Lambda\mathscr{Z}(t)\right)=\frac{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}^{(1)}(0,t)}{\Sigma(t)}+\frac{\Lambda(\mathrm{z}(t)-\mathscr{Z}(t))}{\Sigma(t)}.

(265)

Since
\mathscr{Z}(t)=\lfloor\mathrm{z}(t)\rceil
, the last term in (
265
) has absolute value at most
\Lambda/(2\Sigma(t))
. On the event
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}
, each coordinate endpoint belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{t}
, so the comparison estimate (
204
) applies to every coordinate after increasing the deterministic lower time. Combining (
265
), the rounding bound, and (
204
), we obtain, for every
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and every
t
beyond this deterministic lower time,

\left|V_{i}(t)-\mathscr{U}_{i}(t)\right|\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\leq\left(\frac{\Lambda}{2\Sigma(t)}+\frac{4\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{A}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|^{2}}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}+\frac{2\mathrm{C}_{\mathscr{C}}\gamma(t)^{1/2}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|}{\Sigma(t)}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}.

(266)

This pointwise estimate becomes an expectation estimate by using the moment input already stated in Proposition
3.4
. More precisely, (
183
) gives first-derivative moments. We also use the deterministic lower comparison between the fluctuation scale and time: the linear lower bound on
\mathscr{Z}(t)
from (
61
), together with the upper comparison between
\mathscr{Z}(t)
and
\Sigma(t)^{2}
in
(iv)
of Proposition
2.3
, gives a constant
\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}>0
and a deterministic lower time such that

\Sigma(t)^{2}\geq\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}(1+t)

for every later
t
. Hence, for each coordinate,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}\left[\frac{\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|^{2}}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}\right]\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{2,\mathbf{a}}(1+t)}{\Sigma(t)^{3}}\leq\mathrm{C}_{2,\mathbf{a}}\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{-1}\Sigma(t)^{-1},

and, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality followed by the same scale comparison,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}\left[\frac{\left|\mathfrak{F}_{\mathscr{X}_{i}}^{(1)}(0,t)\right|}{\Sigma(t)}\right]\leq\frac{\mathrm{C}_{2,\mathbf{a}}^{1/2}(1+t)^{1/2}}{\Sigma(t)}\leq\mathrm{C}_{2,\mathbf{a}}^{1/2}\mathrm{c}_{\Sigma}^{-1/2}.

Summing (
266
) over
i
and absorbing the finite coordinate sum into the constant gives constants
\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{VU},1,\mathbf{a}}\in\mathscr{T}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},1,\mathbf{a}}>0
such that, for every
t>\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{VU},1,\mathbf{a}}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}\left[|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},1,\mathbf{a}}\left(\Sigma(t)^{-1}+\gamma(t)^{1/2}\right).

(267)

The same pointwise estimate gives the square-integrable form needed in the determinant argument. We square (
266
), use
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
and
|\mathbf{v}|^{2}\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}|v_{i}|^{2}
, and then apply (
183
) with exponents
2
and
4
. This gives a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},2,\mathbf{a}}>0
such that, for every
t
beyond the deterministic lower time used in the preceding estimates,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}\left[|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},2,\mathbf{a}}^{2}\left(\Sigma(t)^{-2}+\gamma(t)\right).

Therefore,

\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}\left[|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\right)^{1/2}\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},2,\mathbf{a}}\left(\Sigma(t)^{-1}+\gamma(t)^{1/2}\right).

(268)

Finally, Definition
1.12
gives
\gamma(t)\geq\Sigma(t)^{-1/2}
, while (
10
) and the fact that
\gamma(t)\to 0
imply, after increasing the lower time, that both
\Sigma(t)^{-1}
and
\gamma(t)^{1/2}
are bounded by
\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)
. Hence, for the original initial state
\mathbf{x}
, (
267
) gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq 2\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},1,\mathbf{x}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t),

(269)

and, for every fixed initial vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{N}
, (
268
) gives

\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}\left[|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\right)^{1/2}\leq 2\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},2,\mathbf{a}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

(270)

We also record the endpoint-complement estimate in the same place. Proposition
3.2
, applied coordinatewise and combined with the finite union bound, gives a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail},\mathbf{a}}>0
such that, after increasing the lower time if necessary,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{a}}^{N}[\mathbf{X}(t)\notin\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}]\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail},\mathbf{a}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)

(271)

for every later
t
.

We can now finish the free coordinate limit. Taking
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{x}
in (
269
) and (
271
), the Lipschitz and boundedness assumptions on
F
give

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[F(\mathbf{V}(t))\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\left[F(\mathbf{U}(t))\right]\right|\leq 2\mathrm{Lip}(F)\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{VU},1,\mathbf{x}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)+2\|F\|_{\infty}\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{tail},\mathbf{x}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

Combining this estimate with the free part of Theorem
1.20
proves the first assertion of Corollary
1.21
, after absorbing the displayed constants into
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{coord},F,\mathbf{x}}
.

It remains to identify the survival-conditioned limit of
\mathbf{V}(t)
. We apply the same determinant expansion as in Proposition
3.10
, but with the bounded Lipschitz factor
F(\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{m}}(t))
replaced by
F(\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{m}}(t))
, where

\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{m}}(t)\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{1}{\Sigma(t)}\left(\Lambda_{N}(\mathbf{m})-\Lambda\mathscr{Z}(t)\,\underline{1}\right).

This substitution does not alter the algebraic part of the proof. The endpoint-complement estimate in Step 1 of Proposition
3.10
, and the non-leading bounds in Steps 2–4, use the test function only through
\|F\|_{\infty}
. The same bounds therefore apply to
F(\mathbf{V}_{\mathbf{m}}(t))
. Thus the expansion (
253
) is replaced by the identical formula with
F(\mathbf{V}(t))
in place of
F(\mathbf{U}(t))
, except that the final expectation becomes

\mathbb{E}_{\underline{0}}^{N}\!\left[\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))F(\mathbf{V}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N},\,\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right].

We compare this with the leading expectation already evaluated in Proposition
3.10
. The Lipschitz bound gives

\left|\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))F(\mathbf{V}(t))-\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))F(\mathbf{U}(t))\right|\leq\mathrm{Lip}(F)|\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))|\,|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|.

Since
\Delta
is a polynomial of degree
\mathrm{d}_{N}
, Proposition
3.4
, applied with any
p\geq\max(2,2\mathrm{d}_{N})
, gives a uniform bound for the second moment of
|\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))|
on
\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}
. Hence the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (
270
), used with
\mathbf{a}=\underline{0}
, imply that

\mathbb{E}_{\underline{0}}^{N}\left[|\Delta(\mathbf{U}(t))|\,|\mathbf{V}(t)-\mathbf{U}(t)|\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{X}(t)\in\mathscr{C}_{t}^{N}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{C}_{\Delta,\mathrm{VU}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)

for a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\Delta,\mathrm{VU}}>0
and every
t
beyond the deterministic lower time fixed above.
Combining this estimate with Proposition
3.10
gives

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\!\left[F(\mathbf{V}(t))\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathcal{T}>t\}}\right]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\frac{N}{2}(N-1)}}-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{c}}^{N}}F(\mathbf{u})\Delta(\mathbf{u})\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\right|

(272)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{coordKM},F,\mathbf{x}}\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)

for a constant
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{coordKM},F,\mathbf{x}}>0
and all
t
beyond a deterministic lower time.
The denominator is controlled by Proposition
3.10
with the constant test function
1
, and the limiting constant is

\mathrm{L}_{0}=\frac{1}{N!}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(1+i/2)}{\Gamma(3/2)}.

Thus, for every
t
beyond the lower time in Proposition
3.10
,

\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{N}[\mathcal{T}>t]}{\mathfrak{h}_{N}(\mathbf{x})\Sigma(t)^{-\frac{N}{2}(N-1)}}-\mathrm{L}_{0}\right|\leq\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t).

Increase the lower time so that
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})\psi_{\mathrm{G}}(t)\leq\mathrm{L}_{0}/2
. The elementary ratio estimate displayed in the proof of Proposition
4.2
, applied with the numerator bound (
272
), then gives the conditional coordinate estimate with

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{coordGOE},F,\mathbf{x}}\overset{\textnormal{def}}{=}\frac{2\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{coordKM},F,\mathbf{x}}}{\mathrm{L}_{0}}+\frac{2|\mathrm{L}_{F}|\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{KM}}(1,\mathbf{x})}{\mathrm{L}_{0}^{2}}.

The quotient is therefore controlled quantitatively by the ordered
\mathrm{GOE}_{N}
eigenvalue law from Definition
1.19
. This proves the conditional coordinate estimate and completes the proof.
∎
